### [1] [Transfer Learning for Named Entity Recognition of Classical Latin through LLM Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04015)
*Callum Chan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper reports a top-performing system for Latin Named Entity Recognition at EvaLatin 2026 using prompt-engineered commercial LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical Latin is underrepresented in NLP resources and benchmarks, yet digitized Latin corpora are growing and modern LLMs have strong cross-lingual abilities. The author aims to show that these general-purpose LLMs can be leveraged, without task-specific model training, to perform high-quality NER on Latin and to compete in a shared task benchmark (EvaLatin 2026).

Method: The team uses prompt engineering with two commercial LLMs, gemini-2.5-pro and claude-sonnet-4-5, to perform Named Entity Recognition on Latin texts. The task includes two subtasks: (1) coarse-grained NER with 11 entity classes and (2) fine-grained NER with 28 entity classes. Both subtasks are evaluated using strict and fuzzy scoring regimes. The core methodological contribution is designing prompts and workflows that effectively guide these large, general models to recognize and label Latin entities under the shared-task schema, exploiting cross-lingual transfer from languages on which the models are better trained.

Result: The prompted LLM-based system achieves top performance in EvaLatin 2026 for both NER subtasks. It places first overall and obtains the best scores on all evaluation metrics and in both strict and fuzzy evaluation regimes among all submissions to the shared task.

Conclusion: The study concludes that even for an under-resourced ancient language like Latin, high-quality NER can be achieved by carefully prompting strong general-purpose LLMs. Cross-lingual transfer from better-resourced languages and the broader progress in LLM development can be effectively harnessed without building bespoke models, as demonstrated by the system’s state-of-the-art results in EvaLatin 2026.

Abstract: With the increase in digitized resources of Classical Latin texts and modern breakthroughs of Large Language Models (LLMs), I contribute to ancient language research by participating in EvaLatin 2026. This paper describes Team uOttawa's system description and results for the Named Entity Recognition (NER) shared task. The task is divided into two subtasks: coarse-grained NER with 11 classes and fine-grained NER with 28 classes, each evaluated under strict and fuzzy regimes. Through prompt engineering of commercial LLMs gemini-2.5-pro and claude-sonnet-4-5, I show that the underrepresented ancient Latin language can take advantage of cross-lingual transfer learning by using advancements made by the wider LLM development community. Overall, the methods discussed in this report demonstrate very strong results, placing first in both NER subtasks and achieving the best scores across all evaluation metrics and regimes among all submissions.

</details>


### [2] [When More Becomes Less: Position-Dependent Repetition Effects in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04021)
*Han-yu Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that cloze-style repetition probes give very different results depending on where the prediction slot is placed, so repetition count and readout position cannot be treated as independent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many recent probing studies test language models by repeating a target token and examining how its predicted probability changes with the number of repetitions, often assuming that only the repetition count matters and that the readout position is irrelevant. The authors suspect this assumption is wrong and that the discourse or sentence frame around the readout slot crucially shapes model behavior.

Method: They design two nearly identical cloze probes that differ only in where the readout token is predicted: (1) an adjacent probe where the prediction slot immediately follows a block of repeated target tokens, and (2) a displaced probe where the same repeated block appears earlier, but the prediction slot is embedded in a new sentence frame. They systematically vary the number of repetitions N of the target, test 13 open-access encoder and decoder models across multiple languages, and run a six-condition causal ablation to separate the effect of exact lexical repetition from confounds such as length, generic redundancy, or semantic neighbors. They also add a frame-pragmatics control to ensure results are not artifacts of the specific continuation frame, and analyze internal attention patterns over the repeated block.

Result: Adjacent probes behave intuitively: as N increases, the model’s probability for the target climbs and then plateaus. In contrast, displaced probes exhibit an inverted-U pattern: probability increases with a few repetitions but then decreases as N continues to grow. This inverted-U effect is statistically robust: every tested model shows a nonzero per-word decline in the displaced case, and all 42 language–model–language pairs in the multilingual experiments replicate it. Ablation experiments confirm the effect is driven by exact lexical repetition rather than sequence length or general redundancy, and frame-pragmatics controls rule out the readout sentence as the cause. Attention analyses show that, as N grows, attention per token in the repeated block falls while the total attention mass allocated to the block increases in causal LMs, whereas the masked LM shows a different internal allocation profile.

Conclusion: Repetition-count probes are highly sensitive to where the prediction is taken: the location of the readout slot interacts with repetition in systematic ways that can reverse qualitative conclusions. Adjacent repetition largely acts like priming, but displaced repetition induces a saturation and subsequent decline in target probability, reflecting broader discourse and attention dynamics. Therefore, probing methods that vary target repetition cannot assume that readout position is orthogonal to what is being measured and must carefully control or model this interaction when interpreting language model behavior.

Abstract: Cloze-style probes that vary how often a target token appears implicitly assume that more copies of a target affect prediction the same way regardless of where the readout slot sits. We show this assumption fails. Our two-probe design holds a repeated-target prefix fixed and varies only the readout position: the adjacent probe places the slot immediately after the repeated block; the displaced probe places it inside a fresh sentence frame. Adjacent repetition behaves as priming intuition predicts: $P(\text{target})$ climbs with $N$ and plateaus. Displaced repetition produces an inverted-U: $P(\text{target})$ rises to an early peak and then declines as more copies are added. The displaced inverted-U shows a per-word drop with bootstrap CI excluding zero in all 13 open-access encoder and decoder models we test, and replicates across Spanish, Chinese, German, and French in 42 of 42 multilingual cells. A six-condition causal ablation isolates the effect to exact lexical repetition rather than length, generic redundancy, or semantic-neighbour exposure. A frame-pragmatics control rules out an artefact of the readout frame. Internally, per-target-token attention falls with $N$ while the total budget assigned to the repeated block grows in causal LMs but not in the masked LM we probe. Probes that vary repetition count cannot treat the readout position as orthogonal to what they measure.

</details>


### [3] [Reconstructing Persistent Worlds from Narratives for Narrative-Grounded Interactive Experiences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04037)
*Yi-Chun Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes reconstructing explicit, persistent world models from narrative text to better support narrative-grounded interactive experiences such as games.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Creating interactive experiences that remain consistent with a narrative world is labor-intensive. Current methods focus on specific tasks (e.g., narrative planning, scene generation, gameplay generation) and treat the underlying world as an implicit, fragmented by-product, which makes it hard to maintain coherence across interactions and content types.

Method: The authors frame explicit persistent world reconstruction from narrative descriptions as the main computational task. They build a reference prototype system that parses narrative text to reconstruct structured world representations, including entities, locations, semantic relationships, and evolving world states, and then uses this representation to instantiate playable tile-based environments that stay grounded in the source narrative.

Result: Using three case studies (a procedural scenario, an original fantasy narrative, and an adapted public-domain story), the prototype successfully reconstructs persistent world models and generates coherent, playable tile-based game environments that share and reuse a common world representation across different interactive elements.

Conclusion: Explicitly reconstructing and maintaining persistent world models from narrative descriptions can effectively bridge computational narrative understanding and interactive content generation. This provides a robust semantic foundation for AI-assisted game authoring, mixed-initiative design tools, educational simulations, and other narrative-grounded interactive experiences.

Abstract: Designing narrative-grounded interactive experiences remains labor-intensive because interactive content must align with the underlying world implied by the narrative. Existing approaches formulate problems such as narrative planning, scene generation, and gameplay generation, each constructing computational representations tailored to specific downstream tasks rather than explicitly reconstructing and maintaining the persistent world that grounds them.
  We investigate reconstructing explicit persistent worlds from narrative descriptions as the central computational objective for narrative-grounded interactive realization. Rather than treating the world as an implicit by-product of downstream generation, our approach reconstructs and maintains persistent entities, locations, semantic relationships, and evolving world states while inferring only the contextual information required to support coherent interactive experiences.
  To investigate this perspective, we develop a reference prototype that reconstructs structured persistent world representations from narrative descriptions and subsequently instantiates playable tile-based environments. Through three representative case studies spanning a procedural scenario, an original fantasy narrative, and an adapted public-domain story, we demonstrate the feasibility of reconstructing persistent worlds and show how a shared world representation supports coherent gameplay while remaining grounded in the source narrative.
  By explicitly reconstructing persistent worlds prior to interactive realization, this work bridges computational narrative understanding and interactive content generation, providing a semantic foundation for AI-assisted game authoring, mixed-initiative design, educational simulations, and narrative-grounded interactive experiences.

</details>


### [4] [Learning Sexism Detection Using Multi-Agent Perspectivist Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04056)
*Hadi Mohammadi,Tina Shahedi,Robert A. Bagheri,Mehdi Dastani,Masoume M. Raeissi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MAP-PO, a framework that models multiple valid perspectives in sexism annotation by training separate LLM agents for annotator clusters and coordinating them via preference optimization, improving both cluster-faithful and majority-like labeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sexism annotation is inherently subjective, leading to systematic disagreement between annotators that reflects genuine differences in perception rather than simple noise or error. Standard NLP practice collapses this disagreement into a single majority label, erasing minority but valid perspectives and limiting model fairness and expressivity. The authors aim to build models that respect and reproduce multiple perspectives simultaneously, both to better match real human behavior and to avoid privileging a single, monolithic notion of sexism.

Method: Using the EXIST 2024 dataset in English and Spanish, the authors first cluster annotators based on their labeling behaviors instead of demographic attributes. For each cluster, they fine-tune a separate LLM agent to imitate that cluster’s annotation pattern. They then introduce the MAP-PO framework, which coordinates these agents using preference optimization that blends individual rewards (how well an agent matches its own cluster) with team-level rewards (how well the ensemble reflects aggregate behavior such as the majority label). They systematically evaluate across two languages and two backbone LLMs, checking within-cluster fidelity and ability to recover majority labels collectively.

Result: Across all evaluated settings, the authors find that (1) un-fine-tuned LLMs behave very similarly, failing to capture distinct perspectives, which shows that cluster-specific adaptation is needed; and (2) training each agent only on its own cluster’s labels leads to over-specialization and divergence from the cluster it is supposed to represent. Incorporating a shared team-level signal via preference optimization keeps each agent better calibrated to its cluster while also enabling the ensemble to reconstruct majority labels more faithfully.

Conclusion: MAP-PO demonstrates that it is feasible and beneficial to model multiple, genuinely different annotator perspectives on sexism within a single framework by combining cluster-specific agents and coordinated preference optimization. The approach preserves perspectival diversity while maintaining alignment with aggregate labels, suggesting a path toward more nuanced, multi-perspective NLP systems for subjective and sensitive annotation tasks.

Abstract: When people label text for sexism, they often disagree, and not because some of them are wrong: they genuinely perceive sexism differently. Most NLP systems discard this disagreement by collapsing it into a majority vote. We propose the Multi-Agent Perspectivist Preference Optimization (MAP-PO) framework to keep these different perspectives. On the EXIST 2024 dataset of labeled English and Spanish tweets, we first cluster annotators by their labeling behavior rather than their demographic attributes. We then fine-tune one Large Language Model agent per cluster to reproduce that cluster's annotation behavior, and coordinate the agents with preference optimization that combines individual and team-level rewards. We evaluate MAP-PO in four settings defined by two languages and two backbone language models, asking whether each agent reproduces the annotations of its own cluster and whether the agents together reproduce the majority label. Two findings hold in all four settings. First, without fine-tuning the agents behave almost identically, so cluster-specific training is necessary. Second, we show that training each agent only on the labels of its own cluster pushes the agents far beyond the clusters they should represent, while adding a shared team-level training signal consistently keeps each agent calibrated to its cluster.

</details>


### [5] [Mind the Cap: Output-Budget Regimes Change the Measured Multilingual Reasoning Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04160)
*Ankit Goyal,Jaideep Ray*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that evaluation accuracy for multilingual models heavily depends on the maximum output-token budget, which is usually treated as a fixed setting but actually behaves like a hidden experimental variable that can create or erase apparent performance gaps between native and translated benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard multilingual benchmarks report a single accuracy value at one output-token limit, implicitly assuming this limit affects all languages similarly. However, different languages require different token lengths to express equivalent content, so a shared token cap may unfairly penalize some languages and distort comparisons, particularly the observed performance gap between native-language and translated test sets like MGSM.

Method: The authors run controlled experiments on MGSM in German, Thai, and Swahili using Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct models under four prompting strategies. They systematically sweep over output-token budgets, apply length normalization where appropriate, and freeze specific peak-budget settings (including a key value B*=1024) to evaluate large numbers of independently hard-capped decodes. They use Holm-corrected hypothesis tests to assess whether gaps are statistically significant, and they also perform interventions such as Thai vocabulary extension and manipulating the announced budget independently of the enforced cap. Finally, they derive and validate a timing-based identity that predicts where accuracy peaks as a function of the token budget.

Result: Accuracy differences between native and translated MGSM vary dramatically with token budget, with native-vs-translate gaps changing by up to 57 points across budgets. Length normalization can shift scores by nearly 39 points and even change which prompting strategy seems best under tight caps. Statistical tests on 540,000 decodes reject null hypotheses of no budget effect across a family of frozen settings, except at B*=1024 where native performance has already saturated. A Thai vocabulary extension yields negligible gains at the frozen budget but modest gains where truncation still occurs. Changing only the announced budget (e.g., 128 vs 2048 tokens) while keeping the hard cap fixed shifts Thai native accuracy by 5.1 points, proving that performance is not solely determined by the enforced cap. A correct-emission timing identity accurately predicts accuracy peaks and generalizes well to additional benchmarks for Qwen.

Conclusion: What looks like a structural native-vs-translate reasoning gap in multilingual benchmarks can instead be largely an artifact of the output-token budget and where truncation occurs. The token cap functions as a crucial experimental variable that interacts with language, prompting strategy, and even the announced budget, so evaluations should treat output length budget as an independent variable and report accuracy as a function of budget across a regime, rather than at a single cap.

Abstract: Multilingual evaluations report accuracy at a single output-token cap, but languages need different numbers of tokens to express the same content, so the cap is a hidden experimental variable. We test whether the native-vs-translate gap on MGSM (German, Thai, Swahili) is a token-budget artifact for Qwen3-8B and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct under four prompting strategies. The measured gap swings by up to 57 points across budgets, length normalization moves it by up to 38.9 points where the cap binds, and at tight caps normalization can reverse which strategy scores higher. We prospectively froze the sweep's three Qwen peaks and its near-zero value at 1024 and evaluated them on 540,000 independently hard-capped decodes: a second frozen family of six Holm-corrected tests rejects every null. The frozen test at $B^*=1024$ still fails to reject because native accuracy has already saturated there; above saturation, the residual difference is a strategy-performance gap, not an identified reasoning deficit. The same truncation channel prices a cost-ordered adaptation ladder: a cross-fitted Thai vocabulary extension closes 0.0 points of the gap at the frozen budget and 4.9 points where 19% of traces still truncate. A third frozen family varies only the announced budget at a fixed enforced cap; announcing 128 rather than 2048 tokens moves Thai native accuracy by 5.1 points, so accuracy is not a function of the enforced cap alone. A correct-emission timing identity computed from one long-cap run matches the three pre-specified MGSM peaks to 0.65 points and, in an exploratory Qwen-only analysis of three further benchmarks, tracks held-out items to 0.92 points, locating the peak exactly in five of seven cells. Treat the output cap as an independent variable and report accuracy across the budget regime, not at a single budget.

</details>


### [6] [Visualizing Graph-to-Answer Mechanism Recovery in Materials-Science Hypothesis Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04170)
*Shashwat Sourav,Subhadeep Pal,Markus J. Buehler,Sanjay Das,Fiona Y. Wang,Dominik Soos,Tirthankar Ghosal*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They study how an 8B-parameter AI "co-scientist" actually uses intermediate reasoning stages and internal activations to preserve or lose mechanistic (cause-and-effect) explanations when answering materials-science questions, and provide a diagnostic workflow to trace and visualize this process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI models can write plausible materials-science hypotheses, but fluency alone doesn’t guarantee that the answer reflects a coherent, mechanistically grounded scientific explanation. Scientists and model builders need tools to see whether, where, and how an answer stays connected to mechanistic reasoning, especially before using these hypotheses for real experimental planning.

Method: They adapt a Qwen3-8B model into Graph-PRefLexOR-8B, which explicitly separates four stages: brainstorming, graph construction, pattern extraction, and synthesis. They then build a mechanism-tracing pipeline that includes: (1) semantic backtracking from final answers to earlier structured stages, (2) systematic corruption of the intermediate graphs, (3) activation-based recovery analysis over 37 checkpoints in the residual stream (embedding output plus 36 transformer blocks) to see where the model can repair corrupted mechanisms, and (4) layer-by-token-region activation grids. They apply this to 100 open-ended materials-science questions and visualize the whole pathway as a diagnostic workflow.

Result: For the 100 materials-science questions, the model’s final answers align most closely with its own structured intermediate stages, especially the synthesis stage, rather than diverging into unrelated reasoning. When they corrupt the intermediate graph representations and sweep over all 37 residual-stream checkpoints, they find minimal mechanism recovery in the earlier transitional layers (around 7–10). Instead, recovery of mechanistic consistency is concentrated in the later layers, particularly in regions associated with synthesis and the start of the final answer (around layers 30 and 36).

Conclusion: The study shows that for this 8B-parameter model, mechanistic support for hypotheses is primarily consolidated in late transformer layers tied to synthesis and answer generation, not in earlier transitional layers. The proposed visual diagnostic workflow—combining structured stages, semantic backtracking, graph corruption, activation recovery analysis, and layer-by-token-region grids—gives scientists and model developers a practical way to see where hypotheses lose or regain mechanistic grounding. This can guide both safer use of AI-generated hypotheses in experimental planning and targeted model or prompt interventions to preserve mechanism fidelity.

Abstract: AI co-scientists can generate fluent materials-science hypotheses, but fluency does not show that an answer preserves a scientifically meaningful mechanism. We present a graph-to-answer mechanism-tracing case study for Graph-PRefLexOR-8B, a Qwen3-8B model adapted to expose distinct stages for brainstorming, graph construction, pattern extraction, and synthesis. We organize semantic backtracking, graph corruption, activation-based recovery measurements, and layer-by-token-region grids into a visual diagnostic workflow for inspecting this pathway. Across 100 open-ended materials-science questions, final answers remain closest to the model's own structured stages, especially synthesis. Under graph corruption, a full sweep over 37 residual-stream checkpoints, the embedding output and 36 transformer blocks, shows little mechanism recovery in the earlier transition region at layers 7--10, recovery instead concentrates in late synthesis and answer-start regions around layers 30 and 36. The workflow is intended to help scientists and model developers identify where a generated hypothesis loses or regains mechanism support before it is passed to downstream experimental planning.

</details>


### [7] [Test, then Route: How Language Models Execute In-Context Conditional Rules Across Models and Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04183)
*Luxshan Thavarasa,Sivasuthan Sukumar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether language models implement conditional rules via modular runtime circuits separating predicate evaluation from answer routing, using activation patching to localize these processes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the internal mechanism by which language models follow in-context conditional rules like 'if P(x) then A else B', specifically whether they form modular, reusable components for predicate evaluation and routing, which relates to mechanistic interpretability and circuit modularity.

Method: Use activation patching with a four-donor design in which two 'swapped-rule' donors make the condition and the answer word disagree. This allows distinguishing whether a layer’s activations encode the predicate’s truth value or the answer token. Experiments are run on three open-source language models from two model families, across six languages with a fixed item bank, and across five predicate families. They analyze residual stream bands and learned subspaces that flip between A and B, including cross-lingual transfer tests.

Result: They find that a band of mid-stack residual layers reliably carries the predicate’s truth value: activation patching in this band flips the predicate outcome while leaving the mapping from truth value to answer unchanged, satisfying a pre-registered isolation criterion in 17 of 18 experimental conditions and generalizing across predicate families. Conversely, a separate “router” direction shows the opposite behavior and is highly token-bound and non-transferable—near-perfectly flipping A/B within the trained pair but failing to transfer to new pairs or, in most models, across languages. In Gemma-3-4B it transfers well across languages but only for the same answer pair.

Conclusion: The models implement a modular representation of the predicate (the test is modular and localized in mid-stack residual streams), but the routing from predicate to answer is not implemented as an abstract, reusable module. Instead, routing is tightly bound to specific tokens or token pairs and does not generalize as a clean, transferable circuit, indicating a partial but not fully modular implementation of in-context conditional reasoning.

Abstract: When a language model follows an in-context conditional rule such as "if P(x) then A else B," does it assemble a runtime circuit with one module that tests the predicate and another that routes the answer? We probe this with activation patching under a four-donor design whose two swapped-rule donors make the condition and the answer word disagree, so each layer reveals which of the two it carries. Across three open models from two families and six languages sharing one fixed item bank, a mid-stack residual band carries the predicate's truth value: patching it reroutes the answer with predicate-outcome flip near 1.0 and mapping flip near 0.0, meeting a strict pre-specified isolation criterion in 17 of 18 cells, and the same localization holds across five predicate families. The router shows the opposite profile. A learned subspace flips A and B near-perfectly within the trained pair yet transfers to a new pair at approximately 0 in every model, while in Gemma-3-4B (the only model probed cross-lingually) it transfers at approximately 0.98 to the same pair in other languages. Under every probe we ran, the router direction is token-bound and non-transferable (largely answer-readout in Gemma, pair-specific in Qwen) rather than an abstract routing module. Test is modular; under these probes, route is not.

</details>


### [8] [Large Language Models for Low-Resource Languages: A Conceptual Framework for an Electronic Explanatory Dictionary of the Tajik Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04186)
*Mullosharaf K. Arabov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Conceptual framework for an LLM-based electronic explanatory dictionary for Tajik, integrating classical lexicography with modern NLP modules to support low-resource language processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is no comprehensive, functionally rich digital explanatory dictionary for Tajik, unlike those available for high-resource languages. Modern NLP technologies and LLMs are underutilized for low-resource languages like Tajik, limiting tools for both lexicographic work and downstream NLP applications. The paper aims to close this gap by outlining how to systematically combine available linguistic and corpus resources with state-of-the-art models.

Method: Conduct a systematic survey of existing linguistic, statistical, and corpus resources for Tajik, then design a modular dictionary architecture. The architecture includes components for morphological analysis, lemmatization, semantic clustering of word senses, and automatic generation of dictionary entries with LLMs. It employs subword tokenization tailored to Tajik’s agglutinative and morphologically rich structure and uses parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) to adapt LLMs with limited annotated data.

Result: The study produces a holistic conceptual architecture for an electronic Tajik explanatory dictionary that unifies traditional lexicographic practices, language statistics, and LLM-based generative components into one system. It specifies how modules should interact and which NLP techniques (tokenization, PEFT, clustering, generation) are appropriate for Tajik under low-resource conditions.

Conclusion: An LLM-enhanced, modular dictionary architecture is both feasible and well-suited for Tajik, providing a roadmap for creating a comprehensive electronic explanatory dictionary. This framework establishes a methodological basis that can underpin not only lexicographic tools but also a wide range of NLP applications—such as machine translation, summarization, and sentiment analysis—for Tajik and potentially other low-resource languages.

Abstract: This paper presents a conceptual framework for developing an electronic explanatory dictionary of the Tajik language using large language models (LLMs). The relevance of the work stems from the absence of a comprehensive digital lexicographic resource for Tajik that is comparable in functionality to dictionaries for high-resource languages, and from the limited adaptation of modern natural language processing technologies to low-resource language systems. Based on a systematic survey of existing linguistic, statistical, and corpus resources, we propose a dictionary architecture that integrates modules for morphological analysis, lemmatization, semantic clustering, and dictionary entry generation using LLMs. The choice of subword tokenization is justified by the agglutinative nature of Tajik morphology and its high morphological variability, along with a parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) strategy suitable for limited annotated data. The novelty of the work lies in proposing the first holistic conceptual architecture of an explanatory dictionary for Tajik that unifies classical lexicographic methods, language statistics, and generative capabilities of LLMs into a single system. The practical significance of the study is the formation of a methodological foundation for developing a full-featured electronic dictionary that can serve both as a lexicographic tool and as a core resource for machine translation, automatic summarization, sentiment analysis, and other applied NLP tasks. The paper is intended for specialists in computational linguistics, lexicography, and developers of natural language processing systems working with low-resource languages.

</details>


### [9] [Patients-like-me: A Variational LM--GNN Framework for Explainable Clinical Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04193)
*Xinyu Wang,Yixuan Li,Hanwei Wu,Qincheng Lu,Chi-Kuang Yeh,Xiao-Wen Chang,Ziyang Song*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Patients-like-me (PLM), a unified framework combining language models and graph neural networks to improve prediction and explainability on EHR data by leveraging both individual patient notes and inter-patient similarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing language models for EHRs capture rich local patient information but treat each patient in isolation and offer limited interpretability, while graph neural networks can model relationships and explanations via similar patients but depend on having strong patient node features. There is a need for a method that jointly leverages powerful LM-based representations and GNN-based cohort structure to enhance predictive performance and provide patient-level explanations.

Method: The authors propose PLM, a joint LM-GNN framework where the LM produces textual representations of individual patients and the GNN propagates information across a patient graph capturing inter-patient relationships. Training is performed via a Variational Expectation-Maximization algorithm under a supervised variational objective, alternating between updating the LM to better encode patient sequences and updating the GNN to better exploit the cohort structure. The framework is evaluated on ICU EHR datasets (MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV) and instantiated with both encoder-only and decoder-only LM backbones.

Result: On multiple prediction tasks over MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV, PLM consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, including strong LM-only and GNN-only methods, and the performance gains hold across different LM backbone types (encoder-only and decoder-only). These improvements come with only modest additional computational cost compared with LM baselines. The model also yields reference-patient explanations by identifying the most influential similar patients, and edge-masking studies show that these top-ranked reference edges have the largest effect on predictions.

Conclusion: Integrating language models and graph neural networks in the PLM framework effectively combines local semantic representations with global patient-cohort structure, leading to better predictive performance and improved explainability in EHR modeling. The variational EM training scheme makes this integration computationally feasible, and the ability to retrieve influential reference patients provides clinically meaningful, example-based explanations of model decisions.

Abstract: Language models (LMs) offer strong textual representations for electronic health records (EHRs), but they encode patient sequences in isolation and provide limited explainability. Graph neural networks (GNNs) complement LMs by incorporating inter-patient relationships and enabling reference-patient attribution, yet they rely on high-quality patient representations. We propose Patients-like-me (PLM), a unified LM--GNN framework that integrates local patient semantics with global cohort structure. To train PLM efficiently, we introduce a Variational Expectation-Maximization algorithm that alternates LM and GNN updates under a supervised variational objective. Extensive experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV show that PLM consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods, with improvements generalizing across encoder-only and decoder-only LM backbones. These gains are achieved with only modest additional computational overhead. PLM also provides reference-patient explanations by retrieving influential similar patients, while edge-masking experiments confirm that the highest-ranked references have the greatest impact on model predictions.

</details>


### [10] [Hallucinations on the Board: Tool-Augmented Evaluation of LLM Chess Commentary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04240)
*S. Ashwin Hebbar,Peiyao Sheng,Sewoong Oh,Pramod Viswanath*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ACT-Eval, a chess-specific framework for rigorously evaluating the factual correctness, coverage, and move-quality judgments of LLM-generated chess commentary by decomposing explanations into atomic claims and checking them with engines and expert annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Superhuman chess engines can say what moves are good or bad, but they do not explain why in human-understandable language. Large language models can generate such explanations but often hallucinate, and existing generic evaluation methods (reference-based metrics or LLM-as-a-judge) cannot reliably detect these domain-specific factual errors. The authors want a precise, domain-grounded way to evaluate and improve LLM chess commentary so it becomes educationally useful and trustworthy.

Method: The authors design ACT-Eval, an evaluation framework that: (1) decomposes chess commentary into fine-grained atomic claims; (2) routes each atomic claim to appropriate engine-backed tools and expert-annotated gold references; and (3) scores models along three dimensions—factual correctness of claims, conceptual coverage of relevant ideas, and quality of move judgments. They construct a benchmark of 325 position–move pairs across pedagogical, tournament, and critical positions, with 125 positions having expert-verified gold atomic claims and a five-class error taxonomy for categorizing mistakes. They then apply ACT-Eval to compare proprietary and open-weight LLMs, with and without tool use.

Result: Using ACT-Eval, the authors find that factual hallucinations are still frequent in chess commentary. For instance, GPT-5.4 without tools produces incorrect atomic sub-claims 22% of the time, while smaller open-weight models exceed 40% error rates. Adding engine tools substantially improves factual correctness and move-quality judgment, but all models still show limited coverage of expert-level strategic and tactical concepts. Human calibration experiments show that ACT-Eval’s factual correctness judgments fall within the range of inter-human agreement and that its coverage scores correlate strongly with human assessments of how strategically complete a commentary is.

Conclusion: ACT-Eval provides a reliable, domain-grounded framework for evaluating LLM chess commentary by breaking explanations into engine-checkable atomic claims and comparing them to expert gold references. The study reveals that hallucinations remain a major issue, especially for smaller open models, and that tool integration helps but does not solve deficiencies in capturing expert strategic insight. The framework’s alignment with human judgments suggests it is a valid way to benchmark and guide the development of more accurate and pedagogically useful LLM-based chess explainers.

Abstract: Superhuman game engines in domains like chess have made expert-level evaluations easily accessible, yet they communicate what is true without the natural-language explanations that make such expertise educationally useful to experts and non-experts alike. Large language models could, in principle, bridge this gap, but they frequently hallucinate due to limited domain-specific knowledge, and standard reference-based or LLM-as-a-judge frameworks cannot reliably detect these errors. In this work, we present ACT-Eval, an evaluation framework that decomposes chess commentary into atomic claims and routes them to engine-supported tools and expert-annotated gold references to assess factual correctness, conceptual coverage, and move-quality judgment. We release a benchmark of 325 position--move pairs spanning pedagogical, tournament, and critical positions, including 125 positions with expert-verified gold atoms and a five-class error taxonomy. Evaluating leading proprietary and open-weight models, we find that factual hallucinations remain pervasive in chess commentary: GPT-5.4 without tools produces incorrect sub-claims 22.0% of the time, while smaller open-weight models exceed 40%. Although tool augmentation substantially improves factual correctness and move-quality assessment, coverage of expert strategic and tactical ideas remains limited across all models. Human calibration shows that ACT-Eval's factual judgments fall within the observed range of inter-human agreement, while its coverage scores correlate strongly with human assessments of strategic completeness.

</details>


### [11] [Towards End-to-End Multilingual Metaphor Processing: Integrating Detection, Translation, and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04260)
*Jiahui Liang,Lifeng Han*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Unified end-to-end framework for multilingual metaphor processing in NLP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metaphorical language poses significant challenges in multilingual NLP, especially for interpretation and translation that require reasoning beyond literal meanings. Prior work has typically treated metaphor detection, translation, and evaluation as separate problems, lacking an integrated computational framework to handle metaphor holistically.

Method: The proposal outlines three research directions: (1) developing robust, cross-lingual metaphor detection methods; (2) creating metaphor-oriented translation evaluation techniques for both human assessments and automatic quality estimation; and (3) building joint models that connect metaphor detection outputs with translation evaluation metrics. It plans to integrate linguistic theory with large language models to construct new datasets, annotation schemes, benchmarks, and automatic evaluators tailored to metaphor-aware MT.

Result: As this is a PhD proposal, concrete experimental results are not yet available. The expected results include improved metaphor detection across languages, more accurate metaphor-sensitive translation evaluation metrics, and successful joint models that leverage metaphor information to better evaluate translations.

Conclusion: The work is expected to yield a unified computational framework for multilingual metaphor processing that enhances both the development and evaluation of NLP systems dealing with figurative language, ultimately improving machine translation and related multilingual applications.

Abstract: Metaphorical language remains a major challenge for multilingual natural language processing because successful interpretation and translation require reasoning beyond literal lexical meaning. Existing research has largely investigated metaphor detection, machine translation, and translation evaluation as separate tasks, while little work has explored how these components can be integrated into a unified computational framework. This PhD proposal aims to develop an end-to-end framework for multilingual metaphor processing consisting of three complementary research directions: (1) robust metaphor detection across languages, (2) metaphor-oriented translation evaluation for both human assessment and automatic quality estimation, and (3) joint modelling that connects metaphor detection with translation evaluation. The proposed research will combine linguistic theory with recent advances in large language models to develop new datasets, annotation methodologies, evaluation benchmarks, and automatic evaluation approaches for metaphor-aware machine translation. The expected outcome is a unified framework that improves both the development and evaluation of multilingual NLP systems when processing figurative language.

</details>


### [12] [The Fairness Collapse Phenomenon: Bias Amplification in Language Models Trained on Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04268)
*Irina Proskurina,Antoine Gourru,Julien Velcin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how training language models repeatedly on their own synthetic data affects social bias, introducing the concept of 'fairness collapse' and showing that fairness degrades before general model quality metrics flag problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As synthetic content from generative models increasingly enters training corpora, there is concern about 'model collapse' where models degenerate when trained on their own outputs. However, less is known about how such recursive synthetic training impacts existing social biases in language models, which already reflect and sometimes amplify stereotypes. Understanding whether synthetic data exposure intensifies or mitigates these biases is crucial for safe continued pretraining on open data that may be contaminated by model-generated text.

Method: The authors design controlled training regimes using the Bias in Bios dataset, where language models are repeatedly trained (across multiple generations) on synthetic data produced by earlier versions of the model. They measure both standard language modeling performance metrics and fairness-related metrics that capture demographic bias and stereotype amplification, allowing them to compare when and how performance collapse and fairness degradation emerge during recursive training.

Result: Across experiments, they consistently find that fairness-related metrics deteriorate as models are trained on synthetic data over generations, indicating strengthening of biased associations. Importantly, this fairness degradation appears earlier than notable drops in standard language-modeling performance metrics, i.e., bias worsens before conventional indicators of model collapse reveal substantial performance loss.

Conclusion: Recursive training of language models on their own synthetic outputs poses a distinct fairness risk beyond general performance collapse. The hypothesized 'fairness collapse' is empirically supported: bias can silently increase as models are repeatedly exposed to their own stereotyped generations, and this degradation is not immediately visible in typical language-modeling metrics. Therefore, practitioners relying on open or synthetic-contaminated corpora must monitor and mitigate fairness impacts explicitly, not just track standard performance measures.

Abstract: Generative models trained on artificially generated data have been shown to exhibit model collapse, resulting in significant performance degradation. As synthetic content increasingly contaminates the training corpora of language models, this raises critical concerns about the use of open data in continued pretraining. Although previous work has demonstrated model collapse in language models, it remains unclear whether exposure to synthetic data amplifies or attenuates the social biases already present in pretrained models. Because language models are known to reproduce and amplify demographic stereotypes, recursive training on self-generated data may create a self-reinforcing feedback loop in which biased associations become progressively stronger across generations. We call this hypothesized phenomenon fairness collapse. In this work, we construct controlled training regimes in which models are repeatedly trained on synthetic data using the Bias in Bios dataset. Across experiments, we observe a consistent and concerning pattern: fairness degradation emerges before substantial degradation is reflected by standard language-modeling metrics. This result highlights a critical risk associated with synthetic data contamination in language model training: bias can increase silently before strong indicators of model collapse become apparent.

</details>


### [13] [Eliciting Intrinsic Hallucinations in LLMs via Semantically Equivalent Adversarial Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04286)
*Atri Vivek Sharma,Brian Formento,Alessio Lomuscio*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a framework to stress-test retrieval-augmented LLMs for intrinsic hallucinations using meaning-preserving adversarial query variants, showing that even state-of-the-art models become much less faithful to evidence under such perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs with retrieval, such as RAG systems, are meant to improve factual accuracy, but they still hallucinate information that is not supported by retrieved evidence. Existing evaluations often test a narrow set of prompts and do not systematically probe how small, semantics-preserving changes in user queries can trigger hallucinations. The authors want a principled way to measure how robust models are to such intrinsic hallucinations when the query wording changes but the meaning stays the same.

Method: They build a stress-testing framework that generates natural, semantically equivalent variants of a user query using adversarial optimization. The framework enforces strict semantic equivalence constraints while optimizing for an intrinsic hallucination objective—i.e., finding paraphrases that cause the model to generate content not grounded in the retrieved evidence. They instantiate this framework with multiple adversarial attack strategies across white-box, gray-box, and black-box settings, and evaluate a set of 5 open-source and 5 closed-source LLM generators on 3 datasets.

Result: Across all attack settings and datasets, the adversarial, meaning-preserving query variants substantially increase intrinsic hallucinations and reduce contextual faithfulness. Even strong, state-of-the-art models are vulnerable: for example, GPT-5-mini’s faithfulness can drop by up to 50% under these attacks. This shows that slight surface-form changes in queries are enough to seriously degrade how well models stick to the provided context.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that robust, faithful grounding of LLM outputs in retrieved evidence is still fragile, even for top models and even when query meaning is unchanged. The findings imply that current architectures and training methods are inadequate for ensuring reliable use of context under natural paraphrasing. The authors argue that future work should design models and objectives explicitly aimed at grounding robustness that is invariant to surface-level query form, and they release code to facilitate further research.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are often used in conjunction with external knowledge sources to improve their factual accuracy and decrease hallucinations, through methods such as Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG). However, these systems remain susceptible to intrinsic hallucinations, where the model generates unfaithful or fabricated information that is not supported by the retrieved evidence. We propose a novel framework to assess model robustness against this phenomenon by stress-testing using natural, semantically equivalent variations of a user query found via adversarial optimization methods. We apply our framework, which enforces strict semantic equivalence constraints and an intrinsic hallucination objective, to a range of adversarial attack techniques across white-box, gray-box, and black-box adversarial settings. Evaluating these attacks on 5 open-source and 5 closed-source generator models across 3 datasets, we demonstrate that even state-of-the-art models are highly susceptible to meaning-preserving perturbations, which significantly degrade contextual faithfulness (by up to 50% for GPT-5-mini). Our findings indicate that faithful use of in-context evidence remains fragile even in state-of-the-art LLMs, motivating architectures and training objectives that enforce robust grounding independent of surface query form. Code is available at: https://github.com/atriviveksharma/intrinsic_hall

</details>


### [14] [Searching for Sound-Meaning Collisions: Graph-Based Affordance Retrieval and Multi-Evaluator Ranking for Pun Translation at CLEF 2026 JOKER Task 2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04299)
*Russell Taylor,Adam Brikman,Prateek Awate*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Computational model of pun translation treats it as discovery of new sound-meaning overlaps in the target language rather than word equivalence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional pun translation focuses on preserving source words or direct equivalents, which often fails because puns rely on language-specific intersections of sound and meaning. Low’s theory suggests translators should instead seek new sound-meaning contact points in the target language; this paper tests and operationalizes that idea computationally.

Method: They formalize pun translation as a three-stage process: discovery, exploration, and selection. A retrieval system first searches semantic and phonological neighborhoods in the target language to find "affordances"—potential sound-meaning bridges usable for wordplay. Multiple language models then generate alternative translations that exploit these affordances. Finally, a generate-and-rank architecture, using multiple evaluators, scores and selects among candidate translations. They analyze how the retrieved affordances influence each stage.

Result: The study shows that generative models actively use the retrieved sound-meaning opportunities, and that evaluators increasingly favor candidates that realize stronger sound-meaning bridges. When exact phonological collisions are available, they are chosen much more frequently than chance. However, for many source puns, retrieval finds no useful affordances, preventing effective pun translation despite strong generation and ranking components.

Conclusion: Effective computational pun translation depends less on preserving original words and more on discovering new target-language sites where sound and meaning intersect, aligning closely with Low’s account of human pun translation. The critical bottleneck is the retrieval of suitable sound-meaning affordances; improving this component is key to further progress in automatic pun translation.

Abstract: Fifteen years ago, Low proposed that pun translators should stop searching for equivalent words and instead search for new points of contact between sound and meaning. In this paper, we investigate that idea computationally. We model pun translation as a process of discovery, exploration, and selection. A retrieval system searches semantic and phonological neighborhoods for target-language affordances: sound-meaning bridges that may support new wordplay. Multiple language models then explore these opportunities by generating competing translations, while a multi-perspective generate-and-rank architecture selects among them. Beyond system development, our primary contribution is an analysis of how retrieved affordances propagate through the translation process. We find that generators actively exploit retrieved opportunities, evaluators progressively concentrate around stronger sound-meaning bridges, and exact phonological collisions are selected at disproportionately high rates when available. At the same time, many puns still yield no usable affordances, suggesting that retrieval remains the central bottleneck in computational pun translation. The resulting picture is remarkably close to the process envisioned by Low. Successful pun translation emerges not from preserving source-language words, but from discovering new places in the target language where sound and meaning collide.

</details>


### [15] [MIDAS: Multi-LLM Iterative Data-Adaptive Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04307)
*Karen Lee,Dhanashree Balaram,Seojun Shon,Umair Rasheed*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MIDAS, a multi-LLM framework that automatically adapts summarization prompts to diverse enterprise requirements, outperforming existing critique-driven prompt optimization methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world enterprise summarization (e.g., support tickets, legal documents, incident reports) must follow strict domain-specific guidelines, formats, and conventions. Manually crafting and maintaining prompts that reliably meet these evolving constraints is costly and requires substantial human expertise. Existing automated, critique-driven prompt optimization methods still rely on static prompts and struggle to adapt across varied summarization use cases, motivating a more adaptive, data-driven solution.

Method: The authors propose MIDAS (Multi-LLM Iterative Data-Adaptive Summarization), a multi-LLM framework that extends critique-driven prompt optimization with data-driven pattern learning and use-case-specific personalization. MIDAS iteratively refines summarization behavior using multiple LLMs, learning patterns from data to automatically adapt prompts and generation strategies to different summarization formats and domain requirements, without manual prompt engineering. It is evaluated on enterprise customer ticket summarization across five output formats and further tested in cross-model and cross-domain settings, including finance-domain summarization.

Result: On enterprise customer ticket summarization across five output formats, MIDAS surpasses state-of-the-art critique-driven optimization frameworks like CriSPO and ZERA. It improves ROUGE-1 by up to 11.0%, ROUGE-2 by up to 18.2%, and ROUGE-L by up to 8.0%, and consistently boosts BERTScore F1 across all formats and output types. Additional experiments show that MIDAS generalizes across different LLMs and performs well on finance-domain summarization benchmarks, evidencing robust cross-model and cross-domain adaptability.

Conclusion: MIDAS effectively automates the adaptation of summarization systems to diverse, domain-specific requirements without heavy manual prompt engineering. By combining multi-LLM critique with data-adaptive pattern learning and personalization, it outperforms existing critique-driven prompt optimization methods and demonstrates strong generalization across models and domains, suggesting a promising direction for scalable, enterprise-grade summarization solutions.

Abstract: Text summarization is deceptively difficult. While condensing information seems straightforward, real-world enterprise summarization of support tickets, legal documents, incident reports, and more, demands strict adherence to domain-specific guidelines, output formats, and organizational conventions. Crafting prompts that reliably satisfy these constraints is labor-intensive, requiring significant human expertise and continuous maintenance as requirements evolve. Existing automated prompt optimization methods reduce this burden through Large Language Model (LLM) critique-driven refinement, yet remain limited by static prompts that cannot adapt to the diversity of summary applications. We propose Multi-LLM Iterative Data-Adaptive Summarization (MIDAS), a multi-LLM framework that extends this paradigm with data-driven pattern learning and use-case-specific personalization, enabling automatic adaptation to different summarization requirements without manual prompt engineering. Applied to enterprise customer ticket summarization across five output formats, MIDAS achieves the strongest overall performance against state-of-the-art critique-driven optimization frameworks such as CriSPO and ZERA, improving ROUGE-1 by up to 11.0%, ROUGE-2 by up to 18.2%, and ROUGE-L by up to 8.0%, while consistently improving BERTScore F1 across all formats and output types. We additionally demonstrate cross-model and cross-domain generalization through multi-LLM configurations and finance-domain summarization benchmarks.

</details>


### [16] [Pun Intended: Multi-Agent Translation of Wordplay with Contrastive Learning and Phonetic-Semantic Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04311)
*Russell Taylor,Benjamin Herbert,Michael Sana*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares three LLM-based frameworks for translating English puns into French and shows that multi-agent and phonetic-semantic guided systems best preserve humor and creativity, outperforming a discriminator-guided baseline under human evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Translating puns is difficult because it requires preserving creativity, ambiguity, and humor, not just literal meaning or vocabulary. Existing MT and LLM systems focus on lexical or semantic fidelity and standard automatic metrics like BLEU, which poorly capture success for wordplay. The authors aim to design and evaluate translation pipelines that better handle linguistic creativity and measure performance using expert human judgments.

Method: The authors propose and compare three pipelines for English-to-French pun translation built around large language models: (1) a discriminator-guided baseline where an LLM generates French outputs and a prompted discriminator provides feedback using positive/negative examples; (2) a guided reasoning approach that uses combined phonetic-semantic embeddings to retrieve French lexical candidates that are then used by the LLM to construct playful translations; and (3) a multi-agent framework in which multiple specialized agents iteratively generate, evaluate, and refine candidate translations with feedback focused on semantics, phonetics, humor, and naturalness. They evaluate the systems in the CLEF JOKER 2025 Task 2 shared task, using both automatic metrics (BLEU, BERTScore) and expert human assessments.

Result: In the CLEF JOKER 2025 Task 2 competition, the multi-agent framework achieved the best performance under expert human evaluation, with the phonetic-semantic guided reasoning pipeline ranking second. Both significantly outperformed the discriminator-only baseline in terms of preserving wordplay, humor, and overall quality. However, improvements over the baseline in standard automatic metrics such as BLEU and BERTScore were only modest, highlighting a mismatch between these metrics and human perceptions of successful wordplay translation.

Conclusion: Explicitly integrating phonetic-semantic guidance and iterative multi-agent evaluation into LLM-based translation pipelines improves the quality of pun translations compared to simple discriminator-guided generation. Success in wordplay translation depends on balancing semantic fidelity, phonetic similarity, and naturalness in the target language, which is better captured by human evaluation than by traditional automatic metrics. The work underscores the need for specialized architectures and evaluation criteria when tackling creative language phenomena like puns.

Abstract: Translating wordplay across languages has long challenged both professional translators and machine translation systems. We investigate three approaches to translating puns from English to French by combining large language models with linguistic constraints for wordplay generation. Our baseline uses a large language model with feedback from a discriminator prompted with positive and negative French examples. Our guided reasoning pipeline uses combined phonetic-semantic embeddings to retrieve lexical candidates for wordplay generation. Finally, our multi-agent framework iteratively evaluates and regenerates candidate translations using specialized feedback. Moving beyond literal translation, our objective is to preserve the linguistic creativity, ambiguity, and humor of the source-text wordplay rather than simply reproduce its vocabulary. The multi-agent and guided chain-of-thought systems ranked first and second, respectively, in the CLEF JOKER 2025 Task 2 competition under expert human evaluation, despite only modest improvements in BLEU and BERTScore. These findings suggest that both explicit phonetic-semantic guidance and iterative multi-agent evaluation can improve LLM-based wordplay translation relative to direct discriminator-guided generation, particularly when balancing semantic fidelity, phonetic similarity, and natural target-language expression

</details>


### [17] [DataRx: Missingness-Aware Sampling for Safer Large Language Model Task-Specific Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04322)
*Junbo Zhang,Qianli Zhou,Xinyang Deng,Wen Jiang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Task-specific fine-tuning of LLMs can degrade safety. The paper proposes DataRx, a missingness-aware sampling method that selects the most safety-critical data by comparing hidden representations of unsafe model outputs and safe reference answers, substantially reducing attack success rates with minimal extra safety data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While task-specific fine-tuning improves downstream performance, it can erode safety alignment of LLMs. Existing approaches randomly mix safety data into fine-tuning, which helps but lacks a principled understanding of why certain safety examples are more protective. The paper is motivated by the need for a data-centric, theoretically grounded way to select the most effective safety examples for preserving and enhancing safety during fine-tuning.

Method: The authors introduce DataRx, a missingness-aware sampling method. They hypothesize that the best safety samples are those that fill gaps in a model’s safety capabilities. To operationalize this, they compare high-dimensional hidden representations of the model’s native (possibly unsafe) responses to those of a safety reference response for each candidate safety example. By quantifying the “safety signal gap” in representation space, DataRx identifies and selects safety-critical examples whose safety signals are most missing from the target model. This selected subset is then used as additional safety data during task-specific fine-tuning.

Result: Using Llama3-8B-Instruct on seven downstream tasks, adding only 1% extra safety samples from the BeaverTails dataset but selected via DataRx reduces the average attack success rate from 59.23% (random safety-data sampling) to 13.70%. The approach is also shown to be compatible with existing safety data synthesis methods, enabling further improvements in safety defenses during fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Task-specific fine-tuning can significantly weaken LLM safety, but carefully chosen safety data can counteract this. DataRx provides a principled, representation-based method to select safety-critical examples that fill gaps in a model’s safety behavior, achieving large safety gains with minimal additional data. The work underscores the importance of data-centric approaches to safety and suggests that leveraging hidden representations is an effective strategy for robust safety preservation during fine-tuning.

Abstract: Task-specific fine-tuning can improve the performance of large language models (LLMs) on downstream tasks. However, our study reveals that task-specific fine-tuning can also weaken the safety guardrails of aligned LLMs. A widely adopted strategy for preserving safety during fine-tuning is to incorporate safety data. Although previous studies have shown that randomly mixing safety data can alleviate safety degradation, the underlying principle determining why some safety examples are more effective than others still remains unclear. In this paper, we propose DataRx, a missingness-aware sampling method for selecting safety-critical examples. DataRx is based on the hypothesis that a safety sample is more effective when the selected examples provide safety signals that fill the missing parts of LLMs' safety capabilities. DataRx's key insight is leveraging high-dimensional hidden representations rather than discrete tokens to quantify the safety signal gap between the target model's native response and the safety reference response. The results show that, with only 1% additional safety samples from BeaverTails, DataRx reduces the average attack success rate of Llama3-8B-Instruct across seven downstream tasks from 59.23% under random sampling to 13.70%. In addition, DataRx can be combined with the existing safety data synthesis method to further enhance safety defenses during fine-tuning. We hope that DataRx will inspire more data-centric defense research.

</details>


### [18] [Right Reset: Chunking by Prefix Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04330)
*Mike Vegeto*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose Right Reset (RR), a probing method for language models that identifies boundaries in text by checking where removing the left context minimally affects processing of the right-hand tokens. RR segments flattened, low-structure text into original records better than embedding-based baselines and is robust across renderings and models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When text is flattened (e.g., database records concatenated without separators, or OCR output that loses layout), conventional surface cues and separators vanish, making it hard to recover record boundaries without task-specific training. The authors notice that causal language models often have locations where removing prior context has surprisingly little effect on how they process upcoming tokens; these points might align with natural boundaries in the data. They want to exploit this as a general, model-based signal of structure when layout and separators are weak or missing.

Method: They define prefix-removal probing: take a causal language model, remove varying amounts of left context, and compare the resulting hidden-state trajectories for a fixed right-hand token sequence. Right Reset (RR) is a metric that quantifies how much of the original right-hand hidden-state trajectory is preserved when the prefix is removed. Using a dynamic programming algorithm over RR scores at candidate edges, they identify variable-length chunks in a long sequence. They evaluate RR on flattened text formed from topically similar records (originally separated, then concatenated and stripped of separators/layout). RR’s ability to reconstruct the original records is compared to multiple baselines: especially a strong embedding-based boundary method using BGE embeddings, as well as passive scores from the same LM layer and direct prompting of an instruction-tuned model. They also test variants like an observed-token likelihood-ratio readout in some architectures and measure local output disruption around RR-selected vs. other edges across six LMs.

Result: On the flattened record-recovery task, RR correctly recovers 47.7% of the original records as clean, intact units, versus 25.9% for the best baseline (a BGE embedding-based boundary detector), all without task-specific training. The improvement remains even after rendering the records and re-obtaining text via OCR, showing robustness to realistic noise. Passive scores and direct prompting using similar-scale models perform substantially worse. Across six different language models, edges selected by RR exhibit consistently less local output disruption if cut there than other candidate edges, indicating that RR indeed finds more natural boundaries. In some model architectures, a simpler observed-token likelihood-ratio readout approximates RR’s performance, suggesting the key benefit comes from the active prefix-removal intervention rather than the specific hidden-state metric.

Conclusion: Context dependence in causal language models can itself be used as a boundary signal when surface cues like separators and layout are weak or absent. By actively intervening on left context and measuring how much the processing of subsequent tokens changes, their Right Reset (RR) method identifies natural segmentation points more accurately than strong embedding-based and prompting baselines. This approach is robust across models and noisy text conditions and suggests that probing context-reset behavior is a powerful way to recover latent structure from flattened text without task-specific training.

Abstract: Removing the left context from a causal language model reveals a useful kind of boundary: an edge where the model processes the same right-hand tokens with little change. We turn this observation into prefix-removal probing and introduce Right Reset (RR), which measures preservation of the right-hand hidden-state trajectory. A dynamic program converts RR edge scores into variable-length chunks. On flattened text formed by concatenating topically similar records after deleting their separators and layout, RR recovers 47.7% of the original records as clean units, versus 25.9% for a BGE embedding boundary, the strongest tested conventional baseline without task-specific model training. The gain persists after rendering and OCR. Passive scores from the same Qwen3-4B layer and direct prompting of a same-scale instruction model perform substantially worse on flattened records. Across six language models, RR-selected cuts also undergo consistently less local output disruption than unselected candidate edges. An observed-token likelihood-ratio readout is competitive in some architectures, indicating that the central contribution is the intervention: context dependence itself can provide a boundary signal when surface structure is weak.

</details>


### [19] [Equitable System-Prompt Selection via Constrained Mixed-Strategy GroupDRO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04339)
*Mengyu Xu,Qiaoxin Yang,Zhihan Liu,Ruiyao Xu,Zachary Liu,Kezhen Chen,Chongyang Gao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a method to select and mix system prompts for LLMs that improves worst-case answer quality across different phrasings and groups, while keeping average performance high.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Different but semantically equivalent user queries can get very different answer quality from LLMs, and current system prompts tuned for average performance do not protect against these worst-case failures. There is a need for a systematic way to choose prompts that make responses more equitable across query phrasings, groups, and evaluation metrics, without sacrificing overall performance.

Method: They cast system-prompt selection as a constrained mixed-strategy Group Distributionally Robust Optimization (GroupDRO) problem. Instead of editing a single prompt, they start from a pool of existing system prompts and learn weights over them. These weights are chosen to minimize the worst-case information-quality loss across multiple evaluation metrics and user groups, while enforcing a constraint that the mean loss remains close to that achieved by a standard average-based selection. Because the prompt-generation phase is separate from the selection phase, their method can be applied to any pre-defined pool and effectively forms an ensemble of complementary prompts rather than committing to a single prompt.

Result: On five different large language models and two bilingual benchmarks in the medical and consumer-finance domains, their constrained GroupDRO-based selection reduces several worst-case loss measures (Overall Mean, Worst 25% Mean, and Worst-case metrics) by around 13% on average compared to no mitigation. At the same time, the overall answer quality stays close to that obtained by simply choosing prompts based on average performance. Analysis of the learned weights shows that different prompts are favored for different metric-group combinations, indicating complementary strengths among prompts.

Conclusion: Using a constrained mixed-strategy GroupDRO approach to select and mix system prompts from a fixed pool can substantially improve worst-case and tail performance of LLM answers across query variations and groups, without meaningfully hurting average performance. The framework is flexible, model-agnostic, and reveals useful complementarity among prompts, making it a practical way to achieve more equitable information quality in real-world LLM deployments.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used for information seeking, yet semantically equivalent questions phrased in different ways can receive answers of considerably different quality. System prompts are widely employed to steer response behavior, but they are typically optimized for average-case quality, so some question phrasings may still receive incomplete or low-quality answers. To address this, we formulate a constrained mixed-strategy GroupDRO framework for system-prompt selection. Instead of optimizing the system-prompt text, the framework assigns weights to system prompts in an existing pool to minimize the worst-case information-quality loss across evaluation metrics and groups, while constraining the mean loss to stay close to that of average-based selection. Because pool generation and selection are decoupled, the method applies to any system-prompt pool and can leverage an ensemble of complementary system prompts rather than a single one. Across five LLMs on two bilingual medical and consumer-finance benchmarks, the constrained method reduces the Overall Mean, Worst 25% Mean, and Worst by 13.1%, 13.2%, and 13.7% on average relative to no mitigation while keeping overall quality close to Average selection. Its multi-prompt weights reveal complementarity across metric-group pairs. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Rainxu09/equitable-system-prompt-selection.

</details>


### [20] [The Calibration Floor: Format Repair Can Masquerade as Self-Correction at Small-to-Mid Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04355)
*Mingguang Chen,Bo Qu,Licheng Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that accuracy changes after language-model self-revision are often due more to formatting/parseability issues than genuine reasoning/content improvements, using causal tests across multiple models and scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers often interpret accuracy improvements after a model revises its own answer as better reasoning or self-correction. However, answer extraction pipelines usually depend on parsing the final answer string, so formatting or parseability changes can artificially inflate or deflate measured accuracy. The authors want to disentangle genuine content-level reasoning changes from mere formatting effects and to test this in a causal, not just observational, way across model scales.

Method: They study multiple LLMs (Qwen3.5 variants, Gemma-4-12B, Tencent Hy3, Nvidia Nemotron-3-Ultra-550B) across many experimental “cells.” For each cell, they always force a revision step and decompose the shift in measured accuracy into (1) a content margin where both answers are parseable and differ in correctness, and (2) format-recovery or format-loss margins where one of the answers is unparseable. They then run a causal intervention: they take already-generated reasoning traces and pass them through grammar-constrained decoding so that all final answers are parseable by design. This allows them to see how much of the self-revision accuracy change remains when formatting is controlled. They also build a clustered model to compare floor-scale (0.8B/2B) versus capable-scale models, and they replicate a published confidence-gating protocol to see if its reported gains persist under this decomposition. Finally, they run a frontier-model arm and analyze calibration via a content-margin “calibration floor” criterion.

Result: In 12 cells with nontrivial unparseable-answer rates, the formatting margins (recovery/loss from parseability changes) are significantly larger than the content margin (Wilcoxon p=1.7e-3).
