Under grammar-constrained decoding, which makes every answer parseable, a median of 71% of the gap between the naive total effect and the content-margin estimate disappears across 14 cells; in two cells, the naive and content-margin effects converge exactly, with only a small residual on two large-effect cells. The clustered model shows that small “floor-scale” models (0.8B/2B) have much higher odds that self-revision actually changes content correctness and can be harmful, compared to larger “capable-scale” models (p<1e-7). The verbatim replication of a cited confidence-gating protocol on Qwen3.5 fails to reproduce the original reported accuracy gains and instead shows nearly zero content margin. In the frontier-model arm, larger models show even stronger format dominance: the content margin is exactly zero in all 5 cells, despite large total effects (up to +0.275), though this part of the study is statistically underpowered. The calibration-floor analysis shows that small models have potential headroom but poor signal, and larger models have signal but little headroom; only one cell barely qualifies as viable, and its sealed-holdout gains are negligible.

Conclusion: Most of what has been reported as LLM “self-correction” from revision or confidence-gating is not genuine reasoning improvement but instead an artifact of formatting and parseability at the answer-extraction boundary. When formatting is controlled causally via grammar-constrained decoding, the apparent accuracy gains mostly disappear, leaving a small content margin that shrinks further for larger, more capable models. Small models can theoretically benefit more at the content level but are too noisy, while large models are already near a calibration floor. The authors argue that future work on self-correction and evaluation must explicitly separate content from format, and they provide instruments, code, and derived results to support more reliable measurement.

Abstract: Accuracy changes after language-model self-revision are usually interpreted as changes in reasoning. We show this can fail at the answer-extraction boundary, and test the failure causally rather than only observationally. Across Qwen3.5 (0.8B-9B), Gemma-4-12B, and two frontier models via API (Tencent Hy3, Nvidia Nemotron-3-Ultra-550B) in 29 primary cells plus a frontier arm, we decompose the always-revise accuracy shift into a content margin (both answers parseable) and format-recovery/loss margins (parseability changes). On 12 cells with meaningful unparseable-answer rates, format effects exceed content effects (Wilcoxon p=1.7e-3). To test this causally, we force already-generated reasoning through grammar-constrained decoding so every answer is parseable by construction: across 14 cells this closes a median 71% of the gap between the naive total effect and the content-margin estimate, with two cells converging exactly and a residual on the two largest-effect cells reported rather than dismissed. A clustered model confirms floor-scale (0.8B/2B) models have far higher odds of content-level change and harm than capable-scale models (p<1e-7). Replicating a cited confidence-gating protocol verbatim on Qwen3.5 does not reproduce its reported gain and shows the same near-zero content margin. A frontier check on much larger models shows format-dominance intensifying with scale: content margin is exactly zero in all 5 cells despite total effects up to +0.275, though this arm is lower-powered. The calibration-floor criterion on the content margin reveals a squeeze: floor-scale cells have headroom but insufficient signal, capable-scale cells have signal but little headroom; only one cell is marginally viable, with negligible sealed-holdout gain. Content is a minority share of what the field has measured as self-correction. We release the instrument, code, and derived results.

</details>


### [21] [FinReportBench: Measuring and Improving Institution-Grade Financial Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04374)
*Yinghao Tang,Tan Zhenwei,Yiyao Wang,Wanli Gu,Xiaolu Zhang,Jun Zhou,Wei Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FinReportBench is a new expert-designed benchmark for evaluating whether LLM-generated financial reports are suitable for institutional use, plus a distilled “skill” that significantly improves models’ report quality on this benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing large language models can write fluent financial analysis, but institutions need reports that meet strict standards of deliverability, identity, completeness, and data discipline—not just fluency. There was no systematic, expert-grounded way to measure and improve whether LLM-generated reports satisfy these institution-grade requirements.

Method: The authors worked with financial experts to identify recurring failure patterns in LLM-generated reports, then formalized these into a 35-item rubric spanning deliverability, report identity, and institutional completeness. They built FinReportBench from 10,000 Chinese and English financial-research source records, yielding 244 bilingual tasks that separate user-facing queries, reconstructed research trajectories, and hidden source packets. They validated the rubric and task design via three independent judge families that successfully replicate expert partial orders. They then ran nine model families on the benchmark, analyzed performance gaps, and used these insights to perform benchmark-guided skill distillation that encodes better generation and self-review constraints into models.

Result: The benchmark shows that while most models already achieve basic deliverability, they still struggle with maintaining a clear and appropriate report identity and fulfilling institutional completeness requirements, especially around generation traceability, information density, and disciplined use of data sources. The distilled “report-writing skill” derived from the benchmark’s insights, when applied across five model families, yields large gains: an average improvement of 33.85 points on the G1 metric and 13.83 points on G2, with no degradation of the baseline G0 metric.

Conclusion: FinReportBench provides a reliable, expert-aligned framework for evaluating institution-grade financial report generation and reveals that the main challenges are not surface fluency but deeper issues of identity, completeness, and data discipline. Moreover, the benchmark can actively guide training: by turning observed failure modes into explicit generation and self-review constraints, the authors obtain a transferable skill that substantially raises report quality across diverse model families without harming basic deliverability.

Abstract: Large language models can produce fluent financial analysis, but fluency alone does not establish whether a report is suitable for institutional delivery. We introduce FinReportBench, an expert-grounded benchmark for measuring and improving institution-grade financial report generation. Expert review reveals recurring gaps in report identity, institutional components, source discipline, and visual delivery. We derive a 35-item rubric through expert partial orders, multimodal evidence, and audits of decision boundaries, covering deliverability, report identity, and institutional completeness. Starting from 10,000 balanced Chinese and English financial-research source records, we curate 244 bilingual tasks across three research objects and two input tiers. Each task separates the public query, reconstructed research trajectory, and hidden source packet. Three independent judge families reproduce the expert partial order at near-ceiling rates, showing that bounded, observable criteria support reliable evaluation. Across nine model families, basic deliverability is nearly saturated, while report identity and institutional completeness remain the primary bottlenecks. The largest cross-model gaps concern generation-trace control, information density, and data discipline rather than basic report framing. We then use benchmark-guided skill distillation to turn recurrent failures into reusable generation and self-review constraints. Across five model families, the evolved skill improves mean G1 by 33.85 points and mean G2 by 13.83 points over paired no-skill runs while preserving G0 for every pair. Code and benchmark artifacts are available at https://github.com/MisterBrookT/finreportbench.

</details>


### [22] [EdgeLM: Edge Demonstrations for Language Models' Table Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04390)
*Soroush Omidvartehrani,Mohammadamin Habibollah,Mohammadreza Daviran,Davood Rafiei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EdgeLM improves LLM table-centric prediction by retrieving "edge" demonstrations that highlight decision boundaries instead of just similar examples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard in-context example retrieval based on similarity tends to surface demonstrations that are too easy and confirm the model’s current bias, which is unhelpful for ambiguous or borderline cases in table-centric tasks. The authors want a retrieval scheme that provides information about where decisions change—the decision boundary—without retraining models or using task-specific tricks.

Method: They introduce EdgeLM, a retrieval framework that selects two kinds of "edge evidence" demonstrations for in-context learning: (1) data edges—examples that are close to the query in input space but have different ground-truth labels, and (2) model edges—examples that are similar to the query and have previously been misclassified by the deployed model. EdgeLM retrieves and combines these edge examples as demonstrations, without fine-tuning or architecture changes, and can be used with different LLMs.

Result: On five data wrangling tasks across fifteen datasets and five different LLMs (both open-weight and proprietary), EdgeLM achieves best or near-best performance in all setups. Ablation studies demonstrate that both data-edge and model-edge retrieval each provide gains, and that using both together yields complementary improvements over baselines that only use similarity-based retrieval.

Conclusion: Focusing retrieval on edge evidence—examples near decision boundaries and prior model errors—leads to more informative in-context demonstrations for table-centric prediction. EdgeLM is model-agnostic, requires no retraining, and consistently improves performance, suggesting decision-boundary-aware retrieval is a powerful and general strategy for LLM-based table reasoning and data wrangling.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) perform table-centric prediction through in-context learning, making demonstration selection critical to performance. Existing retrieval methods prioritize similarity to the query, but similar demonstrations often reinforce the model's likely prediction rather than reveal the distinctions needed for difficult decisions. We propose EdgeLM, a retrieval framework that instead selects edge evidence, demonstrations that are both relevant to the query and informative about the decision boundary. EdgeLM retrieves two complementary forms of edge evidence by selecting data edges, nearby examples with different ground-truth labels, and model edges, similar examples previously misclassified by the deployed model. EdgeLM requires neither model retraining nor task-specific engineering. Across five data wrangling tasks, fifteen datasets, and five open-weight and proprietary LLMs, EdgeLM consistently achieves the best or near-best performance in every setting, while ablations show that the two forms of edge evidence provide complementary benefits. Our code and datasets are publicly available at https://github.com/soroushomidvar/EdgeLM.

</details>


### [23] [NOLLI: A Difficulty-Calibrated Puzzle Benchmark for Diagnosing the English-Korean Performance Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04397)
*Dasol Choi,Joonyong Park,Daegon Yu,Soo Yong Kim,Youngsook Song,Seunghyeok Hong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents NOLLI, a procedurally generated English-Korean puzzle benchmark that precisely diagnoses where Korean-language performance gaps arise in LLMs, showing that gaps are small for direct translations but large for certain writing-system and Korean-specific tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multilingual benchmarks often cannot pinpoint *why* models underperform in specific languages, especially for structurally different languages like Korean. Researchers lack fine-grained, controlled tools to separate effects of translation, script (Hangul jamo vs Latin), culture-specific knowledge, and task difficulty. There is also a need to move beyond crude size-based difficulty and toward behaviorally calibrated difficulty that reflects actual model performance. This paper aims to create such a diagnostic benchmark focused on Korean, enabling detailed analysis of where and how performance gaps emerge.

Method: The authors design NOLLI, a procedurally generated benchmark of 15 puzzle types (25 tasks, 7,500 items) spanning three levels: (1) matched direct English-Korean translations; (2) script adaptation puzzles exploiting Hangul jamo (sub-syllabic letters); and (3) Korean-only tasks tied to Korean culture or orthography. Each item is seed-regenerable, has a unique solution, and is deterministically scored. Difficulty is behaviorally calibrated: puzzle generators are tuned so that a fixed reference model hits target accuracy bands rather than just increasing structural size. They evaluate 15 large language models (frontier, open-weight, and Korean-specialized), focusing on 12 that exceed a minimal accuracy floor. They compare English vs Korean accuracy, apply TOST equivalence tests, and analyze performance patterns across writing-system-intensive and Korean-only tasks, relating subtask accuracies (e.g., Jamo Composition vs Korean Cipher).

Result: Among the 12 reasonably performing models, English-Korean accuracy on directly matched tasks is statistically equivalent within ±10 percentage points, implying minimal loss from changing presentation language alone. However, tasks that heavily rely on the Korean writing system reveal much larger gaps: for example, Korean Cipher performance can lag English by up to 68.7 percentage points, whereas Cryptarithmetic puzzles using the same jamo do not show consistent penalties. Accuracy on Jamo Composition predicts Korean Cipher performance, highlighting a link to sub-syllabic processing. For Korean-only tasks, the benchmark separates rule-application deficits (which differ by task and model) from a consistent difficulty with Kinship relations, where all 12 models underperform. The study also finds that in 7 of the 15 puzzle types, a salient structural size measure does not increase from Easy to Hard, undermining size as a proxy for actual empirical difficulty.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that raw presentation language (English vs Korean) is not the primary driver of performance gaps on matched tasks for current LLMs; instead, major weaknesses arise in tasks requiring multi-step sub-syllabic manipulation of the Hangul writing system and in certain Korean-specific reasoning domains such as kinship. NOLLI’s behaviorally calibrated, procedurally generated design allows fine-grained diagnosis of these weaknesses, demonstrating that structural size is an unreliable stand-in for difficulty. The benchmark offers a scalable, regenerable tool to analyze and ultimately improve model performance on Korean, with implications for designing better multilingual and script-sensitive evaluations.

Abstract: We introduce NOLLI, a procedurally generated English-Korean puzzle benchmark designed to diagnose where Korean performance gaps arise. It comprises 15 puzzle types (25 tasks; 7,500 items), with every instance seed-regenerable, verified to have a unique solution, and scored deterministically. Rather than equating harder with bigger, we calibrate difficulty behaviorally, tuning each generator until a fixed reference model lands in target accuracy bands. Its three-level design combines matched direct translations, script adaptations over Hangul jamo (sub-syllabic letters), and Korean-only tasks grounded in Korean culture or orthography. We evaluate 15 frontier, open-weight, and Korean-developed models; among the 12 above a 3% overall-accuracy floor, matched English-Korean accuracy is statistically equivalent within a +/- 10 pp margin (TOST), suggesting little cost from presentation language alone. Writing-system-intensive tasks show sharper gaps: Korean Cipher falls behind English by up to 68.7 pp, whereas Cryptarithmetic over the same jamo shows no systematic penalty, and Jamo Composition accuracy predicts Korean Cipher accuracy. These contrasts are diagnostic rather than causal, consistent with difficulty in multi-step sub-syllabic execution. Korean-only tasks separate rule-application deficits, which vary in sign, from a Kinship deficit positive in all 12. Finally, a salient size measure fails to grow from Easy to Hard in 7 of 15 types, making structural size an unreliable proxy for empirical difficulty.

</details>


### [24] [Social Pressure Breaks Majority Voting in LLM Safety Panels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04415)
*Yibo Hu,Jiaming Qu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that panels of LLM safety reviewers can systematically over-flag safe content as unsafe when all models are exposed to the same misleading “unsafe” cue, driving panel false-alarm rates up to 100%, and proposes this as a diagnostic failure mode to test before deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are widely used for content moderation and safety classification, often with multiple models voting to reduce individual errors. However, if all models are influenced by the same biased or misleading context (e.g., others confidently calling something unsafe), this collective setup might amplify errors instead of correcting them. The paper is motivated by the need to understand whether and how such social-influence-like effects occur in LLM panels and how they affect false alarms versus misses for harmful content.

Method: The authors run a controlled two-round experiment on six open-weight LLMs across six safety-related datasets. In round one, each model labels items (safe/unsafe) independently. In round two, the same model relabels the item after being shown simulated peer messages: either six peers unanimously asserting the incorrect label or six peers abstaining without giving a label. The final judgments are then aggregated via majority vote to emulate a panel. They systematically compare error rates (false alarms and harmful misses) across conditions (wrong-label vs abstain, push toward unsafe vs safe) and also run a smaller probe on proprietary models to assess whether the effect generalizes.

Result: When LLMs see peers confidently asserting an incorrect label, their false-alarm rates rise sharply. On average, across models and datasets, the wrong-label peer messages increase the individual reviewer false-alarm rate from 56.5% (with silent peers) to 87.5%. When these influenced judgments are combined via majority vote, the panel false-alarm rate reaches 100% in these conditions. In contrast, panels without an asserted label (only peer abstentions) perform better than the average individual model. The influence is asymmetric: models are much more likely to follow cues that something is “unsafe” than cues that something is “safe” (approximately 75% vs 17% compliance). Harmful-miss rates change little, indicating that the main effect is an over-flagging of content as unsafe. Proprietary models show similar but varied susceptibility.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that LLM safety panels are vulnerable to a form of social conformity: when all models share the same misleading “unsafe” cue, majority voting can systematically amplify false alarms instead of correcting them. This susceptibility is strongly asymmetric toward over-predicting “unsafe,” leaving harmful-miss rates relatively unaffected but markedly reducing usability by over-blocking benign content. The authors frame this as a concrete failure mode of safety-panel design and propose their experimental setup as a straightforward pre-deployment diagnostic for testing models’ robustness to shared social cues and adversarial context in content-moderation pipelines.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to detect unsafe content. A common approach is to combine judgments from a panel of models to correct individual mistakes, but this benefit may disappear when every model sees the same misleading context before voting. We study this risk in a controlled two-round experiment. Each model first judges an item alone, then judges it again after six simulated peers either assert the wrong label or abstain. We combine the final judgments by majority vote. Across six open-weight LLMs and six datasets, we find that the wrong-label peer message raises the average reviewer false-alarm rate from 56.5% under silent peers to 87.5%, and majority voting raises the panel false-alarm rate to 100%. Without an asserted label, the same panel outperforms its average member. The effect is strongly asymmetric: reviewers follow pushes toward "unsafe" far more than pushes toward "safe" (about 75% versus 17%), so the panel's false-alarm rate rises sharply while its harmful-miss rate changes little. The proprietary-model probe shows substantial variation across models. These results identify susceptibility to shared social cues as a failure mode of safety panels and provide a simple pre-deployment diagnostic.

</details>


### [25] [MERaLiON-GR: Speech Gender Recognition Model for English and SEA Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04433)
*Qiongqiong Wang,Ai Ti Aw,Nancy F. Chen,Ying Lay Chiu,Yang Ding,Yingxu He,Ridong Jiang,Zhuohan Liu,Yanfeng Lu,Yi Ma,Muhammad Huzaifah,Nabilah Binte Md Johan,Nattadaporn Lertcheva,Pham Minh Duc,Sailor Hardik Bhupendra,Siti Umairah Binte Mohammad Salleh,Shuo Sun,Tarun Kumar Vangani,Jeremy H. M. Wong,Jinyang Wu,Longyin Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MERaLiON-GR is a multilingual speech gender recognition model that fine-tunes a large conformer-based speech encoder with LoRA and adds an ECAPA-TDNN head, achieving state-of-the-art results across several SEA languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To build a robust, accurate, and cross-lingual speech gender recognition system for English and multiple Southeast Asian languages, and to show that task-specialized speech models can outperform existing SOTA systems and general-purpose audio LLMs for paralinguistic tasks.

Method: Start from MERaLiON-SpeechEncoder-2, a large conformer-based transformer pre-trained on broad speech data. Apply parameter-efficient fine-tuning with Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to adapt the encoder to gender recognition. Attach a multi-scale ECAPA-TDNN downstream network with attention pooling, followed by a lightweight linear classifier. Train and evaluate on multilingual Singaporean and SEA speech (English, Chinese, Malay, Tamil, Thai, Vietnamese, Indonesian, Khmer) in both full-utterance and segment-level modes.

Result: MERaLiON-GR consistently outperforms the state-of-the-art gender recognition model Vox-Profile and a large Audio-LLM across all evaluated SEA languages, for both full-utterance and segment-level gender classification.

Conclusion: A dedicated, task-specific speech model built via parameter-efficient fine-tuning of a strong pre-trained encoder plus an ECAPA-TDNN head can deliver superior speech gender recognition performance and strong cross-lingual generalization compared with existing SOTA models and generic Audio-LLMs, highlighting the value of specialized paralinguistic models.

Abstract: We present MERaLiON-GR, a speech gender recognition system that performs binary classification (female / male) on English and Southeast Asian (SEA) languages. The model finetunes MERaLiON-SpeechEncoder-2, a large conformer based transformer pre-trained on a broad speech corpus, and applies parameter efficient fine-tuning via Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to adapt the encoder to the gender recognition task, and appends a multi-scale ECAPA-TDNN down stream network with attention pooling and a lightweight linear classifier. Extensive evaluations across multilingual Singaporean and Southeast Asian languages (English, Chinese, Malay, Tamil, Thai, Vietnamese, Indonesian, and Khmer) show that MERaLiON-GR consistently surpasses the state-of-the-art gender recognition model Vox-Profile and a large Audio-LLM, in both full-utterance and segment level evaluation modes. The results underscore the value of dedicated speech models in achieving accurate paralinguistic understanding and strong cross-lingual generalization.

</details>


### [26] [D$^2$F-ReAG: Dynamic Decomposition and Filtering for Multi-Hop Reasoning-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04444)
*Jiaoyang Li,Junhao Ruan,Shengwei Tang,Kaiyan Chang,Zhengtao Yu,Tong Xiao,Jingbo Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes D2F-ReAG, a dynamic decomposition and filtering framework that adaptively adjusts reasoning depth for multi-hop RAG, improving accuracy and efficiency on multi-hop QA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs relying on static internal knowledge often generate inaccurate answers, and while RAG helps by using external knowledge, it performs poorly on multi-hop questions that require cross-document reasoning. Existing approaches like graph-structured RAG and fixed question decomposition either lack dynamic control of reasoning depth or effective evidence filtering, leading to inefficiency and reduced accuracy. There is a need for a multi-hop RAG framework that can adaptively decide when and how to decompose questions and filter information.

Method: The paper introduces D2F-ReAG, which first performs root-level reasoning and assesses its reliability. If the root reasoning is deemed reliable, the model directly produces an answer. If not, the method dynamically decomposes the original query into logically coherent sub-questions, retrieves and verifies evidence for each sub-question, and then uses the verified intermediate reasoning to refine and correct the root-level reasoning. This creates an adaptive multi-hop reasoning pipeline that controls reasoning depth on demand and incorporates filtering of untrustworthy or irrelevant information.

Result: On three multi-hop benchmarks, D2F-ReAG outperforms baseline RAG methods—especially on complex multi-hop questions—showing higher answer accuracy and better efficiency in reasoning compared to graph-structured RAG and fixed question decomposition approaches.

Conclusion: Adaptive control of reasoning depth and the combination of dynamic question decomposition with effective evidence filtering significantly improve multi-hop RAG performance. D2F-ReAG demonstrates that verifying and reusing sub-question reasoning to refine root reasoning is a promising direction for handling complex multi-hop queries in LLM-based systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often generate inaccurate answers due to their reliance on static internal knowledge. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) addresses this limitation by integrating external knowledge and excelling at single-hop queries. However, it struggles with multi-hop questions that require cross-document reasoning. Existing methods, such as graph structured RAG or question decomposition, often lack dynamic decomposition and effective filtering, which leads to lower efficiency and accuracy. To overcome these limitations, we propose Dynamic Decomposition and Filtering for Multi-Hop Reasoning-Augmented Generation (D2F-ReAG), a novel paradigm that adaptively controls reasoning depth by judging the reliability of the root-level reasoning. If the root reasoning is reliable, the model directly generates the answer. Otherwise, the question is logically decomposed into sub-questions, and the verified reasoning derived from these sub-questions is used to refine the root reasoning. Experiments on three multi-hop benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our method in handling complex multi-hop questions.

</details>


### [27] [The Evaluator Is Part of the Experiment: Measuring Open-Ended LLM Conformity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04463)
*Alicia Guerra,Yibo Hu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how large language models and human/AI evaluators conform to peer answers in open-ended tasks, showing that wrong peer answers degrade revisions, evaluators systematically shift judgments when seeing peers, and that common flip-rate metrics and poorly calibrated anchors are inadequate for measuring open-ended conformity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on LLM conformity mainly uses discrete, right/wrong multiple-choice-style settings where label correctness is known and flips are easy to count. However, many real LLM uses are open-ended, where answer quality is graded, latent, and evaluation is noisy or biased by visible peer answers. There is a need to understand how LLMs revise open-ended responses when exposed to peer content, how evaluators’ judgments are distorted by peer context, and whether standard measurement tools like flip rates and anchors remain valid in this more realistic setting.

Method: The authors design an experimental protocol that separates several effects in open-ended revision: (1) ordinary re-answering without peer input, (2) exposure to peer candidate content, (3) a residual effect from how peers are presented, and (4) directional sensitivity of human and model judges to visible peer context. They build a pooled main corpus under peer conditions and several decomposition corpora. They run experiments across four open-weight LLM generators and three benchmarks, manipulating peer answers so that in some conditions all peers are wrong. They then collect blind ratings (evaluators see only the answer) and informed ratings (evaluators see the answer plus peer endorsements), and also run audits with GPT-4o, GPT-5.4-mini, and anchor items to check calibration and stability of the latent quality scale.

Result: Across all combinations of generator and dataset, providing all-wrong peer answers leads to the worst-quality revised outputs, indicating harmful conformity. When identical answers are evaluated blindly versus with peer context, different judges show systematic, directional shifts: one tends to move toward the peer-endorsed position, two tend to move away from it, and one remains roughly neutral; GPT-4o and GPT-5.4-mini used as evaluators also exhibit non-neutral shifts. An anchor audit further reveals that brief but correct anchor answers are frequently misinterpreted, which can distort the inferred latent quality scale if calibration is not carefully checked.

Conclusion: The study concludes that: (1) simple flip-rate statistics are not sufficient to characterize conformity in open-ended tasks; (2) exposure to incorrect peer answers reliably harms the quality of LLM revisions; (3) human and model evaluators are themselves influenced by peer information and are not neutral arbiters; and (4) careful calibration and validation of anchors are necessary to maintain a stable latent quality scale when measuring open-ended answer quality and conformity effects.

Abstract: Prior work on LLM conformity largely measures discrete answer flips under verifiable labels. Open-ended revisions require a different measurement strategy because answer quality is graded, latent, and judged imperfectly. We introduce an experimental protocol implemented across a pooled main peer-condition corpus and separately constructed decomposition corpora, allowing us to separate ordinary re-answering, candidate-content exposure, a bundled peer-presentation residual, and directional judge sensitivity to visible peer context. Across four open-weight generators and three benchmarks, all-wrong peer input produces the lowest-quality revisions in every generator-dataset cell. Blind and informed ratings of identical answers also differ by evaluator: one judge shifts toward the peer-endorsed position, two shift away, one is approximately neutral, and GPT-4o and GPT-5.4-mini audits are likewise non-neutral. Finally, an anchor audit shows that terse correct anchors can be misread often enough to destabilize the latent scale unless calibration is checked explicitly. These results support four conclusions: flip rates are insufficient as a complete measure of open-ended conformity, wrong peers harm open-ended revision, evaluators are not neutral, and anchor calibration is necessary.

</details>


### [28] [Energy- and Memory-Efficient PEFT Methods for Personalized On-Device SLMs on Consumer GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04488)
*Kuanysh Akhmetzhanov,Jurn-Gyu Park*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods on small language models for on-device deployment, evaluating both accuracy and resource use. It finds LoRA+ best under energy constraints and QLoRA best under memory constraints, showing that compact SLMs with PEFT can enable practical personalized on-device models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying and personalizing large language models on devices with limited VRAM, time, and energy is difficult. While small language models with parameter-efficient fine-tuning are promising, there is a lack of systematic comparison across architectures and tasks that also accounts for energy and memory constraints. The authors aim to fill this gap to guide practical on-device personalization choices.

Method: The authors evaluate five fine-tuning strategies—Full Fine-Tuning, LoRA, LoRA+, QLoRA, and BitFit—on four small language models from two architectural families: Transformer-based (TinyLlama-1.1B, Qwen3-1.7B) and state space model-based (Mamba-1.4B, Mamba-2-1.3B). They run experiments on three GLUE benchmarks (SST-2, QNLI, STS-B) for general language understanding and three LaMP tasks (LaMP-1, LaMP-2, LaMP-3) for personalization. For each model–task–method configuration, they compute NetScore-E (energy-focused) and NetScore-M (memory-focused) metrics, then select the preferred method by an energy-first rule based primarily on NetScore-E and breaking ties with NetScore#.

Result: LoRA+ attains the highest energy-focused NetScore-E in 19 of 24 model–task configurations and the highest memory-focused NetScore-M in 13 of 24, becoming the selected method in 18 of 24 when using the energy-first selection rule. QLoRA, applicable only to Transformer models, reduces peak fine-tuning VRAM by up to 3.9× compared with LoRA and wins NetScore-M in 5 of 12 Transformer configurations, but its de-quantization overhead hurts energy efficiency so it is selected in only one configuration when energy is prioritized. BitFit and full fine-tuning perform poorly on both energy and memory metrics. Among models, TinyLlama-1.1B dominates on efficiency, leading NetScore-E on five of six benchmarks and NetScore-M on four of six.

Conclusion: Compact small language models combined with parameter-efficient fine-tuning can enable practical, personalized on-device deployment when both accuracy and resource constraints are considered. The best method depends on the bottleneck: LoRA+ is generally preferable when energy is the main constraint, while QLoRA is advantageous when memory/VRAM is the limiting factor. Traditional full fine-tuning and BitFit are rarely competitive under realistic on-device energy and memory budgets.

Abstract: Despite rapid advances in large language models (LLMs), deploying and personalizing them on resource-constrained devices remains impractical due to high VRAM, time, and energy costs. Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) of Small Language Models (SLMs) offers a promising alternative, yet few studies compare PEFT methods across architectures using both general and personalization benchmarks while accounting for energy consumption. We compare five fine-tuning approaches (Full Fine-Tuning, LoRA, LoRA+, QLoRA, and BitFit) on four SLMs from two families (Transformer-based: TinyLlama-1.1B, Qwen3-1.7B; SSM-based: Mamba-1.4B, Mamba-2-1.3B) across three GLUE tasks (SST-2, QNLI, STS-B) and three LaMP personalization tasks (LaMP-1, LaMP-2, LaMP-3). Each configuration is evaluated with the energy-focused NetScore-E and the memory-focused NetScore-M, the two variants that reflect the constraints binding on-device deployment. Methods are selected with a strict energy-first rule (highest NetScore-E, ties broken by NetScore#). LoRA+ achieves the highest NetScore-E in 19 of 24 configurations and the highest NetScore-M in 13 of 24, and is the selected method in 18 of 24. QLoRA, available only for the Transformer models, cuts peak finetuning VRAM by up to 3.9x relative to LoRA and therefore takes the best NetScore-M in 5 of the 12 Transformer configurations, although its de-quantization overhead leaves it selected in only one of them once energy decides. BitFit and full fine-tuning are almost never competitive on either variant, and TinyLlama-1.1B leads the energy-focused NetScore-E on five of the six benchmarks and the memory-focused NetScore-M on four. These results show that compact SLMs paired with PEFT provide a practical, energy-aware path to personalized on-device deployment, with the optimal method set by the dominant constraint: LoRA+ for energy and QLoRA for memory.

</details>


### [29] [K-EXAONE 2.0 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04505)
*Eunbi Choi,Kibong Choi,Sehyun Chun,Seokhee Hong,Junwon Hwang,Hyojin Jeon,Ahra Jo,Hyunjik Jo,Yeonsik Jo,Minhyeok Jung,Doyoung Kim,Heegyu Kim,Joonkee Kim,Seonghwan Kim,Soyeon Kim,Sunkyoung Kim,Yireun Kim,Yongil Kim,Byungoh Ko,Changhun Lee,Dohaeng Lee,Haeju Lee,Jinsik Lee,Kyungmin Lee,Minwoo Lee,Wonkee Lee,Sangha Park,Sungjune Park,Kwangrok Ryoo,Kijung Seo,Minju Seo,Yongwoo Song,Sejong Yang,Heuiyeen Yeen,Stanley Jungkyu Choi,Yemuk Choi,Yongchan Chun,Jiwon Ham,Dasol Hong,Sujeong Im,Kijeong Jeon,Gerrard Jeongwon Jo,Hyeongjun Jo,Yujin Jo,Jiyeon Jung,Naeun Kang,Daeseong Kim,Euisoon Kim,Hayeon Kim,Hyosang Kim,Myoungshin Kim,Unsol Kim,Youchul Kim,Chaeeun Lee,ChaeYoon Lee,Edward Hwayoung Lee,Honglak Lee,Hwansoo Lee,Minkyung Lee,Sangeun Lee,Solji Lim,Woohyung Lim,Chanwoo Moon,Jueun Mun,Jimin Park,Seojeong Park,Yongmin Park,Hyerin Seo,Donghyeon Shin,Donghyun Son,Eunyong Son,Kaehyun Um,Sihoon Yang,Chang En Yea,Sihyuk Yi,Kyungjae Yoo,Chansik Yoon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: K-EXAONE 2.0 is an open-weight, multilingual Mixture-of-Experts LLM (750B total / 37B active parameters) that extends K-EXAONE with longer context (256K), more languages, and improved reasoning, coding, long-context, and safety, targeting practical real-world use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move toward globally competitive, frontier-scale foundation models while leveraging an existing model (K-EXAONE) instead of training entirely from scratch, and to provide a strong, open-weight multilingual model particularly well-adapted to Korean sociocultural contexts and practical deployment scenarios.

Method: They "upcycle" the original K-EXAONE by expanding its architecture into a Mixture-of-Experts model (750B parameters with ~37B active per token), extend context length to 256K tokens, add more languages (from six to ten), and use a multi-stage training pipeline: continual pre-training, difficulty-focused mid-training, and post-training aimed at boosting reasoning, agentic coding, multilingual capabilities, and safety. They then evaluate the model across nine practically relevant categories and compare against K-EXAONE and other open-weight models.

Result: K-EXAONE 2.0 outperforms its predecessor K-EXAONE across nine evaluation categories, especially in agentic coding and long-context understanding, and demonstrates strong performance in long-context retrieval and safety while remaining competitive with other open-weight models.

Conclusion: K-EXAONE 2.0 represents a substantial, practical upgrade over K-EXAONE, showing that an upcycling strategy plus targeted multi-stage training can produce a large, competitive multilingual MoE model with strong long-context and safety characteristics. Released under Apache 2.0, it is positioned as a foundation for further research, adaptation, and deployment, and as an intermediate step toward future global frontier-scale models.

Abstract: This technical report presents K-EXAONE 2.0, an open-weight multilingual foundation model developed by LG AI Research as a step in our effort toward global frontier-scale foundation models. Rather than training from scratch, we upcycle K-EXAONE and expand its architecture, yielding a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with 750B total parameters and approximately 37B activated per token---more than three times the capacity of its predecessor. K-EXAONE 2.0 supports context lengths of up to 256K tokens and expands multilingual coverage from six to ten languages. Its training pipeline combines continual pre-training, difficulty-focused mid-training, and post-training to strengthen reasoning, agentic coding, multilingual capability, and safety grounded in Korean sociocultural contexts. Across nine evaluation categories selected to reflect the conditions of practical use, K-EXAONE 2.0 improves over K-EXAONE and remains competitive with open-weight models, showing its largest gains in agentic coding and long-context understanding and its clearest strengths in long-context retrieval and safety. Released under the Apache 2.0 license, K-EXAONE 2.0 enables the wider AI ecosystem to evaluate, deploy, adapt, and build upon it, while marking the beginning---rather than the endpoint---of our challenge toward the global frontier.

</details>


### [30] [RESPClinBench: Benchmarking Multimodal Clinical Decision-Making and Longitudinal Disease Management in Respiratory Specialty Care](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04514)
*Mouxiao Bian,Zhi Chen,Ruiyao Chen,Lu Lu,Hengrui Liang,Chaoyi Huang,Yiluo Lin,Jingru Ding,Yun Zhong,Yuming Su,Jie Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces RESPClinBench, a real-world benchmark to evaluate large language models on complex respiratory clinical decision-making tasks, particularly COPD management and pulmonary nodule assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medical AI benchmarks are mostly exam-style and fail to capture the multimodal, longitudinal, and safety-critical nature of real respiratory specialty care. There is a need for a benchmark that reflects realistic clinical workflows and decisions in respiratory medicine.

Method: The authors built RESPClinBench from de-identified real respiratory cases. Three attending-level respiratory physicians curated and refined cases, reference answers, and atomic clinical-action points, with final adjudication by a senior specialist. RESPClinBench has two sub-benchmarks: AECOPD-PIM with 427 open-ended COPD cases, and PNBIM with 196 multimodal pulmonary nodule cases (CT images + structured data). Seven large language models were evaluated via standardized API calls (temperature=0, max tokens=8192), yielding 4,361 responses. A scoring framework combined atomic-action recall with rubric-based LLM-as-a-judge grading and tracked independent safety risks (e.g., hallucinations, medication risk).

Result: Across all 623 cases, the average model score was 68.58/100. Qwen3.6-27B achieved the best overall performance (71.22) and led the COPD benchmark AECOPD-PIM (71.11), while Qwen3.5-397B-A17B led the pulmonary nodule benchmark PNBIM (72.48). However, substantial safety concerns remained: imaging hallucinations and serious medical risks occurred in 31.85% and 8.16% of PNBIM responses, respectively, and medication-safety issues and serious medical risks appeared in 26.93% and 1.44% of AECOPD-PIM responses.

Conclusion: RESPClinBench exposes important, task-specific weaknesses of current LLMs in multimodal pulmonary nodule evaluation and longitudinal COPD management. By integrating detailed clinical-action coverage, holistic qualitative scoring, and separate safety indicators, the benchmark offers a clinically meaningful framework for comparing models and guiding their selection and real-world validation in respiratory care.

Abstract: Background: Respiratory specialty care requires multimodal interpretation, longitudinal risk assessment, guideline-concordant intervention, and whole-course management, which are poorly represented by examination-oriented medical benchmarks. Objective: To develop RESPClinBench, a real-world scenario-based benchmark for respiratory clinical decision-making, and evaluate seven contemporary large language models across AECOPD-PIM and PNBIM. Methods: RESPClinBench cases were adapted from de-identified respiratory clinical data. Three attending-level respiratory physicians revised cases, reference answers, and atomic clinical-action points, while one senior respiratory specialist performed cross-review and final adjudication. AECOPD-PIM comprised 427 open-ended COPD cases, and PNBIM comprised 196 multimodal pulmonary nodule cases combining chest CT with structured clinical information. Seven models generated 4,361 responses through standardized API inference with temperature 0 and a maximum output length of 8192 tokens. An automated framework calculated the final score as the arithmetic mean of atomic-action recall and rubric-based LLM-as-a-Judge assessment. Results: Across 623 cases, the mean final score was 68.58. Qwen3.6-27B ranked first overall at 71.22, Qwen3.5-397B-A17B led PNBIM at 72.48, and Qwen3.6-27B led AECOPD-PIM at 71.11. Imaging hallucination and serious medical risk occurred in 31.85% and 8.16% of PNBIM responses; medication-safety risk and serious medical risk occurred in 26.93% and 1.44% of AECOPD-PIM responses. Conclusions: RESPClinBench identifies task-specific limitations in multimodal pulmonary nodule assessment and longitudinal COPD management. Combining explicit clinical-action coverage, holistic evaluation, and independent safety flags provides a clinically grounded basis for model selection and prospective validation.

</details>


### [31] [ODRA: Synthesizing Cognitive Behavioral Therapy Sessions with Structured Chain-Of-Thought and Dynamic Patient Resistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04524)
*Javier Rodriguez-Juan,Hiba Arnaout,Jose Garcia-Rodriguez,David Tomás,Iryna Gurevych*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ODRA is a new framework to synthetically generate realistic CBT therapy dialogues that better follow CBT structure and model patient resistance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Synthetic CBT dialogue generation is hard because you must follow strict CBT structure while realistically capturing resistant, unpredictable patient behavior; existing methods either fail at structure, realism, or both and tend to produce overly compliant, unrealistic patients.

Method: The authors propose ODRA, which uses a Chain-of-Thought strategy grounded in CBT guidelines plus a dedicated resistance orchestrator that steers the simulated patient’s behavior according to a specified resistance level, aiming to balance CBT protocol adherence with realistic non‑sycophantic patient responses.

Result: Automated metrics and expert (licensed psychologist) evaluations show ODRA-generated sessions outperform prior script-based and multi-agent approaches on CBT skill quality, CBT guideline alignment, and realism of patient behavior, with experts preferring ODRA in 12 of 13 clinical metrics; models fine-tuned on ODRA data also perform better when interacting with both cooperative and resistant patients.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling and orchestrating patient resistance in synthetic CBT dialogues yields more structurally accurate, clinically realistic therapy data and improves downstream robustness of therapeutic models in varied patient interaction scenarios.

Abstract: Synthetic generation of Cognitive Behavioral Therapy (CBT) sessions is challenged by two competing demands: adhering to strict therapeutic structure while modeling the resistant, unpredictable behavior of real patients. Existing script-based methods fail to capture dynamic therapeutic interactions, while multi-agent approaches struggle to adhere to CBT's sequential structure; both suffer from sycophancy, producing overly compliant patients that misrepresent real clinical settings. In this work we introduce ODRA, a novel framework for synthesizing therapy dialogues through a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) strategy grounded in foundational CBT guidelines (Beck, 2020). ODRA further incorporates a resistance orchestrator to solve patient sycophancy, which employs steering techniques to elicit behaviors aligned with their resistance level. Automated and expert evaluations show that ODRA significantly outperforms existing methods across therapeutic skills, CBT alignment, and patient behavioral fidelity, with licensed psychologists preferring ODRA sessions across 12 of 13 clinical metrics. Furthermore, models fine-tuned on our dataset demonstrate superior therapeutic performance against both cooperative and resistant patients, validating that explicit resistance modeling in synthetic training data directly translates to downstream clinical robustness.

</details>


### [32] [EuroExec: Frontier Language Models Fall Short of Expert Judgment on European Executive Decision Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04549)
*Pau Arnal,Khaled Denfir,Danylo Smahliuk,Amrut Avhad,Marcus A. Castro*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces EuroExec, a human expert-based benchmark of 413 real-world European executive tasks, and shows that even top frontier LLMs solve only about half of them, performing far below human experts and exposing limits of automatic evaluation on open-ended, subjective problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier LLMs are increasingly deployed for complex, open-ended professional tasks, but most existing benchmarks focus on narrower, more well-defined questions and rely heavily on automatic metrics. There is a gap in understanding how these models perform on realistic executive-level work, where tasks are open-ended, subjective, and require nuanced judgment, and where current evaluations may overestimate their readiness as near-human replacements.

Method: The authors construct EuroExec, a benchmark of 413 long-form open-ended tasks derived from real European executive work and written by 47 vetted domain experts. They dedicate over 4,000 human expert hours to evaluate six frontier LLMs. Each model response is scored using (1) a multi-attribute rubric, (2) an item-specific checklist of requirements, and (3) pairwise preference rankings versus alternatives (including expert-written references). They synthesize these scores into an aggregate metric called the “Solve Rate.” They also perform rigorous statistical analyses to check evaluator consistency and compare human- versus automatic evaluation approaches.

Result: On EuroExec, the best-performing frontier LLM achieves a Solve Rate of only 56.9%, meaning it fully satisfies the task requirements on just over half of the problems. In contrast, human expert reference answers are judged to solve tasks at near-ceiling levels and are preferred to any model output in 74% of blind pairwise comparisons. Automatic evaluation metrics do not reliably capture quality on these open-ended tasks and misalign with human expert judgments, indicating that standard automated benchmarks overstate model capability in this domain.

Conclusion: The study concludes that current frontier LLMs fall significantly short of professional human standards on realistic open-ended executive tasks, despite being deployed in such settings. Reliable assessment of their capabilities in this domain requires intensive human expert evaluation with robust rubrics and statistical validation; existing automatic metrics are insufficient and can be misleading for subjective, real-world problems. The EuroExec benchmark highlights substantial remaining performance gaps and provides a more realistic yardstick for judging readiness of generative systems for high-stakes executive work.

Abstract: Frontier LLMs are increasingly put to use on open-ended complex questions, different in nature from the ones they are typically evaluated on. We dedicate more than 4,000 human expert hours to evaluate a selection of six frontier LLMs on a member of this class of problems: EuroExec, our introduced human expert-based benchmark composed of 413 open-ended long-form European executive tasks authored by 47 vetted domain experts, each question drawn from experience in a real case. Every response is manually evaluated through a multi-attribute rubric, an item-specific checklist of requirements, and a preference rank ordering, extracting an aggregate metric "Solve Rate". The strongest model solves only 56.9% of tasks, while expert-written reference answers judged blindly are solved at near-ceiling levels and are preferred over every model response in 74% of direct rankings, placing frontier generative systems well below the professional standard of work they are already used for. We see that the best way to extract this kind of conclusion is by employing human evaluators, carefully checking their consistency through rigorous statistical analysis, and observe that automatic measurements also fall short when evaluating on this case of real-world open-ended problems with a subjective ground truth.

</details>


### [33] [Relational Response Fields: A General Theory of Black-Box LLM Response Consistency and Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04552)
*Song Zichen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a formal framework for understanding when and how we can reliably repair or recover correct answers from a set of possibly corrupted black-box language-model outputs, and defines a key quantity, γ_k(D,A), that characterizes the intrinsic difficulty of this recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reliability techniques for black-box language models—sampling, prompting, voting, verification, and iterative refinement—focus on improving or aggregating individual outputs, but they lack a principled understanding of when true answers are even recoverable from a collection of noisy responses. The authors want to understand the fundamental, information-theoretic conditions under which reliable recovery is possible, distinguish consistency from truth, and characterize hallucination directions that cannot be fixed by structure alone.

Method: They formalize the set of model responses to systematically transformed versions of a query as a Relational Response Field (RRF), where nodes are responses and edges encode required transformations under task symmetries (e.g., paraphrase, scaling, decomposition, refactoring). They introduce relation operators D that specify these edge constraints and anchor operators A that incorporate trusted external signals (like execution results or verifiers). Under an assumption of at most k corrupted responses, they define γ_k(D,A), an intrinsic identifiability parameter. Using theoretical analysis, they show when γ_k>0 every k-node corruption is identifiable, derive stability bounds proportional to 1/γ_k, and prove a matching minimax lower bound showing the dependence on γ_k is optimal. They then design sparse field-repair algorithms that exploit the RRF structure, and run controlled theorem tests plus black-box experiments on mathematical and coding tasks to empirically validate several predicted phenomena.

Result: Theoretically, they prove that γ_k(D,A) is positive if and only if all patterns of up to k corrupted responses are identifiable from the relational and anchor structure, and they establish tight stability bounds: any estimator’s performance must scale at least inversely with γ_k. They demonstrate that relation-only approaches cannot distinguish certain directions (null directions) corresponding to shared hallucinations, formalizing a separation between consistency and truth. Algorithmically, they provide sparse repair methods that work under stronger conditions related to the null space of the relational operators. Empirically, via theorem-based tests and experiments on math and code problems, they observe and quantify: (1) specific cases where consistent fields still deviate from the truth (consistency–truth separation), (2) sharp phase transitions in recoverability as anchor information passes critical thresholds, (3) diminishing returns from adding redundant relational information (redundancy saturation), and (4) that γ_k-based predictions transfer across models and tasks, enabling cross-model, cross-task predictions of repair difficulty.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that γ_k(D,A) captures an intrinsic, measurable difficulty of recovering true answers from collections of black-box language-model outputs under structured relations and anchors. This parameter is not just a heuristic score but governs fundamental identifiability and stability limits for any estimator. The work shows that using only relational consistency is insufficient to guarantee truth due to null directions like shared hallucinations, and that anchors are essential to break these ambiguities. Their theory-backed algorithms and experiments suggest that future reliability work should explicitly model and estimate γ_k(D,A) for tasks, and design repair methods and evaluation protocols around the information-theoretic structure of the response field rather than around ad hoc aggregation heuristics.

Abstract: Black-box language-model reliability is commonly pursued by sampling, prompting, voting, verifying, or iteratively revising individual answers. We ask a prior question: \emph{what determines whether a collection of black-box responses is recoverable at all?} We represent responses to typed transformations of a query as a \emph{relational response field} (RRF). Edge transports encode how valid responses must change under paraphrase, scaling, decomposition, refactoring, or other task symmetries; anchors encode independently trusted evidence such as execution or a verifier. For relation operator $D$, anchor operator $A$, and at most $k$ corrupted response nodes, we identify $γ_k(D,A)$ as the intrinsic difficulty of black-box response recovery. It is positive exactly when every $k$-node corruption is identifiable; it gives a deterministic stability bound proportional to $1/γ_k$; and a matching two-point minimax lower bound shows that no estimator can improve this dependence. Thus consistency is not truth: relation-only methods are blind to null directions, including shared hallucinations. We derive sparse field-repair algorithms while separating information-theoretic identifiability from the stronger null-space conditions required by convex optimization. Controlled theorem tests and black-box mathematics/code experiments evaluate four theory-fixed consequences: consistency--truth separation, anchor phase transitions, redundancy saturation, and cross-model, cross-task prediction of repair difficulty. The results support $γ_k(D,A)$ as a measurable property of a response-recovery instance, rather than a score attached to one repair heuristic.

</details>


### [34] [Representing Visual Evidence for Item Difficulty Prediction: Visual Textualization and Image-Native Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04554)
*Han Chen,Ming Li,Hong Jiao,Tianyi Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how to best represent visual information in math questions to predict item difficulty, comparing plain text, textualized images, and image-native models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Newly created test questions lack enough student response data to calibrate their difficulty, so predicting difficulty directly from item content is valuable. Many math items contain diagrams or visual components, and current systems usually convert these visuals into text before prediction. It is unclear whether this textualization is optimal, or whether models that consume images directly might perform better or differently.

Method: Using a dataset of Eedi math questions with empirically calibrated difficulty values, the authors train both large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) to perform difficulty regression. They compare three input interfaces: (1) question text only, (2) visual textualization that converts diagrams into textual descriptions, and (3) image-native input where the original images are used. They evaluate performance using RMSE, run test-time interventions to probe reliance on visual information, and analyze item-level error patterns and computational workflows between textualization and image-native modeling.

Result: Both visual interfaces—textualization and image-native modeling—produce lower difficulty-prediction errors than using question text alone. Among LLMs, using textualized visual information from Open-VLM consistently lowers RMSE. Among image-native VLMs, broader adaptation of the vision-language model leads to better RMSE. Overall, the visual-enhanced approaches are competitive with each other, and the leading systems cannot be reliably ordered by performance. Test-time analyses indicate models depend on the full-item image but do not clearly isolate the incremental contribution of visual components. The two visual approaches make different and partially complementary errors and differ in their computational characteristics.

Conclusion: Visual information is important for predicting difficulty of math items, and both textualized and image-native visual interfaces are effective. Textualization should not be assumed to be the only or default practical interface. Image-native modeling is a viable and competitive alternative, with its success dependent on how the VLM is adapted. The complementary error patterns suggest potential gains from combining both visual interfaces, and design choices should also consider computational trade-offs, not just predictive accuracy.

Abstract: Predicting item difficulty from content can provide an initial estimate for newly developed questions before sufficient student responses are available. Existing approaches typically represent the question stem and answer choices as text. When mathematics items contain visual components, a common pipeline first textualizes that evidence and then applies a text predictor. We ask: how should visual evidence be represented for item difficulty prediction? We compare question text alone, visual textualization, which expresses visual evidence in language, and image-native modeling, which retains the original image. Using Eedi items with difficulty calibrated from student responses, we train large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) directly for difficulty regression. Both visual interfaces achieve the lowest point estimates, although the leading systems cannot be reliably ordered. Open-VLM textualization yields lower RMSE point estimates for all evaluated LLMs, while broader adaptation does so for all image-native VLMs. Test-time interventions show dependence on the paired full-item image, but do not isolate the additional visual component. The two visual interfaces also make partially complementary item-level errors and differ substantially in computational workflow. Thus, textualization should not be treated as the only practical interface: image-native modeling is a competitive alternative whose effectiveness depends on how the VLM is adapted.

</details>


### [35] [STRIVE: Probing Reasoning Limits in Graded Plausibility Generation and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04567)
*Bhiman Kumar Baghel,Anna Chrabaszcz,Tessa Warren,Michael Walsh Dickey,Haley C. Dresang,Xiang Lorraine Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces STRIVE, an LLM-based framework to automatically generate and evaluate tightly controlled sets of events that vary only in one role (e.g., agent or patient) across plausibility and difficulty conditions, reducing manual labor in psycholinguistic experiments on event knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Psycholinguistic studies on event knowledge often rely on plausibility judgments over highly controlled event sets (e.g., who does what to whom) where only one aspect of an event changes while others are held constant. Constructing these sets by hand is very time-consuming and does not scale well, especially when studies require large numbers of items and specific distributions of plausibility and classification difficulty (easy vs. hard). There is a need for a scalable, semi-automated method that can reliably generate such materials while preserving the fine-grained experimental control required in cognitive science.

Method: The authors propose STRIVE, a framework built on large language models. Given a verb, STRIVE first induces a shared event frame that specifies roles such as agent, patient, and other arguments. It then generates four events per verb that cross two dimensions: plausibility (plausible vs. implausible) and classification difficulty (easy vs. hard). Each event manipulates only one slot in the frame (e.g., changing just the agent), holding all other slots fixed to maintain experimental control. The framework integrates a global reasoning scratchpad to improve coherence and uses an LLM-based evaluator to judge plausibility and difficulty. An iterative, evaluator-guided refinement loop further improves item quality. The authors test multiple LLMs, including GPT-5.1, on 60 verbs and measure quality of the generated sets and agreement between the LLM evaluator and human plausibility judgments.

Result: Using a simple baseline prompt, GPT-5.1 produced acceptable, fully controlled event sets only 16.7% of the time. Incorporating global reasoning and evaluator-guided refinement increased this success rate to 75.0%, indicating major gains from structured prompting and iterative evaluation. Increasing the model's reasoning effort also improved alignment between the LLM evaluator's plausibility judgments and human ratings. However, the framework still struggles most with events that lie near the plausibility boundary: these items generate substantial human disagreement and pose difficulty for the evaluator, whose best accuracy on the implausible-hard condition is just 57%.

Conclusion: STRIVE significantly reduces the manual burden of constructing controlled event sets for psycholinguistic research by automating initial generation and evaluation while maintaining strict experimental constraints. The framework's reasoning-augmented prompting and evaluator-guided refinement markedly improve the quality and reliability of generated items. Nonetheless, borderline-plausible cases remain challenging and require human oversight, so STRIVE is best viewed as a semi-automated assistant rather than a full replacement for expert item construction. This approach can scale up experimental materials and may generalize to other domains where fine-grained control over linguistic stimuli is crucial.

Abstract: Event knowledge concerns who does what to whom. Psycholinguists use event-plausibility judgments to examine how this knowledge supports human language processing. To isolate plausibility effects, these studies require controlled event sets in which one event slot varies across plausibility levels while all other event features remain fixed. Constructing such sets manually is labor-intensive. We therefore introduce STRIVE, an LLM-based framework for jointly generating and evaluating controlled event sets crossing plausibility class (plausible vs. implausible) with intended classification difficulty (easy vs. hard). Given a verb, STRIVE constructs a shared event frame, then produces one event per condition by varying one slot while holding all others fixed. In experiments with six models across 60 verbs, GPT-5.1 produced high-quality sets only 16.7% of the time using the baseline generation prompt. Adding a global reasoning scratchpad and evaluator-guided refinement raised this rate to 75.0%. Greater reasoning effort also improved evaluator--human agreement. Nevertheless, events near the plausibility boundary remain most difficult. They elicit the greatest human disagreement, and the best evaluator reaches only 57% accuracy on the implausible-hard condition, indicating a need for human input. Overall, STRIVE offers a scalable approach to reducing manual effort by automating initial event-set generation and evaluation for psycholinguistic studies.

</details>


### [36] [Relevant but Incomplete: Referential Dangling as a Paradigm-Level Failure Mode in Hard Prompt Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04569)
*Zhengpei Hu,Kai Li,Dapeng Fu,Xuechao Zou,Yuanhao Tang,Yue Li,Tengfei Cao,Jianqiang Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that hard prompt compression methods that independently select tokens/sentences/chunks frequently break apart pieces of evidence needed to interpret answers, creating “referential dangling” and causing large accuracy drops in long-context QA; it proposes a lightweight restoration step that detects and reinserts omitted but referentially necessary sentences, improving QA accuracy with almost no loss in compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context language model inference is expensive, so many methods use hard prompt compression: scoring and selecting only the most relevant text under a token budget. However, these methods typically rank units independently by relevance and ignore cross-sentence dependencies. The authors observe that this can delete crucial definitional or referential context (e.g., an entity definition) while keeping only an answer fragment that refers back to it, leaving the model unable to interpret the answer. Existing benchmarks and compressors do not systematically quantify or fix this failure mode, so the paper aims to (1) define and measure it and (2) design a practical mitigation.

Method: 1) Conceptualize and formally define “referential dangling,” where retained context contains an answer span but omitted context provides the referential information needed to interpret it. 2) Empirically audit several hard compression systems (including Beaver using Qwen3-0.6B embeddings) across multi-hop QA datasets such as HotpotQA, MuSiQue, and LongBench-v2 Single-Document QA. Measure how often compression at a fixed ratio (e.g., 0.30) drops one half of a multi-hop “bridge” evidence pair. 3) For examples identified as dangling, run controlled evaluations with a downstream LLM (e.g., Qwen3-8B, GPT-5.5): compare accuracy when (a) using the compressed context, (b) restoring the missing supporting paragraph while keeping total tokens constant by removing nonsupporting text, and (c) using both supporting paragraphs. 4) Train a small classifier that, given omitted sentences and the retained context, predicts which omitted sentences are necessary for interpreting the retained text. At inference, reinsert top-ranked omitted sentences under the budget, without needing explicit support labels, and evaluate the impact on QA accuracy and compression ratio.

Result: 1) Independent scoring in hard compression frequently causes referential dangling. For Beaver at 0.30 compression, 34–54% of bridge questions across three multi-hop QA datasets lose part of the answer path. On a shared HotpotQA bridge subset, all six tested hard compressors show dangling rates up to 60%. In LongBench-v2 Single-Document QA, every document contains at least one dangling reference. 2) Restoring the missing supporting paragraph while removing nonsupporting paragraphs—keeping the token budget fixed—boosts Qwen3-8B accuracy on dangling examples by 29–34 percentage points (p < 0.0001), recovering at least 88% of the performance gap relative to having both supporting paragraphs. 3) Even strong answer models like GPT-5.5 on MuSiQue still suffer: they are 8.8 points less accurate on compressed contexts than on fully supported contexts, indicating that model scale does not fully compensate for referential loss. 4) The proposed compact classifier for automatic restoration, applied to HotpotQA with Qwen3-8B, increases accuracy by 4.7 points while only slightly relaxing the compression ratio from 0.30 to 0.31.

Conclusion: Independent, relevance-only hard prompt compression systematically breaks referential structures in long-context QA, often dropping critical bridging evidence and causing large accuracy losses that are not remedied by stronger answer models. The notion of “referential dangling” captures this structural failure and is empirically widespread across datasets and compressors. Restoring omitted but referentially necessary context—either by explicitly reinserting missing supporting paragraphs or via a lightweight classifier that predicts which omitted sentences are needed to interpret retained text—substantially improves performance while preserving a tight compression budget. Effective hard compressors must therefore jointly optimize for both relevance and referential completeness, rather than treating units independently.

Abstract: Hard prompt compression reduces long-context inference cost by independently scoring tokens, sentences, or chunks and retaining the highest-scoring units under a budget. We identify a structural failure in this procedure: independent selection can split dependent evidence pairs, retaining one member while deleting the other. When retained text contains an answer but deleted text defines the entity needed to interpret it, we call the result referential dangling. At a compression ratio of 0.30, Beaver, which ranks coherent chunks using Qwen3-0.6B embeddings, leaves the answer path incomplete in 34-54% of bridge examples across three multi-hop question answering datasets. On a shared HotpotQA bridge set, all six hard compressors we test exhibit dangling at rates up to 60%, and every document in LongBench-v2 Single-Document QA contains at least one dangling reference. On dangling examples evaluated with Qwen3-8B, reinserting the missing supporting paragraph while removing nonsupporting paragraphs to maintain the token budget improves accuracy by 29-34 percentage points (p < 0.0001), recovering at least 88% of the gap to contexts retaining both supporting paragraphs. Stronger answer models do not absorb the loss: on MuSiQue, GPT-5.5 is 8.8 points less accurate on compressed contexts than on contexts retaining both supporting paragraphs. Finally, we train a compact classifier to rank omitted sentences by whether they are needed to interpret retained text and reinsert the top-ranked candidates without support annotations at inference. On HotpotQA with Qwen3-8B, this automatic restoration improves accuracy by 4.7 points while changing the compression ratio only from 0.30 to 0.31. Hard compressors should optimize both relevance and referential completeness.

</details>


### [37] [The Personalization Mirage: How LLMs Fabricate User Profiles, and Why Self-Monitoring Misleads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04570)
*Yushi Sun,Yanjie Zhang,Rui Sheng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MirageBench to evaluate and reveal pervasive over-inference in personalized LLMs, showing that models often fabricate user attributes and that external verification is more reliable than models’ self-assessment for trustworthy personalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personalized LLMs are widely used with persistent user memory, but no one has rigorously examined how faithfully they infer and store user attributes. The authors are motivated by the risk that models over-infer personal traits from sparse evidence, potentially harming users and undermining trust in personalization.

Method: The authors define and study over-inference (OI), where LLMs infer user attributes unsupported by evidence. They build MirageBench, a benchmark with 150 synthetic personas that are balanced across stereotypical, counter-stereotypical, and neutral profiles, and design 6 personalization tasks along an “imagination gradient.” They introduce a four-way faithfulness taxonomy judged by an independent model, which they validate against blind human annotation on 400 claims using Cohen’s kappa. They then evaluate 12 LLMs from 7 families across 143,616 judged claims, comparing models’ self-assessed OI to externally judged OI, and running a small multi-turn pilot to observe how inferred attributes accumulate over a conversation.

Result: All 12 evaluated models exhibit substantial over-inference, fabricating unsupported user attributes in roughly 35%–49% of their claims, with an average around 42%. No model avoids OI. They find a Self-Monitoring Inversion at the model-selection level: models that self-report the least OI actually show the highest OI when judged externally, with a negative rank correlation (rho ≈ -0.60). However, within a single model, its self-audit scores still moderately distinguish more and less faithful claims (AUROC 0.58–0.83). OI varies notably by task (27%–59%), and in multi-turn interactions, inferred attributes accumulate roughly linearly over turns with little correction.

Conclusion: Over-inference is widespread in personalized LLMs with memory, and current self-monitoring mechanisms are unreliable for comparing models’ faithfulness at the system-selection level. Because models that seem most confident about avoiding OI can in fact be the worst offenders, relying on self-reported confidence is misleading. Instead, benchmarks and external verification like MirageBench are needed as a stronger basis for trustworthy and safe personalization, especially in settings where incorrect inferences about user attributes could have significant consequences.

Abstract: Personalized LLMs with persistent memory are increasingly deployed, yet the faithfulness of their user models remains unexamined. We study over-inference (OI): the phenomenon where LLMs fabricate user attributes beyond what evidence supports. We introduce MirageBench, comprising 150 personas balanced across stereotypical, counter-stereotypical, and neutral profiles, 6 personalization tasks spanning an ``imagination gradient'', a four-way faithfulness taxonomy operationalized by an independent judge (validated against a blind human annotator on 400 claims: Cohen's kappa = 0.863 four-class, kappa = 0.900 binary), and a leaderboard of 12 models across 7 families on 143616 judged claims. We find that over-inference is pervasive: every one of the 12 models over-infers 35%--49% of its claims (cross-model mean 41.6%; claim-weighted 41.8%), with no model in this evaluation escaping it. Most strikingly, we surface a Self-Monitoring Inversion: at the model-selection level, models' self-assessed OI is negatively rank-correlated with their judge-measured OI (rho = -0.60, p = 0.044; exploratory, wide bootstrap CI [-0.90, +0.06], n = 12). The models that report the least over-inference tend to be flagged as fabricating the most, so self-reported confidence is a misleading signal for comparing models, even though within a single model self-audit still ranks that model's own claims moderately well (AUROC 0.58--0.83). We further show that OI is task-dependent (27%--59%) and that, in a multi-turn pilot, inferred attributes accumulate approximately linearly with little revision.
