MirageBench positions external verification, rather than model self-report, as a more reliable foundation for trustworthy personalization.

</details>


### [38] [When Memory Lies: An Empirical Study of Spatial Memory Staleness in VLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04574)
*Yushi Sun,Yanjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper studies safety risks from stale spatial memory in vision-language model (VLM) agents using a dynamic FrozenLake environment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Memory-augmented agents rely on stored spatial knowledge, which becomes incorrect as environments change. The paper investigates what happens when an agent’s confident memory conflicts with new observations, and whether current VLMs can detect and handle this staleness before it leads to safety-relevant errors.

Method: Introduce a dynamic FrozenLake testbed where the environment layout can change, creating stale memories. Design a staleness-detection task plus a downstream navigation task. Evaluate three closed-source and three open-weight VLMs, under both text-only and image-input conditions: 1,800 runs for staleness detection and 12,000 navigation episodes in text mode across four LLM-based navigators, using shared random seeds for comparability.

Result: (1) Text-based detection of stale memory does not guarantee proper visual grounding: some models that do well with text perform poorly with images, with vision F1 scores ranging from 0.887 down to 0.067, and the weakest model still acting confidently while ignoring visual evidence. (2) Using stale memory without auditing is dangerous: in a main GPT-4o configuration, an agent that trusts unaudited memory fails over twice as often as an agent with no memory. (3) Adding a read-time audit/filter reduces safety failures in text mode, but even perfect (oracle) stale labels do not further improve performance at the current grid size, and unreliable visual auditing can negate benefits from filtering.

Conclusion: Spatial-memory staleness is identified as a concrete safety failure mode in memory-augmented agents. The key open problems are ensuring reliable visual grounding and robust action selection when memory conflicts with current observations, especially in visually grounded, dynamic environments.

Abstract: Memory-augmented VLM agents act on persistent spatial knowledge, yet that knowledge silently goes stale as the environment changes. We ask what happens when an agent must reconcile a confident memory claim with a contradicting observation, and whether current models can catch the conflict before it becomes a safety-relevant mistake. Using a dynamic FrozenLake testbed, we pair a staleness-detection task with a downstream navigation task across three closed-source models and three open-weight VLMs under both text and image inputs (1,800 detection runs, and 12,000 text-mode navigation episodes over four LLM navigators at a shared 50-seed scale). Three findings emerge. First, text solvability does not imply visual grounding: models that flag stale entries reliably from text nonetheless span vision F1 from 0.887 down to 0.067 on the identical grids, and the weakest keeps making fluent, confident decisions that ignore the image. Second, consuming stale memory without an audit is a safety liability: in our primary GPT-4o setting, an agent that trusts raw memory dies more than twice as often as the same agent given no memory at all. Third, auditing helps but does not close the gap: a transparent read-time filter removes much of the safety cost in text mode, yet even oracle stale labels bring no further significant gain on the current grid size, and when visual auditing is unreliable, filtering yields no consistent benefit. Together these results frame spatial-memory staleness as a safety failure mode and isolate reliable visual grounding and action selection under memory--observation conflict as the central open challenges for memory-augmented agents.

</details>


### [39] [Causal Evidence Extraction and Triangulation in Crisis Reports using Large Language Models: A ReliefWeb-based Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04576)
*Yuanjun Zhang,Mourad Oussalah*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces an LLM-based pipeline to extract and aggregate causal evidence from long, noisy humanitarian reports, achieving high accuracy and proposing a framework to quantify cross-context evidence convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanitarian decision-makers rely on long, noisy, multi-topic narrative reports (e.g., ReliefWeb) that are hard to systematically search for causal evidence about what works in crises. Existing methods struggle to extract structured intervention-outcome relationships, control over-extraction, ensure auditability, and summarize evidence strength across diverse disasters and sources. The paper aims to automate and standardize this process so that causal claims from humanitarian reports can inform policy and programming more reliably and efficiently.

Method: The paper studies reports from ReliefWeb (2000–2024) and proposes a two-stage LLM pipeline for structured causal information extraction. Stage 1 performs query-conditioned extraction: given a specified intervention class (e.g., cash assistance), the LLM extracts only relevant intervention-outcome relations from reports, each annotated with direction (positive/negative/neutral) and strength attributes, which helps mitigate over-extraction caused by irrelevant retrievals. Stage 2 performs snippet grounding, linking each extracted relation to its exact supporting text span, enabling auditability and facilitating downstream classification. The authors evaluate multiple LLMs on an expert-annotated set of 100 reports, including closed-source models and an open-source Llama-3.1-8B fine-tuned with supervision for this task. Beyond extraction, the paper introduces a context-preserving triangulation approach: within each disaster×source cell, it aggregates evidence using strength-weighted signals, applies Laplace smoothing to stabilize estimates, and gives equal weight to each cell to avoid dominance by data-rich contexts. This produces a Level-of-Evidence (LoE) score that quantifies cross-context convergence on the effect of a given intervention on specific outcomes.

Result: In evaluation on the expert-annotated dataset of 100 humanitarian reports, the best closed-source LLM attains a weighted F1 score of 90.73% for intervention-outcome extraction with direction and strength attributes, doing so cost-efficiently. A supervised fine-tuned Llama-3.1-8B model performs even better, achieving 94.15% weighted F1, indicating that smaller open models can match or exceed closed-source performance when tailored to the task. Applying the full pipeline to cash assistance interventions, the context-preserving triangulation yields strong positive convergence for food-related outcomes, quantified by a high Level-of-Evidence score (LoE=0.865), and shows these positive effects persist over longer time horizons.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that LLM-based, query-conditioned extraction with snippet grounding is an effective and cost-efficient way to transform unstructured humanitarian reports into structured causal evidence. The high F1 scores from both closed-source and fine-tuned open-source models demonstrate that the approach is practically viable. The proposed context-preserving triangulation and Level-of-Evidence metric offer a principled way to aggregate heterogeneous textual evidence across disasters and sources while preserving context diversity, enabling more robust judgments about intervention effectiveness. Applied to cash assistance, the framework indicates strong, stable positive effects on food-related outcomes, showcasing its potential to inform humanitarian policy and program design at scale.

Abstract: Humanitarian reports are long, noisy, and multi-topic, making it difficult to consolidate decision-relevant causal evidence. We present a ReliefWeb study (2000-2024) and a two-stage Large Language Model (LLM) pipeline that extracts structured intervention-outcome records with direction and strength attributes. Query-conditioned extraction restricts output to a specified intervention class, reducing retrieval-induced over-extraction, while snippet grounding links each relation to supporting text for auditability and classification. In an expert-annotated dataset of 100 reports, the best closed-source LLM achieved a weighted F1 score of 90.73% with strong cost-efficiency, while Llama-3.1-8B with supervised fine-tuning reached 94.15% weighted F1 score. We further propose context-preserving triangulation that aggregates strength-weighted evidence within disaster$\times$source cells, applies Laplace smoothing and equally weights cells to quantify cross-context convergence via a Level-of-Evidence score. Applied to cash assistance, food-related outcomes show strong positive convergence (LoE=0.865) and stable long-horizon trajectories.

</details>


### [40] [Breaking the Curse ofMultilinguality inMany-to-Many Speech-to-Text Translation via a Resource-AwareMixture of Speech Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04586)
*Yexing Du,Kaiyuan Liu,Youcheng Pan,Bo Yang,Chengpeng Fu,Yu Wang,Ming Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes MSRT, a multilingual speech-to-text translation framework that uses a Mixture of Speech Encoders (MoSE) and curriculum learning to mitigate the curse of multilinguality, greatly improving low-resource languages without hurting high-resource ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multilingual speech-to-text translation systems typically rely on a single shared speech encoder for all languages. This shared encoder suffers from the curse of multilinguality: representation capacity is dominated by high-resource languages, causing clear performance degradation on medium- and low-resource languages. The authors aim to design a framework that preserves strong performance on high-resource languages while significantly boosting low-resource and medium-resource languages, and that can be trained with limited paired S2TT data per language.

Method: The authors introduce MSRT, centered on a resource-aware Mixture of Speech Encoders (MoSE). MoSE employs an explicit language router that assigns each input utterance to one of multiple expert speech encoders. At least two experts are defined: a frozen expert that retains capabilities for high-resource languages, and a trainable expert focused on adapting to and specializing in medium- and low-resource languages. On top of this architecture, they design a five-stage curriculum learning strategy that gradually aligns speech and text modalities and reduces dependence on large amounts of supervised data, requiring only about 10 hours of paired S2TT data per language for effective cross-lingual alignment. The approach is integrated into a 4B-parameter multimodal LLM for end-to-end S2TT.

Result: Across 45 languages and all 45×44 translation directions, the 4B-parameter MSRT model achieves state-of-the-art S2TT performance compared to substantially larger baseline models. The MoSE architecture improves translation quality simultaneously for high-, medium-, and low-resource languages, with the largest relative gains observed for low-resource speech inputs. The curriculum learning procedure enables effective training under very limited per-language paired data (≈10 hours), reducing data requirements compared to prior work.

Conclusion: MSRT with MoSE successfully mitigates the curse of multilinguality in multilingual speech-to-text translation. By using a resource-aware mixture of speech encoders and a carefully designed curriculum learning schedule, the framework enhances low-resource language performance without sacrificing high-resource results, achieving SOTA results with a relatively compact 4B-parameter model. The released code and models are intended to facilitate future research on scalable, data-efficient multilingual S2TT systems.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved significant success in speech-to-text translation (S2TT). However, when processing multilingual speech inputs, a single speech encoder shared across all languages suffers from the curse of multilinguality: languages at different resource levels compete for limited representation capacity, leading to strong high-resource performance but substantial degradation on low-resource speech. To address this problem and improve multilingual consistency, we propose MSRT, a novel framework built around a resource-aware Mixture of Speech Encoders (MoSE). MoSE uses an explicit language router to assign each utterance to an appropriate expert encoder. A frozen expert preserves high-resource language capabilities, while a trainable expert adapts to and specializes in medium- and low-resource languages. We further introduce a five-stage curriculum learning strategy that substantially reduces data dependence, requiring only 10 hours of paired S2TT data per language for effective alignment. We conduct extensive experiments on 45 languages, systematically evaluating all $45 \times 44$ translation directions. Our 4B-parameter model achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforming substantially larger baselines. Empirical analyses show that MoSE improves high-, medium-, and low-resource languages simultaneously, with the largest gains on low-resource speech, thereby breaking the curse of multilinguality without compromising high-resource performance. To support future multilingual S2TT research, we release our code and models.

</details>


### [41] [EASy: Towards Efficient LLM-Based Agentic System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04588)
*Junnan Liu,Linhao Luo,Thuy-Trang Vu,Gholamreza Haffari*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EASy is a reinforcement-learned agentic orchestration framework that coordinates heterogeneous executors to jointly optimize task success and computational efficiency via milestone-based planning and tree-structured rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based agentic systems focus mainly on maximizing task success and use simple routing heuristics, neglecting practical constraints like executor capability heterogeneity and computational cost. Router-based approaches cannot fully reason over rich, evolving task contexts, multi-step dependencies, or intermediate feedback, and generalize poorly to new executors. There is a need for a principled, trainable orchestration framework that optimizes both performance and efficiency under real-world constraints.

Method: EASy introduces an LLM-based orchestrator that is explicitly informed about the capability and cost profiles of diverse executors. It uses a milestone-plan-act workflow: (1) decompose complex tasks into milestones, (2) build a dependency-aware execution graph, (3) assign appropriate executors to each step, (4) parallelize independent steps, and (5) adapt future decisions based on intermediate results. Training uses a tree-structured rollout procedure that explores different milestone decompositions and execution plans, coupled with reinforcement learning. A multi-component reward function encodes task correctness, computational efficiency, and trajectory completeness to guide optimization.

Result: Across benchmarks in mathematical reasoning, embodied decision-making, and deep research, EASy yields better trade-offs between performance (task success) and efficiency (computational/resource cost) than strong agentic baselines. It demonstrates improved coordination of heterogeneous tools and better utilization of executor capabilities under cost constraints.

Conclusion: A reinforcement-learned, capability- and cost-aware orchestrator with milestone-based decomposition can substantially improve the performance-efficiency balance of agentic LLM systems compared with conventional router-based or heuristic orchestration. EASy offers a general framework for context-sensitive, adaptive coordination of heterogeneous executors and shows strong empirical gains across diverse complex task domains.

Abstract: Agentic systems have emerged as a promising paradigm for solving complex tasks by coordinating specialized LLM-based agents. However, most existing systems primarily optimize task success while giving limited consideration to execution efficiency under practical constraints such as executor capability and computational cost. Existing router-based methods have limited ability to reason over rich, evolving task contexts, multi-step dependencies, and intermediate execution feedback, and often generalize poorly to unseen executors. We propose EASy, a trainable agentic framework that jointly optimizes task performance and computational efficiency through reinforcement learning. EASy equips an LLM-based orchestrator with explicit knowledge of the capability and cost profiles of heterogeneous executors, enabling context-sensitive coordination beyond performance-only routing. It further introduces a milestone-plan-act workflow that decomposes complex tasks into manageable milestones, constructs dependency-aware execution graphs, assigns suitable executors, and parallelizes independent steps while adapting subsequent decisions to intermediate outcomes. To train the orchestrator, we develop a tree-structured rollout procedure that explores alternative milestone decompositions and execution plans, together with multi-component rewards that capture task correctness, execution efficiency, and trajectory completeness. Extensive experiments on mathematical reasoning, embodied decision-making, and deep research benchmarks show that EASy consistently achieves stronger performance-efficiency trade-offs than strong agentic baselines.

</details>


### [42] [When Absence Is Evidence: Evaluating Completeness-Sensitive Negative Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04591)
*Byoungjae Min,Kennedy Edemacu,Sae-Hong Cho,Yoonhyuk Choi,Beakcheol Jang,Jong Wook Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Study introduces CROWN-QA to test whether LLMs can correctly say “no” only when evidence fully covers a query, finding that models frequently overconfidently answer negatively when evidence is incomplete.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are widely used for question answering and often must determine whether something is absent from provided information (e.g., records, lists, retrieved passages). Correctly answering “no” requires understanding if the evidence set is complete with respect to the question; otherwise, the answer should be “unknown.” Existing benchmarks under-test this completeness-sensitive negative reasoning, so the authors aim to systematically probe it.

Method: The authors create CROWN-QA with two components: (1) CROWN-Synth, a synthetic, controlled paired dataset where questions and observed facts are held fixed but query-relative coverage is varied (fully covering vs. partially covering), and (2) CROWN-Real, a real-document-based contrast set with controlled coverage variants. They evaluate three different LLM families on these datasets, probing how models judge whether evidence is complete and whether they issue justified negative answers. They also experiment with prompting strategies and structured “certificate” elicitation to see how models characterize evidence coverage and where reasoning breaks down.

Result: Across all three LLM families, models exhibit unstable judgments about whether the evidence is complete and frequently over-closure: they answer “no” when the evidence is actually incomplete and the correct answer is “unknown.” In CROWN-Synth, the main failure pattern is asymmetric: models usually handle implicitly complete evidence correctly but often misinterpret implicitly partial evidence as fully covering the query. Prompting changes the distribution of errors between over-closure and under-closure instead of reliably reducing total errors. Certificate-style prompts reveal that many mistakes originate from mischaracterizing how much of the query the evidence covers. On CROWN-Real, this partial-coverage asymmetry also appears with real documents, although its intensity and the over-/under-closure balance differ by model, prompt, and data source.

Conclusion: LLMs are not reliably completeness-sensitive when reasoning about negative answers: they often treat incomplete evidence as if it fully covers the query and thus wrongly assert that something is absent. This over-closure persists across synthetic and real-document settings and is not robustly fixed by prompting alone. Effective use of LLMs in applications that depend on absence judgments will require methods that explicitly model or check evidence coverage rather than relying on current black-box LLM behavior.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are often asked whether something is absent from a record, list, or retrieved context. Yet non-observation licenses a negative answer only when evidence completely covers the query scope; otherwise, the answer should remain unknown. We call this completeness-sensitive negative reasoning. We introduce CROWN-QA, comprising CROWN-Synth, a controlled paired core that fixes the question and observed facts while varying only query-relative coverage, and CROWN-Real, a real-document contrast-set evaluation with controlled coverage variants. Across three LLM families, models show unstable closure judgments and substantial over-closure, failing to reliably distinguish a justified negative answer (Certified-Negative) from insufficient evidence (Unknown). The dominant CROWN-Synth failure is asymmetric: models often recognize implicitly complete evidence yet treat implicitly partial evidence as query-covering. Prompting redistributes errors between over- and under-closure rather than consistently resolving them. Structured certificate elicitation traces many errors to evidence-coverage mischaracterization. CROWN-Real shows that the core partial-coverage asymmetry persists on real-document content, while its strength and the balance between over- and under-closure vary by model, prompt, and source.

</details>


### [43] [Evaluating Theory of Mind in Reasoning Models: Robustness over Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04646)
*Ian B. de Haan,Peter van der Putten,Max van Duijn*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether improved Theory of Mind (ToM) performance in reasoning-focused LLMs reflects genuine ToM abilities or increased robustness in reasoning under varied prompts and task conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent LLMs show strong performance on Theory of Mind tests, raising questions about whether they possess ToM-like capabilities or are exploiting test artifacts. Meanwhile, reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has produced reasoning-oriented LLMs that perform better on many benchmarks. The authors want to understand what lies behind these ToM gains and whether they stem from genuine ToM abilities or from more general reasoning robustness.

Method: The authors apply reasoning-oriented LLMs, trained with reinforcement learning and verifiable rewards, to a combination of adapted machine-psychological Theory of Mind experiments and existing ToM benchmarks. They systematically vary prompts and perturb ToM tasks to observe how model performance changes and to compare robustness between standard and reasoning-focused LLMs.

Result: Reasoning-oriented LLMs show consistently greater robustness: their ToM task performance degrades less under prompt variations and task perturbations, and they reach correct answers more reliably than baseline models when conditions are changed.

Conclusion: The improved performance of reasoning-oriented LLMs on Theory of Mind tasks appears to be better explained by increased robustness in reaching correct answers under variation, rather than by the emergence of a distinct, Theory of Mind-specific cognitive ability. The authors thus favor a robustness-based account of ToM performance in these models.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have recently shown strong performance on Theory of Mind (ToM) tests, prompting debate about the nature and validity of the underlying capabilities. At the same time, reasoning-oriented LLMs trained via reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards have demonstrated notable improvements across a range of benchmarks. In this work, we examine the behavior of such reasoning models in ToM tasks using novel adaptations of machine psychological experiments together with results from established benchmarks. We observe that reasoning models consistently exhibit increased robustness to prompt variations and task perturbations. Our analysis suggests these gains come at least partly from models being more robust at reaching the correct answer under prompt and task variation. We read this as evidence for a robustness-based account rather than for a new ToM-specific ability.

</details>


### [44] [Easy to Complete, Hard to Choose: Investigating LLM Performance on the ProverbIT Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04670)
*Enrico Mensa,Lorenzo Zane,Calogero Jerik Scozzaro,Matteo Delsanto,Tommaso Milani,Daniele Paolo Radicioni*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents ProverbIT, an Italian benchmark of 100 multiple-choice questions to test how LLMs complete and reason about Italian proverbs, revealing that models know the proverbs but fail when correct answers are missing from choices, exposing shallow, pattern-based reasoning about figurative language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLMs excel on many NLP tasks, we lack precise understanding of how they handle culturally embedded, figurative expressions like proverbs. Existing evaluations rarely target nuanced cultural and figurative competence or differentiate between memorization of surface forms and genuine semantic understanding. The authors aim to fill this gap by systematically probing how well LLMs handle Italian proverbs, especially under conditions that stress reasoning rather than recall.

Method: The authors build ProverbIT, a benchmark of 100 Italian proverb-completion questions in multiple-choice format. They test 13 advanced models, including Large Reasoning Models and standard LLMs, on three tasks: (1) free proverb completion, (2) multiple-choice selection where the correct ending is present, and (3) multiple-choice selection where all given endings are wrong. Performance is compared across tasks, and for two LRMs they perform detailed Chain-of-Thought analysis to study the internal reasoning, focusing on how models choose options and whether they recognize missing correct answers.

Result: Most models accurately complete Italian proverbs when asked to fill in the ending, indicating that they have memorized or learned many proverb patterns. They also perform well on multiple-choice tasks when the correct completion is among the options. However, when none of the provided options is correct, performance degrades sharply—even for state-of-the-art reasoning models. Chain-of-Thought analysis shows that models often generate the correct ending in their reasoning, yet still choose an incorrect option, typically one that is a literal synonym or superficially similar to the proverb’s correct form.

Conclusion: The study concludes that current LLMs, including advanced reasoning models, rely mainly on memorized patterns and surface similarity rather than deep semantic understanding when dealing with culturally grounded figurative language such as proverbs. Their inability to detect when the correct proverb ending is absent from multiple-choice options, despite often articulating it in their reasoning, reveals a key limitation in their reasoning and figurative comprehension. ProverbIT thus exposes an important gap between linguistic fluency and genuine cultural-semantic understanding in LLMs.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have transformed computational linguistics and achieved remarkable performance across numerous natural language processing tasks, yet significant gaps persist in understanding how these systems process culturally embedded linguistic expressions. This paper introduces ProverbIT, a novel Italian benchmark comprising 100 multiple-choice questions designed to evaluate LLMs' ability to complete Italian proverbs. We assess 13 frontier models, including Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) and traditional LLMs, across three tasks: proverb completion, multiple-choice selection with correct answers, and multiple-choice selection without correct answers. Our evaluation reveals surprising results: while nearly all models demonstrate knowledge of the proverbs through successful completion tasks, performance drops dramatically when transitioning to multiple-choice formats without correct answers, with even state-of-the-art reasoning models showing substantial degradation. Through detailed Chain-of-Thought analysis of two LRMs, we uncover that models exhibit a strong bias toward selecting literal synonyms and frequently mention correct proverb endings during reasoning without successfully identifying their absence from the given options. These findings suggest that current LLMs rely heavily on memorized patterns rather than deeper semantic understanding of culturally grounded expressions, highlighting important limitations in their reasoning capabilities for figurative language comprehension.

</details>


### [45] [Kathleen Writes: Autoregressive Generation and Data Scaling Without Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04678)
*George Fountzoulas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates a small, attention-free byte-level language model and shows it can outperform similarly sized transformers in language modeling, proposes a new metric for text quality, and demonstrates that decoding strategies and in-corpus retrieval significantly improve generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether a compact, attention-free architecture that previously worked well for classification can also scale effectively to language generation, and to better measure and improve the "reads like text" quality of generated outputs without relying on vulnerable parametric evaluators.

Method: They use a wavetable encoder with a multi-scale reverberant state as an attention-free byte-level language model trained directly on raw UTF-8 WikiText-103 at multiple dataset sizes. They compare it to parameter-matched transformers, introduce a non-parametric FORM DISTANCE metric over nine statistical properties of human text and five synthetic baselines, and systematically vary decoding policies, including widening sampling and a retrieval-augmented decoding scheme that injects sparse phrases from the model’s own training corpus. They also test several architectural tweaks and build a compact computed lexicon to approximate a learned embedding table.

Result: The reverberant model consistently beats a size-matched transformer in bits-per-byte across all dataset scales from 2–512 MB (e.g., 1.84 vs 2.04 bpb at 512 MB with ~0.5M parameters). FORM DISTANCE successfully rejects several constructed fake texts and quantifies generation quality. Generation quality depends more on decoding than architecture: a wider sampler halves FORM DISTANCE (3.17→1.52), and retrieval-augmented decoding further improves it (1.52→1.14) without extra training, with gains attributable to the injected phrases. However, retrieval helps only when phrases come from the model’s own training data; a much larger external corpus brings no benefit, mirroring behavior in the transformer baseline. The tested architectural additions provide no gains, while the computed lexicon achieves 94% of a learned table’s top-1 accuracy using only one fifth of the parameters. All experiments are runnable on a single free Kaggle T4 GPU.

Conclusion: Small, attention-free, byte-level language models with reverberant state can outperform parameter-matched transformers in language modeling when trained from scratch, provided appropriate decoding strategies are used. Text quality is better understood with a robust non-parametric metric like FORM DISTANCE rather than only perplexity. For generation, decoding policy and in-corpus retrieval-augmented sampling matter more than modest architectural modifications, and in-context use of retrieved information appears limited by whether that information overlaps the model’s training distribution and by model scale. Efficient lexicon computation can further reduce parameters with minimal accuracy loss, enabling fully reproducible experiments on modest hardware.

Abstract: Papers 1-2 of the Kathleen series showed that a byte-level, attention-free architecture built from a wavetable encoder and multi-scale reverberant state can match strong baselines on classification at ~450-700K parameters, without pretraining. We ask whether the same ingredients can generate. (1) Scaling: on byte-level language modeling (WikiText-103, raw UTF-8, no tokenizer), the reverberant model beats a parameter-matched transformer at every dataset scale measured (2-512 MB), e.g. 1.84 vs 2.04 bits/byte at 512 MB with ~0.5M parameters; the transformer needs more than 512 MB to match what the attention-free model learns from 32 MB. (2) Measurement: we introduce FORM DISTANCE, a non-parametric, gaming-resistant instrument for "reads like text": nine statistical axes of human text define a reference cloud, and five constructed fakes are all rejected. (3) Generation: decoding policy dominates architecture -- widening the sampler halves the same model's distance (3.17 to 1.52), and a retrieval-augmented decoding scheme takes the frozen model further (1.52 to 1.14) with no training step involved; the ablation attributes the gain to the sparse phrase dose itself, not the selection gate. The gain has a sharp boundary condition: the phrases must come from the model's own training corpus -- a 40x larger foreign library helps not at all, an effect the attention twin shares, consistent with in-context integration being a capability of scale. We also report four architectural additions that did not help, and a computed lexicon reaching 94% of a learned table's top-1 accuracy at one fifth of the parameters. Everything runs offline; all experiments are reproducible on a free Kaggle T4.

</details>


### [46] [IslamicTurathBench: A Multi-Task, Multi-Discipline Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models on the Islamic Scholarly Tradition (turath)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04703)
*Shahd Gaben,Heba Sbahi,Samer Rashwani,Abdessalam Bouchekif,Mutaz Al-Khatib,Emad Mohamed,Somaya Eltanbouly,Mohammed Ghaly*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build and release IslamicTurathBench, a benchmark to test LLMs on classical Islamic scholarship using expert-curated questions from authoritative sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are being used in religious and cultural domains, but for Islamic Studies the core classical scholarly tradition (turath) lacks high‑quality, annotated evaluation resources, making it hard to judge whether models answer in line with authoritative scholarship or hallucinate/misrepresent it.

Method: Domain experts collected and curated 3,465 QA items from 35 canonical works spanning 12+ centuries and seven subfields of Islamic Studies. They organised them into a 2D framework: three scholarly-demand levels (Beginner, Intermediate, Advanced) and three task formats (MCQ, passage comprehension, open-ended questions). They obtained aggregated judgments from a panel of scholars and ran zero-shot evaluations on ten LLMs as baselines.

Result: The outcome is the IslamicTurathBench (ISTB) dataset plus baseline performance scores for ten LLMs and aggregated human reference scores, enabling fine-grained analysis of model strengths and weaknesses across books, disciplines, difficulty levels, and task types.

Conclusion: ISTB offers a reproducible, expert-grounded benchmark for assessing LLM behaviour on historically layered Islamic scholarly material, filling a gap in resources and allowing systematic profiling and comparison of models in this sensitive, specialised domain.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for question answering, education, and research, including in religious and cultural domains where answers depend on specialised source traditions. Yet in Islamic Studies, key concepts, methods, and debates preserved in the authoritative scholarly tradition, known as turath, lack high-quality annotated resources. We introduce IslamicTurathBench (ISTB), a multi-task, multi-discipline dataset for evaluating LLMs on classical Islamic scholarship. Developed and reviewed by domain experts, ISTB contains 3,465 question-answer items drawn from 35 recognised source works spanning more than 12 centuries of scholarship across seven key fields of Islamic Studies. To enable comprehensive profiling of model capabilities, ISTB is structured along two axes: scholarly demand (Beginner, Intermediate, and Advanced) and task format (multiple-choice questions, passage-based comprehension, and open-ended knowledge questions). ISTB includes aggregated scores from a scholarly human reference panel and zero-shot baselines from ten systems. The dataset supports reproducible evaluation of language-model behaviour across source works, disciplines, scholarly-demand levels, and question formats in a historically layered scholarly domain.

</details>


### [47] [EmpaAva: An Open-source Agentic 3D-Avatar Empathetic Live Chatbot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04709)
*Jie Yang,Wenhao Xu,Shuhui Lin,Hao Fei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EmpaAva is an open-source, agentic 3D-avatar empathetic chatbot that turns text-based empathetic response generation into live, face-to-face multimodal interaction and outperforms text-only and 2D-avatar baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most empathetic chatbots focus on text-only settings and cannot support rich, face-to-face interactions with synchronized voice, facial expressions, and 3D embodiment. There is a gap between research on empathetic response generation and practical, multimodal, controllable agents that feel like human conversations while remaining open-source and inspectable.

Method: The authors design EmpaAva, a system where a large language model coordinates a Tri-Agent Architecture: (1) a perception agent that infers user affect from speech and optional vision; (2) an empathetic response planning agent that generates an intent and a multimodal response plan; and (3) an embodied rendering agent that produces emotional speech, lip-synced facial motion, and 3D Gaussian rendering. A Response Planning layer compiles each reply into an executable multimodal plan so that voice, expressions, and rendering remain aligned with a single empathetic intent. The system is built by integrating strong open-source modules and adding coordinating intelligence for control and interpretability.

Result: In both automatic metrics and human evaluations, EmpaAva outperforms text-only chatbots, 2D talking-face systems, and other multimodal avatar baselines on emotion understanding, quality of responses, and consistency between audio and visual outputs.

Conclusion: EmpaAva demonstrates that an open-source, agentic, 3D-avatar chatbot can bring empathetic response generation into live, face-to-face human-AI interaction and achieve superior performance over existing baselines. By coordinating perception, planning, and rendering in a closed loop and exposing controllable, inspectable components, it offers a practical platform for empathetic multimodal conversational agents, which the authors release publicly with code and a live demo.

Abstract: This paper presents EmpaAva, to our knowledge the first open-source, agentic 3D-avatar empathetic chatbot, which carries empathetic response generation (ERG) from text-only exchanges into live, face-to-face interaction. Through a video-call-like interface, a user speaks to a 3D digital human that reads their affect from speech and optional vision, and replies with emotional speech, lip-synced facial motion, and photorealistic 3D Gaussian rendering. At its core, an LLM coordinates a Tri-Agent Architecture, in which perception, empathetic response planning, and embodied rendering form a closed loop, paired with a Response Planning layer that compiles each reply into an executable multimodal plan, keeping voice, expression, and rendering on one empathetic intent. Building on strong open-source modules, EmpaAva supplies the intelligence that binds them into one controllable, inspectable experience. In automatic and human evaluations, EmpaAva surpasses text-only, 2D talking-face, and multimodal avatar baselines in emotion understanding, response quality, and audio-visual consistency. We open-source EmpaAva with an online live demo.

</details>


### [48] [Caching for the Future: Scrub Jay Episodic Memory Principles for Agent Memory Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04746)
*Kartikey Singh Bhandari,Aarya Wadhwani,Dhruv Kumar,Pratik Narang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes ScrubJay-MEM, a memory system for LLM agents that applies type-conditioned temporal decay to stored memories, improving temporal reasoning and retention of perishable facts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent memory systems treat all memories as equally persistent, which leads to contamination of retrieved context with outdated or invalid information. Biological inspiration from western scrub jay episodic memory suggests that incorporating temporal decay per memory could mitigate this issue and better handle perishable information.

Method: The authors design ScrubJay-MEM, where each memory is stored as a What--Where--When tuple augmented with an auto-classified perishability coefficient (π_i) and utility horizon (τ_i). Memories are retrieved via query-adaptive scoring that accounts for perishability and are updated retroactively with O(1) LLM calls. They also introduce a new benchmark, the Temporal Generalization Test (TGT), with held-out retention intervals and a Generalization Gap (GenGap) metric to evaluate temporal generalization.

Result: On the TGT benchmark, ScrubJay-MEM is the only retrieval-based system with a substantially positive Generalization Gap (+0.108). On the MemoryAgentBench EventQA-64k benchmark, ScrubJay-MEM improves F1 score by +2.66 over Mem0 and +3.09 over Qwen3-Embedding-4B under a specified LLM backbone. An ablation study removing temporal decay drastically reduces GenGap by 5.7×.

Conclusion: Type-conditioned temporal decay in LLM-agent memory is necessary and effective for improving temporal reasoning over perishable facts. However, benefits diminish with stronger LLM backbones and can reverse on fact-consolidation tasks, indicating that the approach is especially suited to managing temporally sensitive, perishable information rather than stable factual knowledge.

Abstract: LLM agents that persist across sessions accumulate stored memories whose validity varies enormously by content type, yet existing memory architectures treat all memories as equally persistent and systematically contaminate retrieved context with outdated facts. We show that per-memory, type-conditioned temporal decay, a property of western scrub jay episodic memory, can be operationalized as an auto-classified coefficient $π_i$ in an external LLM-agent memory store, yielding ScrubJay-MEM: each memory is encoded as a jointly-bound What--Where--When tuple with an estimated perishability $π_i$ and utility horizon $τ_i$, retrieved by query-adaptive scoring, and revised retroactively at $O(1)$ LLM calls per update. We introduce the Temporal Generalization Test (TGT), a benchmark with held-out retention intervals and a Generalization Gap (GenGap) metric. On TGT, ScrubJay-MEM is the only retrieval-based system with substantially positive GenGap ($+0.108$); on MemoryAgentBench EventQA-64k it improves F1 by $+2.66$ over Mem0 and $+3.09$ over Qwen3-Embedding-4B under a llm backbone. A decay ablation collapses GenGap by $5.7\times$, establishing type-conditioned decay as necessary for the result. Gains narrow under stronger backbones and reverse on fact-consolidation tasks, scoping the contribution to temporal reasoning over perishable facts.

</details>


### [49] [InsightEmb: Learning Action-Intent Embeddings for Agentic Insight Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04761)
*Tsz Ting Chung,Jiangnan Li,Jie Zhou,Mo Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They introduce InsightEmb, a new embedding method for agents that retrieves past insights based on whether they help current decision-making, not just semantic similarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-improving agents gather a lot of past trajectories and insights, but current retrieval mainly focuses on semantic similarity. This often fails to surface the specific prior insight that would actually resolve the agent's current decision bottleneck. There is a need for a retrieval mechanism that is progress-oriented and can transfer across tasks and domains without environment-specific supervision.

Method: They propose InsightEmb, a contrastive embedding framework trained only on mathematical reasoning data. The method learns a retrieval geometry that is progress-oriented by: (1) aligning concrete problem states with abstract heuristic rules, and (2) clustering reasoning trajectories that share similar progress structures. This creates embeddings where states are close to the insights that help them move forward and trajectories with similar reasoning progress are grouped together.

Result: In experiments on both dynamic agent tasks and a static skill-retrieval benchmark, InsightEmb improves performance over existing reasoning embedding models without any environment-specific training. It consistently yields better retrieval of helpful insights, which leads to improved agent performance.

Conclusion: The geometry of matching between states and helpful insights can be learned in a way that generalizes across domains. By training only on publicly available mathematical reasoning data and avoiding environment-specific supervision, InsightEmb can provide effective, progress-oriented insight retrieval for various agentic tasks.

Abstract: Self-improving agents accumulate reusable insights from prior trajectories, making retrieval increasingly important for turning accumulated experience into actionable guidance. At each decision step, retrieving the right insight can help the agent progress toward its goal, a setting we refer to as agentic insight retrieval. However, existing retrieval methods primarily model semantic similarity, while overlooking whether a retrieved insight resolves the agent's current decision bottleneck. We propose InsightEmb, a contrastive embedding framework that learns transferable progress-oriented retrieval geometry using only mathematical reasoning data. InsightEmb jointly learns to align concrete situations with abstract heuristic rules and to cluster reasoning trajectories with similar progress structures. We evaluate InsightEmb on dynamic agent tasks and a static skill-retrieval benchmark. Without any environment-specific training, InsightEmb improves over all these evaluations, surpassing the performance of existing reasoning embedding models. These results suggest that the geometry of state-insight matching can transfer across domains, enabling effective training from publicly available reasoning data without expensive environment-specific supervision.

</details>


### [50] [Guideline-as-Oracle: Zero-Annotation Training of an Ophthalmic Telephone Triage Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04772)
*Chenyu Wang,Yi Liu,Baoqing Li,Min Tu,Diping Song*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes GAO, using ophthalmology guidelines as the sole supervision source to train multi-turn medical triage agents, avoiding costly and privacy-sensitive expert dialog annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling multi-turn medical dialogue agents is hindered by expensive expert annotation and restrictions on using real clinical conversations due to privacy concerns. The authors seek a scalable, guideline-based supervision method that can still yield high-quality triage performance without relying on large frontier models at inference.

Method: They compile American Academy of Ophthalmology guidelines into a 70-row operational rule table, then generate and label 3,000 training dialogues using only these rules as instance-level supervision. They define eight strategies for constructing dialogues and labels from the rules (e.g., cited-row tier assignment, one-fact boundary pairs, metadata-only repair, label repair), and analyze their evidential status (how directly they connect rules to labels). They fine-tune a 9B-parameter base model on this corpus to create GAO-Triage and evaluate it against an operational reference set, as well as test robustness across seeds and a separate patient simulator. They also perform ablations like permuting label-dialogue assignments.

Result: GAO-Triage, the fine-tuned 9B model, improves agreement with a 201-case ophthalmic triage reference from 61.7% to 74.1% and raises recall on emergent cases from 9.5% to 69.0%, with statistical significance (McNemar p=0.0046). These improvements are consistent across a second random seed and a different patient simulator. Seven tested general-purpose systems do not simultaneously beat GAO-Triage on both agreement and emergent recall, and GAO-Triage does not require frontier-scale models at inference. When dialogue-label pairings are permuted, performance collapses to a constant-routine predictor, confirming that the real signal comes from guideline-based label assignments rather than superficial dialogue patterns. Label repair also removes an observed late-training safety degradation.

Conclusion: Guideline-as-Oracle shows that compact, rule-based supervision derived from clinical guidelines can effectively train multi-turn medical triage agents without direct expert dialogue annotation or large proprietary models. Carefully designed dialogue–label construction strategies are crucial, as evidenced by ablations, and label repair can mitigate safety issues that emerge late in training. This approach may generalize to other guideline-rich medical domains, offering a scalable, privacy-preserving path to high-quality triage systems.

Abstract: Scaling supervision for multi-turn medical agents is difficult because expert dialogue annotation is costly and clinical conversations are privacy-restricted. We introduce Guideline-as-Oracle (GAO), which compiles American Academy of Ophthalmology guidance into a 70-row operational rule table and uses it as the sole source of instance-level supervision for 3,000 training dialogues, reserving human labeling for evaluation. Because converting rules into dialogues is itself a design problem, we catalog eight construction strategies, including cited-row tier assignment, one-fact boundary pairs, metadata-only repair, and label repair, and characterize the evidential status of each: labeling mechanism, null, confounded, or evaluated only as a package. Fine-tuning a 9B backbone on this corpus yields GAO-Triage, improving agreement with a 201-case operational reference from 61.7% to 74.1% (exact McNemar p=0.0046) and emergent-case recall from 9.5% to 69.0%; the gains persist across a second seed and patient simulator. None of the seven general-purpose systems we test dominates GAO-Triage on both metrics, and GAO-Triage requires no frontier model at inference time. Permuting label-dialogue assignments collapses the model to a constant-routine predictor, indicating that the signal lies in guideline-derived assignment rather than dialogue surface form. Label repair coincides with the disappearance of a late-training safety degradation.

</details>


### [51] [Reachability in 3-VAS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04786)
*Łukasz Kamiński,Sławomir Lasota*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper establishes the precise computational complexity of the reachability problem for low-dimensional stateless vector addition systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The complexity of reachability in low-dimensional VAS, especially dimensions 2-4, had an unresolved gap, known only to lie between NP and PSPACE. Clarifying this is important for both theory and applications in verification and model checking.

Method: The authors analyze symmetric 3-dimensional VAS, constructing reductions that show PSPACE-hardness of the reachability problem in this restricted setting, and combine this with known PSPACE upper bounds.

Result: They prove that reachability in symmetric 3-dimensional VAS is PSPACE-hard, which implies that reachability in 3-VAS and 4-VAS (and their symmetric fragments) is PSPACE-complete.

Conclusion: The work settles the open problem of the exact complexity of reachability in low-dimensional VAS (dimensions 3 and 4), showing that the problem is PSPACE-complete even for symmetric systems, thereby closing the NP–PSPACE gap in these dimensions.

Abstract: We settle the exact complexity of the reachability problem in (stateless) vector addition systems (VAS) in fixed low dimension. In dimensions 2-4 it has only been known to be sandwiched between NP and PSPACE. We prove PSPACE-hardness of the reachability problem for symmetric vector addition systems in dimension 3 (3-VAS), a restricted fragment of general 3-VAS. Combined with previously established PSPACE upper bounds, our result settles the complexity of the problem to be PSPACE-complete in 3-VAS and 4-VAS, as well as in their symmetric fragments.

</details>


### [52] [A Modular Part-of-Speech Tagger for Scottish Gaelic using spaCy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04808)
*Peter Stefan,Peter J Barclay,Alistair Lawson*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper builds POS taggers for Scottish Gaelic using spaCy with minimal configuration and no external resources, achieving accuracies comparable to prior work, and showing that simple, off-the-shelf pipelines can work well for low-resource, morphologically rich languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-resource, morphologically rich languages like Scottish Gaelic lack annotated corpora and digital tools, which makes standard NLP approaches hard to apply. Existing taggers are few and often complex or resource-intensive. The authors want to see whether a lightweight, modular, off-the-shelf framework (spaCy) can produce competitive POS taggers for such a language with minimal manual preprocessing and without relying on large external embeddings or pre-trained language models.

Method: Using the Annotated Reference Corpus of Scottish Gaelic, the authors train two supervised POS taggers within the spaCy framework. They perform minimal preprocessing and configuration, avoiding external word embeddings and pre-trained language models. One model is trained with a fine-grained POS tagset reflecting detailed morphological distinctions; the other uses a reduced, coarse-grained tagset. Both models rely solely on the annotated corpus for learning, and their performance is evaluated in terms of tagging accuracy and compared with two existing Scottish Gaelic taggers.

Result: The fine-grained POS tagging model reaches an accuracy of 88.6%, while the coarse-grained model achieves 93.7% accuracy. These scores are on par with, or comparable to, the two previously published Scottish Gaelic POS taggers, despite using simpler architectures and no external resources.

Conclusion: Even with limited annotated data and without external embeddings or pre-trained models, a straightforward spaCy-based pipeline can yield POS taggers for Scottish Gaelic whose performance matches existing systems. This suggests that simple, off-the-shelf NLP frameworks are a viable and efficient approach for building tools for other low-resource, morphologically complex languages, lowering the barrier to creating basic language technologies for endangered languages.

Abstract: Part-of-speech tagging for low-resource languages remains challenging due to limited annotated data, especially for linguistically complex languages. Gaidhlig (Scottish Gaelic) is a morphologically rich and endangered language with limited digital resources, making it suitable for examining a lightweight language processing approach. This paper describes using the modular spaCy Natural Language Processing framework to build part-of-speech taggers for Gaidhlig using the Annotated Reference Corpus of Scottish Gaelic. We train two models with minimal pre-processing and configuration: one using a fine-grained tagset and another using a reduced coarse-grained tagset. Both models are trained without external embeddings or pre-trained language models, using only supervised learning from the available corpus. The fine-grained model achieves 88.6% tagging accuracy, while the coarse-grained model achieves 93.7%. The results are comparable to those of the two previously published Gaidhlig taggers, indicating that simple, off-the-shelf language processing pipelines can demonstrate good performance in low-resource and morphologically complex linguistic settings.

</details>


### [53] [Skill-Use: Can LLMs Actually Use Skills in Agentic Harnesses?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04828)
*Jinyi Han,Yuanjian Xu,Ying Liao,Xinyi Wang,Zishang Jiang,Zixiang Di,Fanyang Lu,Zhichao Hu,Yanghua Xiao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Skill-Use, a benchmark to test whether LLM agents can correctly trigger, follow, and respect the boundaries of structured skills under limited initial information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While many works design skills/tools and measure final task success, they rarely test if an LLM agent can independently recognize when a skill applies, retrieve it, and execute it correctly. This gap obscures whether failures are due to bad skills or bad skill use. The authors want a principled way to evaluate the agent’s ability to use skills themselves, under realistic conditions where only names and short descriptions are initially visible.

Method: The authors design Skill-Use, a benchmark in which agents start with only the name and brief description of skills. Agents must decide when to trigger a skill, then retrieve and follow its full procedure while avoiding forbidden actions. They decompose evaluation into three dimensions: Trigger (invoking the right skill), Compliance (following the procedure faithfully), and Boundary (respecting prohibitions). These are combined into a Skill-Use (SU) score that awards execution only if the skill is triggered. The benchmark includes 79 real skills and 177 executable tasks across nine domains, each executed in isolated Docker sandboxes and scored based on the action trajectory using a rubric. Eight LLMs are evaluated under two different agent harnesses.

Result: The best-performing model–harness configuration attains an SU score of only 0.613, indicating that reliable skill use is still lacking. Both Trigger and Compliance are limiting factors, and neither alone explains failures. Model performance and relative rankings vary substantially across harnesses, revealing a strong dependence of skill-use capability on the agent harness rather than solely on the underlying LLM.

Conclusion: Skill-Use shows that current LLM agents are far from reliably recognizing and executing structured skills, with failures both in triggering appropriate skills and in following procedures/boundaries. Skill-use behavior is highly conditioned on the surrounding agent harness, meaning that evaluating or improving skill use requires considering the whole agent system, not just the base model. The benchmark offers a structured way to diagnose and compare these capabilities across models and harness designs.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on skills, structured documents that specify when to act, which procedure to follow, and which tools are allowed. Existing evaluations mostly judge the quality of a skill or its contribution to task success, leaving unexamined whether an agent can recognize a relevant skill and apply it on its own. We introduce Skill-Use, a benchmark that evaluates skill use under progressive disclosure, where an agent sees only a skill's name and short description and must retrieve the full procedure before following it. Skill-Use separates three facets of skill use. Trigger measures whether the agent invokes the relevant skill, Compliance measures how faithfully it follows the prescribed procedure, and Boundary measures whether it avoids forbidden operations. A Skill-Use (SU) score combines the three and credits execution only after the skill is triggered. Skill-Use pairs 79 real skills with 177 executable tasks across nine domains, each grounded in real files, run in an isolated Docker sandbox, and scored by a trajectory-based rubric. Evaluating eight LLMs under two agent harnesses, we find that reliable skill use remains out of reach, as the strongest configuration reaches an SU of only 0.613. Triggering and procedural compliance fail as independent bottlenecks, and both scores and model rankings shift with the harness, so skill use behaves as a capability conditioned on the harness rather than a fixed property of the model.

</details>


### [54] [Do Language Models Know Their Slang? Queer Slang Understanding in User-Generated Content](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04847)
*Arianna Denitto,Beatrice Savoldi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces Slang-Q, a curated dataset and taxonomy of queer slang to evaluate language models’ ability to understand and define queer slang.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Queer slang, though culturally important and widespread, is largely absent from NLP research and benchmarks. This lack of representation means we don’t know how language models handle sensitive, community-specific expressions, nor whether they provide accurate information about them.

Method: The authors construct a taxonomy of 118 queer slang terms, then manually curate a dataset of naturally occurring English sentences containing these terms, pairing each with the slang item and a reference definition. Using this dataset (Slang-Q), they run exploratory evaluations of language models, testing their capacity to interpret and define queer slang under different prompting setups.

Result: They obtain initial empirical evidence on how well current language models understand and define queer slang, and how performance varies with changes in prompting conditions. The abstract does not provide numeric results but positions these evaluations as a first systematic look at this capability.

Conclusion: Slang-Q offers a foundation for systematically studying language models’ behavior on queer slang and, more broadly, on sensitive, community-specific language. It is proposed as a resource to gauge whether models can give accurate, reliable information about identity-linked and culturally embedded linguistic expressions.

Abstract: Despite its cultural relevance and diffusion, queer slang remains underrepresented in Natural Language Processing research. Towards addressing this gap, we introduce Slang-Q, a manually curated dataset of naturally user-generated English sentences paired with queer slang terms and reference definitions, built upon a newly constructed taxonomy of 118 queer terms. We use this resource to conduct a first exploratory evaluation of language models on their ability to understand and define queer slang under varying prompting conditions. Slang-Q is intended as a basis for studying how current models handle sensitive, community-specific language and whether they can provide accurate and reliable information about such forms of identity and linguistic expression.

</details>


### [55] [Preverbal Uninflected and Underived Roots in Mapudungun. Wuno and Its Implications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04869)
*Andres Chandia*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper reassesses the grammatical status of preverbal uninflected roots in Mapudungun (especially wuno), arguing that they are best analyzed within a radical concatenation model rather than as auxiliaries, prefixes, or particles in the traditional sense.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is long-standing disagreement about how to classify certain preverbal elements in Mapudungun, variously treated as auxiliaries, modal prefixes, particles, or parts of complex verb stems. This lack of consensus obscures their true grammatical behavior and complicates descriptions of Mapudungun, particularly because the language had no pre-contact written tradition and early orthographic practices may have distorted prosodic and syntactic realities.

Method: The authors conduct a critical review of previous classifications (Smeets, Longkon, Zúñiga) and perform a diachronic corpus analysis covering texts from 1606 to the present. They track the distribution and behavior of several preverbal roots (wuno, kim, shinge, pepi, kalli, kupa) across time, examining their positional patterns (V1/V2), orthographic variation, and prosodic clues inferred from historical transcriptions.

Result: The analysis identifies three distinct behavioral profiles: (1) stable V1 compounds like kim and shinge; (2) volatile V1 items like pepi and wuno whose written forms vary with orthographic conventions; and (3) a genuine particle, kalli. The authors also uncover V2 uses of kim and kupa, demonstrating that these elements can function as independent lexical verbs. These findings undermine prior classifications that treated them solely as bound or auxiliary forms.

Conclusion: The authors propose that what has been described as variable syntactic binding largely reflects the fossilization of prosodic pauses that early missionaries encoded as spaces, later reinterpreted as syntactic boundaries. On this basis, they argue that Zúñiga’s radical concatenation model best captures the grammar of these elements in Mapudungun. The study further suggests that analyses of languages without pre-contact writing systems must carefully disentangle prosodic, orthographic, and syntactic evidence when building grammatical models.

Abstract: This study examines the grammatical status of preverbal uninflected and underived roots in Mapudungun, with particular focus on wuno 'return/re-'. Through a critical review of scholarly classifications--auxiliaries (Smeets, 2008), modal prefixes (Longkon, 2011), and preverbal particles/complex verb stems (Zúñiga, 2006)--we demonstrate the limitations of existing frameworks. A diachronic corpus analysis spanning four centuries (1606-present) reveals that these elements exhibit three distinct profiles: stable V1 compounds (kim, shinge), volatile V1 rates reflecting orthographic shift (pepi, wuno), and a true particle (kalli). The discovery of V2 attestations for kim and kupa confirms their status as full lexical verbs. We propose a prosodic-orthographic hypothesis: apparent "variable binding" results from the fossilization of prosodic pauses transcribed by early missionaries as spaces, a convention later reanalysed by speakers as syntactic boundaries. The evidence supports Zúñiga's radical concatenation as the correct grammatical model, with implications for the study of languages with no pre-contact written tradition.

</details>


### [56] [A-SR: Self-Evolving Agentic LLMs for Symbolic Regression via Hierarchical Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04872)
*Wenxiao Zhao,Dong Liu,Kaiyi Xu,Feng Liu,Zhen Zhao,Fei Ben,Shu Wang,Wenhao Li,Yingnian Wu,Fenghua Ling,Haobo Li,Lei Bai*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces A-SR, an agentic symbolic regression framework that uses role-conditioned agents and process memory instead of a single monolithic proposal loop, yielding substantially better accuracy on benchmarks and real scientific tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-guided symbolic regression methods treat search as a unified loop driven by a single prompt and scalar score. This collapses different types of search failures, provides little structure for learning from them, and limits adaptability across tasks and runs. The authors want a framework that can explicitly manage heterogeneous feedback, reuse motifs and failure traces, and systematically improve both the search process and the underlying models over time.

Method: They design A-SR, a self-evolving multi-agent framework where control is organized around role-conditioned evidence views rather than direct expression edits. The system uses: (1) multiple coordination protocols that define how agents interact; (2) an online evaluator-reward role policy that tracks reliability and productivity of different roles or agents; and (3) state-routed process memory that stores and routes elite formula motifs, failure traces, and diagnostics to appropriate agents. Evaluator feedback is used online to update role utilities and to decide how information flows among agents, enabling adaptation of the search strategy within a run. Across runs, trajectories are distilled into open-source LLMs as role-conditioned priors for future proposal generation, optionally via LoRA for smaller models like Qwen3-4B.

Result: On the LLM-SRBench benchmark across the four LSR-Synth scientific domains, A-SR boosts Acc@0.01 from 25.79% to 48.30% when using Llama3.1-8B. A LoRA-based variant, A-SR-LoRA, improves Qwen3-4B’s Acc@0.01 from 24.58% to 38.29%. On four real-world scientific discovery problems, A-SR achieves the best in-distribution or out-of-distribution normalized MSE on 7 out of 8 evaluated metrics, outperforming prior LLM-based symbolic regression approaches.

Conclusion: By reframing symbolic regression as a coordinated, role-based agentic process, A-SR can better exploit heterogeneous feedback signals and search histories, improving both search efficiency and final equation quality. The framework adapts online without changing base LLM weights and can further self-improve across runs by distilling trajectories into role-conditioned proposal priors, leading to consistent gains over baselines on both synthetic benchmarks and real scientific tasks.

Abstract: Symbolic regression aims to discover closed-form equations from data, but existing LLM-guided methods often rely on a unified proposal loop that compresses heterogeneous search failures into a scalar score and a single prompt. We propose A-SR, a self-evolving agentic framework that shifts the control unit from expression edits to role-conditioned evidence views. A-SR coordinates formula discovery through routing among coordination protocols, an online evaluator-reward role policy, and state-routed process memory. During search, evaluator feedback characterizes reliability and productivity, updates role-level utilities, and routes elite motifs, failure traces, and validity diagnostics to different agents. The framework self-evolves at two timescales: within a run, it adapts the search process without updating LLM parameters; across runs, recorded trajectories can be distilled into open-source LLMs as role-conditioned proposal priors. Averaged over the four LSR-Synth scientific domains in LLM-SRBench, A-SR improves Acc@0.01 over baselines from 25.79% to 48.30% with Llama3.1-8B, while A-SR-LoRA improves the corresponding Qwen3-4B result from 24.58% to 38.29%. On four real-world scientific discovery tasks, A-SR obtains the best in-distribution or out-of-distribution normalized mean squared error on 7 of 8 reported metrics.

</details>


### [57] [Evaluation Pitfalls and Sparsity Limitations in LLM-based Confidence Estimates for Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04899)
*Elena Merdjanovska,Omar Zaidan,Andreas Rücklé*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper studies confidence estimation for LLM-based classification and proposes an improved method to densify and better evaluate confidence outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used for classification, but their confidence scores, often obtained via verbalization, are extremely sparse and clustered (e.g., many 95% scores). This sparsity both reduces practical usefulness and distorts evaluation metrics such as AUARC depending on interpolation choice, making method comparison unreliable.

Method: 1) Empirically analyze confidence verbalization behavior of LLMs on multiple datasets, documenting sparsity patterns. 2) Show how different AUARC interpolation schemes (stepwise vs linear) can reverse rankings between methods like consistency sampling. 3) Argue for and standardize stepwise interpolation as the fairer choice given discrete confidence outputs. 4) Propose "verbalization logprobs": compute confidence by weighting the digits of verbalized numerical confidences with their token probabilities, which produces denser confidence values without extra inference calls.

Result: They observe extreme sparsity in verbalized confidences (e.g., only eight unique values, many exactly 95%) across four datasets and two LLMs. They demonstrate that AUARC rankings are highly sensitive to interpolation, with consistency sampling going from best to worst when switching from linear to stepwise interpolation. Their proposed verbalization logprobs significantly reduces sparsity and achieves the best AUARC, improving by 2.3 points over standard verbalization without additional inference cost.

Conclusion: Confidence verbalization from LLMs is highly sparse, undermining both practical reliability estimation and fair evaluation. AUARC computation must use stepwise interpolation to avoid misleading method rankings. A simple adjustment that weights verbalized digits by their token log-probabilities (verbalization logprobs) mitigates sparsity and yields superior confidence estimation performance at no extra inference cost.

Abstract: Confidence estimation is essential when LLMs are used for classification, indicating when predictions can be trusted. However, common approaches such as verbalization produce extremely sparse outputs. For instance, Qwen3-32B verbalizes only eight unique confidence values on SST-2, with over half being exactly 95%, a pattern we observe consistently across four datasets and two LLMs. Besides limiting practical utility, we show that this sparsity critically affects evaluation: the choice of interpolation in area under the accuracy-rejection curve (AUARC) dramatically alters rankings, with consistency sampling dropping from best to worst under stepwise versus linear interpolation. We advocate for standardizing stepwise interpolation for a fairer comparison. Under such a fair evaluation, we find that weighting verbalized digits by token probabilities, a method we term verbalization logprobs, addresses sparsity and achieves the best AUARC (+2.3 points over vanilla verbalization) without incurring additional inference cost.

</details>


### [58] [Strengthening Target-Language Features: SAE-Based Steering for Multilingual Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04904)
*Hongsheng Wang,Phlipp Koehn*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They boost low-resource language performance in multilingual LLMs at inference time by steering with sparse autoencoder features, without retraining the model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilingual LLMs perform unevenly across languages, and current adaptation approaches need extra training, parameter updates, and substantial multilingual data. A lighter-weight, training-free method to improve specific target languages at inference is desirable.

Method: Train (or reuse) sparse autoencoders on model activations, then use multilingual parallel sentences to compare SAE feature activations across languages and identify a small set of layer-specific features correlated with each target language. Decode these features into steering vectors and add them to the model’s hidden states at inference as a language-specific control signal, without changing model weights.

Result: Applying this inference-time steering to Gemma-3-12B-it yields measurable improvements on multilingual benchmarks: +10.9 accuracy points on XCOPA, +5.3 on XNLI, and +1.9 on MGSM on average for the targeted languages.

Conclusion: Language-specific concepts are partially disentangled in sparse autoencoder features and can be exploited as steering directions. Injecting these directions at inference can significantly and cheaply enhance multilingual performance without fine-tuning or extra parameters.

Abstract: Multilingual large language models exhibit substantial performance differences across languages, while existing adaptation methods often require parameter updates and considerable multilingual training data. We propose an inference-time multilingual steering method that uses pretrained sparse autoencoders to identify and strengthen target-language-related features. Using multilingual parallel sentences, we compare SAE activations across languages and select a small number of layer-specific features associated with each target language. These features are decoded into steering signals and injected into the model's hidden states without additional training. Experiments with Gemma-3-12B-it show average accuracy improvements of 10.9 percentage points on XCOPA, 5.3 points on XNLI, and 1.9 points on MGSM.

</details>


### [59] [Does Out-of-Sight Equal Out-of-Mind in CoT Monitorability?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04928)
*Pedro Ferreira,Wilker Aziz,Ivan Titov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how well we can still monitor model behavior when explicit chain-of-thought traces are replaced with latent (non-textual) reasoning, using hint-exploitation as a testbed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explicit chain-of-thought reasoning makes it easy to inspect and monitor a model's decision process, but it is expensive at inference time and can leak sensitive information. Latent CoT methods reduce cost by compressing reasoning into continuous vectors, but this removes the human-readable trace that monitoring tools rely on. It is unclear whether alternative monitoring techniques—like probing internal activations or re-verbalizing latent states—can provide comparable insight into whether the model is behaving undesirably (e.g., secretly using biased hints).

Method: They set up a hint-based intervention paradigm, where inputs contain subtle biasing cues (hints) that a model could exploit without admitting it. They treat reliance on these hints as the behavior to be monitored. They then compare different monitoring strategies across several reasoning modes: standard explicit CoT, weakly-supervised latent CoT, and strongly-supervised latent CoT. The experiments are run on math reasoning and QA tasks. Monitors vary in how much access they have to model internals (e.g., activations, latent states) and in whether they must rely on verbalized reasoning or can operate directly on internal representations.

Result: In the tasks studied, the success of monitoring—detecting when the model relies on hints—depends more on (1) properties of the task, such as how tightly the correct answer constrains the underlying reasoning, and (2) how much internal access the monitor has, than on whether the model uses explicit or latent CoT.
