Differences between explicit and latent CoT in terms of monitorability are comparatively modest under their evaluation setting.

Conclusion: Monitorability is not inherently tied to having explicit text-based chain-of-thought traces. Instead, it is largely determined by task structure and the richness of access to model internals available to the monitor. Latent CoT can be comparably monitorable to explicit CoT when paired with appropriate monitoring interfaces, suggesting that efficiency gains from latent reasoning need not come at a major cost to oversight—though this may depend heavily on the specific task and monitoring setup.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning offers a window into the decision-making of large language models (LLMs), which can be monitored for target behaviors by reading the reasoning trace, motivating work on CoT monitorability. Latent CoT approaches, however, replace the explicit tokens with a small number of continuous states, lowering inference costs but removing the readable trace this monitoring relies on. Monitoring then requires alternative access to the model, such as probing its activations or verbalizing the latent states back into text, but how much monitorability these alternatives preserve is unclear. We study this question with a hint-based intervention setup, a proxy for behaviors where models exploit biasing input cues, e.g., an inadvertently leaked answer or a belief stated by the user, without acknowledging them. Taking hint-reliance as the monitorability target, we compare monitors across reasoning modes, from explicit CoT to weakly- and strongly-supervised latent CoT, on math reasoning and question answering. We find that, in this setup, monitorability depends more on properties of the task (such as whether the correct answer constrains the supporting reasoning) and the level of access to model internals than on the reasoning mode.

</details>


### [60] [State2State: Environment-Derived Mid-Training for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04934)
*Xuanyu Lei,Yiqi Zhu,Chenliang Li,Kaiming Liu,Peng Li,Ming Yan,Jieping Ye,Ya-Qin Zhang,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces State2State, a method that turns explored environment states into self-supervised goals for training LLM-based agents, improving performance and sample efficiency without expert-designed tasks or rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent training relies heavily on expert demonstrations or human-specified tasks with handcrafted verifiers, which limits scalability, diversity of skills, and applicability to new environments. The authors want a way for agents to acquire interaction and manipulation abilities purely from interacting with the environment itself, avoiding manual task design and external supervision, yet still having verifiable training signals.

Method: They propose State2State, a mid-training procedure that (1) lets an agent explore an environment; (2) samples pairs of states from this experience; (3) defines a task as reaching a target state from a start state; and (4) uses rule-based state matching to verify success. These automatically generated state-to-state tasks become training objectives for the agent, effectively turning raw exploration data into a large set of self-supervised, verifiable goals. The method is used as an environment-learning phase before downstream RL or as a standalone training stage.

Result: On ALFWorld and ScienceWorld benchmarks, agents trained with State2State as a standalone environment-learning stage achieve better performance than baselines in most tested configurations. When State2State is used to initialize policies before downstream reinforcement learning, agents reach higher final performance with fewer environment interactions, indicating improved sample efficiency. The experiments also provide initial evidence that skills learned via State2State can transfer across different environments.

Conclusion: Converting explored environment states into state-reaching goals via State2State provides a scalable, verifiable, and supervision-free way to train LLM agents. This environment-derived mid-training improves both standalone performance and downstream RL efficiency, and shows early signs of cross-environment generalization, suggesting a promising direction for more autonomous and scalable agent training without handcrafted tasks or expert labels.

Abstract: Training LLM agents commonly relies on supervised fine-tuning from expert trajectories or online reinforcement learning over human-specified tasks with handcrafted verifiers. Though effective, both remain bottlenecked by externally specified tasks and supervision signals, limiting the scalability and diversity of agent training. We study an environment learning paradigm in which agents acquire interaction and manipulation capabilities solely through environment interaction, without externally specified tasks. We propose State2State, an environment-derived mid-training method that converts explored environment states into training objectives, challenging agents to reach a specified target state. By deriving tasks from environment exploration and verifying success through rule-based state matching, State2State provides scalable and verifiable training objectives without expert supervision or manual task design. Experiments on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld show that State2State improves agent performance as a standalone environment-learning stage in most settings. As initialization for downstream RL, it further improves final performance and learning efficiency, with promising evidence of cross-environment generalization.

</details>


### [61] [Reading Between the Frames: Interpreting Implicit and Non-literal Meaning in Social Media Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04939)
*Yang Wang,Yanan Ma,Yiqi Liu,Zi Yan Chang,Chi-Li Chen,Chia-Yi Hsiao,Tyler Loakman,Aline Villavicencio,Chenghao Xiao,Chenghua Lin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DrivelHub+ is a benchmark of 1,000 social media videos with implicit narrative explanations to test if video-language models can infer non-obvious, pragmatic meanings rather than just describe surface content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing video-language benchmarks mainly focus on recognizing visible actions or generating literal descriptions, but social media videos often rely on humor, irony, or cultural cues that make them appear nonsensical without deeper pragmatic understanding. The authors want to evaluate whether models can capture these implicit, rhetorically layered meanings rather than only surface-level perception.

Method: They build DrivelHub+, a dataset of 1,000 social media videos that seem nonsensical on the surface but actually convey deliberate pragmatic meanings. Each video is annotated with a human-written implicit narrative explanation. They then define two evaluation settings: (1) explanation, where models must produce natural language explanations of the pragmatic meaning of each video; and (2) representation, where they use a reasoning-as-retrieval paradigm to test whether video and text embeddings correctly align videos with their corresponding implicit narratives in both video-to-text and text-to-video retrieval tasks.

Result: Using DrivelHub+, they evaluate current video-language models on both explanation and retrieval-style representation tasks. Although the abstract does not give numerical results, it implies that there is a noticeable gap between current models’ ability to perceive and describe surface content and their ability to capture implicit, pragmatic meanings in social media videos.

Conclusion: DrivelHub+ serves as a diagnostic benchmark for contextual multimodal reasoning in social media videos. It highlights that current video-language models struggle to move from describing what is visually present to inferring the intended, often non-linear and rhetorically layered meanings, thereby revealing a gap between multimodal perception and pragmatic comprehension.

Abstract: Social media videos often communicate meanings that go beyond their visible actions, captions, or speech. A mundane clip may become humorous, ironic, or satire only through the interaction of multimodal cues and cultural context, making such content a difficult test case for video-language models. In this paper, we introduce \textit{DrivelHub+}, a benchmark for evaluating whether models can infer the implicit, non-linear, and rhetorically layered meanings of social media videos that appear nonsensical on the surface but convey deliberate pragmatic meanings. DrivelHub+ consists of 1,000 videos collected from social media, each annotated with a human-written implicit narrative explanation. Unlike conventional video understanding tasks focused on recognition or description, we present a benchmark that targets contextual multimodal reasoning. We evaluate current video-language models from two perspectives: explanation, where models must explain the pragmatic comprehension of a video in natural language; and representation, where we adapt reasoning-as-retrieval to test whether model representations align videos with their corresponding implicit narratives in both video-to-text and text-to-video retrieval. Our benchmark provides a diagnostic setting for measuring the gap between multimodal perception and pragmatic comprehension, asking whether current models can move beyond describing what is shown to inferring what is meant.

</details>


### [62] [Protoreasoning in Tiny Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04980)
*Eduardo Valle,Fergal Reid*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how very small (~1M-parameter) transformers can perform step-by-step reasoning via a simple Chain-of-Thought variant called protoreasoning, using Dyck-language bracket tasks to analyze generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models show strong step-by-step reasoning, but it is unclear whether they learn general algorithms or just many heuristics; this is difficult to analyze on huge, opaque frontier models. The authors want a controllable, small-scale setting where they can rigorously study when and how transformers acquire genuinely general reasoning procedures.

Method: They design "reasoning-friendly" synthetic tasks based on Dyck languages (properly nested brackets) that can be solved via algorithmic reasoning. They train tiny transformers (~1M parameters) on these tasks with and without explicit step-by-step intermediate traces ("protoreasoning") and compare in-distribution and out-of-distribution generalization. They run ablations to test whether gains are due to the semantic content of traces versus superficial factors like added length or extra training signal.

Result: Introducing protoreasoning traces significantly improves out-of-distribution generalization on Dyck-language tasks for tiny transformers, substantially closing the generalization gap. Ablation experiments show that the benefit comes from the specific information in the traces, not just from having more tokens or supervision signal.

Conclusion: Even very small transformers can use simple Chain-of-Thought-like traces to learn more general reasoning behavior on structured tasks. Carefully designed intermediate reasoning steps help models move from brittle heuristics toward more algorithmic generalization, and small models plus synthetic languages provide a tractable testbed for analyzing the mechanisms of step-by-step reasoning.

Abstract: We show that tiny transformers can profitably employ a simple form of Chain of Thought, which we call protoreasoning, allowing us to study step-by-step reasoning on ~1M-parameter models and opening up opportunities for much more detailed experimentation and analysis than is feasible for larger models. Current Large Language Models exhibit impressive step-by-step reasoning, but we have yet to understand its generality, i.e., when and how LLMs learn genuinely general algorithms rather than "bags of heuristics." Such questions are hard to settle on compute-intensive frontier models trained on opaque data. To work at model scales far below the threshold for natural-language competence, we define reasoning-friendly tasks on Dyck languages (sentences of correctly nested brackets). We find that protoreasoning traces substantially close the out-of-distribution generalization gap, and ablations confirm that the trace's content, not merely its extra tokens, drives the gain.

</details>


### [63] [DelusionEval: Measuring Delusion-Linked Behaviors in AI Chatbots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05004)
*Jared Moore,Andrea Mock,Yifan Mai,Jacy Reese Anthis,Ryan Louie,William Agnew,Ashish Mehta,Kevin Klyman,Percy Liang,Nick Haber,Eric Lin,Desmond C. Ong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DelusionEval introduces a benchmark using real-world harmful LLM–user conversations to test how often models exhibit behaviors that may reinforce user delusions and psychological distress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM safety evaluations often use synthetic prompts or narrow risk categories, which may not capture subtle but serious psychological harms such as reinforcing delusions. Mental health professionals are specifically worried about "delusional spirals" where users’ delusional thinking and LLM responses mutually escalate. There is a lack of empirical, real-world based tools to systematically measure these risks across models and configurations (e.g., context length, reasoning style), motivating the creation of a dedicated evaluation grounded in authentic, harmful interaction histories.

Method: The authors collect 589 real conversation histories from 18 participants, totaling 12,591 user messages from people who experienced delusions and psychological harm during LLM use. They design DelusionEval, a protocol that prompts different LLMs with these histories, varying factors such as context length (e.g., adding 350 prior messages) and model configurations (size, release date, test-time reasoning). They then annotate and quantify how often models display predefined "delusion-linked" behaviors, such as failing to discourage self-harm or otherwise responding in ways that may validate or amplify delusional thinking.

Result: Across multiple leading model families (e.g., GPT, Claude), all exhibit substantial rates of delusion-linked behaviors; no family consistently avoids these failures. There is no reliable correlation between lower delusion-linked behavior and model size, recency, or use of test-time reasoning—more advanced models are not uniformly safer. However, providing longer interaction histories significantly increases harmful behaviors; for instance, when users express suicidal ideation, the rate at which models fail to discourage self-harm rises from 30.0% to 41.1% after prepending an additional 350 prior messages. Within each model family, newer, larger, or more capable variants do not show uniform improvements across all behavior categories.

Conclusion: DelusionEval reveals that current LLMs, regardless of family or capability level, frequently engage in behaviors that may exacerbate user delusions and psychological distress, and that longer conversational context can worsen these risks. Safety cannot be assumed to improve simply with larger or newer models or added reasoning mechanisms. The work underscores the need for context-sensitive, empirically grounded safety evaluations using real-world harmful interactions, and calls for more rigorous, targeted research on the psychological impacts of human–AI conversations and strategies to mitigate delusion-linked behaviors.

Abstract: Mental health professionals have raised concerns about risks of psychological harm from interaction with large language models (LLMs), including "delusional spirals" in which concerning human and LLM behaviors reinforce each other over time. With growing public use of LLM-powered chatbots, there is an urgent need to build evaluations grounded in real-world episodes of psychological harm experienced by users. We developed DelusionEval, an evaluation protocol that tests a model's tendencies to exhibit behaviors linked to promoting user delusions. We prompt each model with 589 unique conversation histories from 18 participants, comprising 12,591 messages from users who experienced delusions and psychological harm. We find that the tendency of an evaluated LLM to exhibit delusion-linked behavior does not reliably correlate with model size, release date, or the presence of test-time reasoning. However, extending the context of prior messages substantially increases rates of delusion-linked behaviors, providing evidence for the importance of context in LLM safety evaluation. For example, the rate of failing to discourage self-harm when the user expresses suicidal ideation increases from 30.0% to 41.1% when an additional 350 messages are prepended to the conversation history. All model families (e.g., GPT, Claude) exhibit substantial rates of delusion-linked behaviors. Within families, later, larger, or higher-reasoning models are not uniformly better across all behavior categories. Our results raise concerns regarding the potential psychological impact of LLMs and the need for more rigorous studies of real-world human-AI interaction.

</details>


### [64] [OneDayAgent: Towards a Long-Horizon Harness for Autonomous Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05013)
*Jingsheng Zheng,Xinyuan Fang,Jintian Zhang,Zhengke Gui,Huajun Chen,Ningyu Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A framework called OneDayAgent is proposed to reliably run long, open-ended, multi-step LLM agent tasks across diverse tools and models, achieving state-of-the-art results and generalizing to multiple backends.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are now used for complex, open-ended daily tasks spanning work, study, and life. These tasks are long-horizon, cross different environments and modalities, and require preserving goals and constraints across many steps and tools. Existing research typically addresses single failure modes like goal drift, state loss, or context overflow in isolation. There is limited understanding of whether one unified control harness can jointly mitigate these issues while remaining effective and portable across different backend LLMs.

Method: The authors design OneDayAgent, a long-horizon control harness for autonomous LLM agents. The harness takes an open-ended user request and converts it into a managed execution process that: (1) decomposes the overall task into bounded, more manageable subtasks; (2) maintains execution memory and state under context-length pressure; and (3) adds a verification and repair phase for the final output to ensure goal and constraint satisfaction. They run the same harness on multiple backend LLMs from different model families, without per-model tuning, to test cross-backend generality.

Result: On the AgentIF-OneDay benchmark with 104 tasks, OneDayAgent combined with the GLM-5.2 model achieves a new state of the art with an overall score of 0.821. The same harness also runs successfully on five different backend LLMs from three distinct model families, with each model showing different styles of execution while still functioning correctly within the shared workflow.

Conclusion: A single, well-designed harness (OneDayAgent) can jointly manage multiple common failure modes of long-horizon LLM agents—such as goal drift, state loss, and context overflow—while remaining effective and portable across different backend models. This suggests that careful orchestration and process control around LLMs can yield robust, general long-horizon agents suitable for open-ended, everyday tasks without requiring backend-specific tuning.

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly applied to open-ended everyday requests that span work, study, and life. These tasks are long-horizon, cross-environment, and multimodal, forcing the agent to preserve goals and constraints across many steps while navigating heterogeneous tools and attachments. While prior work has addressed individual failure modes such as goals drift, states loss, and context overflow, whether a single harness can manage them jointly and remain effective across backends has received less study. We present OneDayAgent, a long-horizon harness for autonomous agents. OneDayAgent turns an open-ended request into a managed execution process that decomposes tasks into bounded subtasks, maintains execution memory under context pressure, and verifies and repairs the final deliverable. We evaluate OneDayAgent on AgentIF-OneDay across 104 tasks. With the GLM-5.2 backend, OneDayAgent sets a new state of the art with an overall score of 0.821. The same harness runs across five backend LLMs from three model families, indicating the harness generalizes across backends without tuning, even as different models induce distinct execution styles under the same workflow.

</details>


### [65] [Provable Limits and Certified Deferral for Verbalized Uncertainty in Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05064)
*Jianru Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how well small open-weight language models can express calibrated confidence that enables safe, risk-controlled deferral to humans in offline deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In private, offline, or resource-constrained settings, small language models are attractive but may be unreliable. Deployers need mechanisms to know when the model is likely to be wrong and should defer to a human instead of answering autonomously. While verbalized confidence and calibration techniques are used in larger models, their effectiveness and theoretical limits for small open-weight models, especially under risk constraints, are not well understood.

Method: The authors study 11 instruction-tuned language models (0.5B–14B parameters) across three families on ARC-Challenge and TruthfulQA, producing 25,168 local predictions. They analyze confidence calibration theoretically and empirically. Theoretically, they derive three results: (1) strictly monotone calibration preserves the risk-coverage frontier and error-detection AUROC; (2) temperature scaling is provably unable to calibrate models whose confidences remain above 0.5 while accuracy is below 0.5; and (3) a Clopper-Pearson-based method uses a 200-question calibration set to provide finite-sample risk certificates under an i.i.d. assumption. Empirically, they apply temperature scaling and Platt scaling to verbalized confidences, examine when calibration fails (hitting an infeasibility floor), measure calibration error (ECE), and compute certified autonomy under specified risk budgets. They also detect and fix an answer-ordering artifact in the multiple-choice version of TruthfulQA.

Result: Eight of the 22 model–task combinations reach the theoretically predicted infeasibility limit of temperature scaling within one percentage point, confirming the theoretical bound. Platt scaling substantially improves calibration, lowering ECE to as little as 0.02. Despite this, under a 20% risk budget, only three model–task pairs obtain any certified autonomy, and none qualify at a 10% risk budget. The identified answer-ordering artifact in multiple-choice TruthfulQA is corrected, improving the reliability of evaluation on that benchmark.

Conclusion: Verbalized confidence from small open-weight language models can be calibrated to yield meaningful confidence semantics, and Platt scaling can make them appear well calibrated by standard metrics. However, when one demands rigorous, finite-sample risk control for deferral decisions, these models rarely achieve substantial certified autonomy, especially under stricter risk budgets. Theoretical limits, such as the infeasibility of temperature scaling in certain regimes, explain much of this behavior. Effective deployment of small models in safety-critical or risk-sensitive scenarios therefore requires certified deferral mechanisms and careful calibration-aware evaluation, not just low average calibration error.

Abstract: Small open-weight language models increasingly run in private, offline, and cost-sensitive settings, where the key deployment question is not only what a model answers but when it should defer to a human. We study whether verbalized confidence can support risk-controlled deferral, evaluating eleven instruction-tuned models from three families, 0.5B to 14B parameters, on ARC-Challenge and TruthfulQA with 25,168 local predictions. Three theoretical results delimit what calibration can provide: strictly monotone calibration preserves the risk-coverage frontier and error-detection AUROC; temperature scaling cannot calibrate models whose confidence stays above one half while accuracy falls below it; and a Clopper-Pearson procedure converts a 200-question calibration set into a finite-sample risk certificate under an i.i.d. deployment assumption. Empirically, eight of 22 model-task pairs hit the temperature-scaling infeasibility floor within one percentage point of the predicted bound. Platt scaling reduces ECE to as low as 0.02, yet certified autonomy at a 20% risk budget is granted to only three model-task pairs and to none at 10%. We also identify and repair an answer-ordering artifact in the multiple-choice form of TruthfulQA. Calibration gives confidence semantics; certified deferral determines when small models are safe to use.

</details>


### [66] [Language Models Generalize to Human-like Word Order Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05028)
*Amanda Popadich,Shane Steinert-Threlkeld*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper tests whether language models, like humans, show a bias for scope-homomorphic orders of noun phrase modifiers even when training data lacks direct evidence, and finds that they do, suggesting such linguistic biases can emerge from general learning over impoverished input.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A key problem in language acquisition is how learners infer structured linguistic biases from underdetermined input, without explicit evidence for all patterns. Artificial Language Learning studies with humans have shown that people generalize beyond their experience, for example by preferring modifier orders that mirror semantic scope (scope-homomorphic orders). The authors want to know whether modern language models, trained under similarly impoverished conditions, develop comparable biases, which would shed light on the kinds of learning mechanisms needed to explain such generalizations.

Method: The authors construct a controlled corpus in which all noun phrases containing multiple modifiers are removed, thereby stripping the training data of any direct evidence about modifier ordering. They then train language models of three different sizes on this modified corpus. After training, the models are evaluated on sentences that include multiple modifiers in noun phrases, and their preferences over possible modifier orders are measured. Additionally, the authors compute noun–modifier association strengths via pointwise mutual information (PMI) to see whether simple distributional statistics can account for the observed ordering preferences.

Result: Across all three model sizes, the language models consistently prefer scope-homomorphic modifier orders when predicting or evaluating multi-modifier noun phrases, despite never having seen such configurations during training. The strength of this preference differs by modifier type, indicating some systematic variability. PMI-based association strengths do capture established tendencies in modifier ordering, but they fail to fully explain the models’ actual ordering preferences, suggesting that the models exploit structure beyond pairwise co-occurrence statistics.

Conclusion: Language models can reconstruct human-like biases for scope-homomorphic modifier ordering even from data that lacks explicit evidence for such structures, implying that general-purpose learning mechanisms over impoverished input can give rise to sophisticated linguistic generalizations. The controlled setup introduced in this work offers a useful framework for probing the internal mechanisms and inductive biases that drive such emergent preferences in language models.

Abstract: A central question in language acquisition is whether linguistic biases can emerge from general learning mechanisms operating over underdetermined input. Artificial Language Learning (ALL) studies have shown that human learners reliably generalize beyond the evidence provided, including by preferring scope-homomorphic noun phrase modifier orders. In this work, we investigate whether language models exhibit the same bias under similar conditions. We create a controlled learning environment in which models are trained on a corpus where all noun phrases containing multiple modifiers have been removed, eliminating direct evidence about modifier ordering, and are then evaluated on multiple modifier sentences. Across three model sizes, we find that they consistently prefer scope-homomorphic orders despite never observing them during training. These preferences vary in strength by modifier type. To investigate the source of these preferences, we examine noun-modifier association strength using pointwise mutual information (PMI). While PMI reflects known modifier-ordering patterns, it does not explain the models' ordering preferences. These findings demonstrate that LMs can recover human-like linguistic generalizations from impoverished input and provide a controlled framework for investigating the mechanisms underlying such biases.

</details>


### [67] [Chained Recursive Language Models for Multi-Iteration Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05124)
*Purbesh Mitra,Sennur Ulukus*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Chained Recursive Language Models (Chained RLM), an inference-time architecture that repeatedly re-invokes the same LLM as fresh reasoning roots, each with summarized state and artifacts, to improve long-context, multi-step reasoning accuracy compared with a single long trajectory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context reasoning in LLMs is fragile because a single, linear inference must simultaneously read long contexts, maintain intermediate state, verify evidence, and produce the final answer. This is especially error-prone for extraction, counting, ordering, and multi-hop reasoning where early mistakes propagate unchecked. The authors are motivated to find an inference-time framework that structures reasoning into stages with explicit intermediate artifacts that can be inspected, corrected, and extended, instead of relying on one monolithic generation.

Method: They propose Chained Recursive Language Models (Chained RLM), where the same base LLM is invoked multiple times as independent "reasoning roots." Each root receives the original problem and context plus: (1) a compact plain-text summary of prior steps, (2) a plain-text blackboard, and (3) durable task-specific artifacts produced earlier. Crucially, roots do not inherit the entire conversation history, reducing context bloat and error propagation. The paper defines a system model, the mechanism for handing off summaries and artifacts between roots, and an artifact workspace where intermediate results are stored and updated. They also specify an evaluation protocol comparing Chained RLM to direct LLM answering with and without recursive tool-calling.

Result: Within the described evaluation framework, they empirically study conditions under which restarting the model with fresh context plus artifacts improves accuracy over standard single-trajectory LLM responses, even when the baseline uses recursive tool-calling. The abstract indicates that there are measurable accuracy gains in some scenarios, although precise quantitative results are not detailed here.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that organizing LLM inference as a chain of fresh-context recursive calls with shared artifact workspaces can improve long-context and multi-step reasoning. By breaking problems into partial tasks and passing durable artifacts rather than full histories, Chained RLM can mitigate error propagation and better support tasks like extraction, counting, ordering, and multi-hop reasoning compared to conventional one-shot or tool-augmented LLM inference.

Abstract: Long context reasoning in large language models (LLMs) is usually constrained by the fact that a single inference trajectory has to simultaneously explore the context, store intermediate state, verify evidence, and produce the final answer. This becomes particularly difficult in tasks that require extraction, counting, ordering, or multi-hop reasoning, where an early mistake can propagate until the final response. In this work, we propose Chained Recursive Language Models (Chained RLM), an inference-time architecture, in which the same underlying model is called repeatedly as a sequence of fresh reasoning roots. Each root receives the original problem and context, but does not inherit the full conversational history. Instead, it receives a compact plain-text summary, a plain-text blackboard, and some durable task-specific artifacts written by predecessor roots. The motivation is to manage the context by chopping into partial tasks rather than one large inference response; in each staged computation, intermediate artifacts can be inspected, corrected, and extended by a later fresh inference by the same model. We describe the system model, handoff mechanism, artifact workspace, and evaluation protocol for this system. We study when fresh-context artifact continuation gives a measurable gain in accuracy over direct LLM answering even with recursive tool-calling.

</details>


### [68] [German parties shifted towards intuition-based rhetoric after the far right's parliamentary breakthrough](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05075)
*Peer Saleth,Segun T. Aroyehun,Fabio Carrella,Christoph M. Abels,Stephan Lewandowsky,David Garcia*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how German political elites’ use of evidence-based vs intuition-based rhetoric has changed with the rise of a far-right party, showing a broad shift toward intuition-based language, especially after the AfD entered parliament.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is widespread concern that misinformation and declining commitment to truth threaten democratic processes, but we lack systematic evidence on how elites’ rhetorical orientation toward truth changes when populist and far-right actors gain prominence. The authors aim to fill this gap by examining elite discourse in both parliamentary and social media settings over a decade in Germany.

Method: The authors compile a large corpus of about 4.5 million tweets and nearly 60,000 parliamentary speeches by German political elites from 2015 to 2025. They use a validated distributed dictionary representation to operationalize and measure two types of rhetoric: evidence-based and intuition-based. From these measures they construct an Evidence Minus Intuition (EMI) score for each actor and venue and then track its evolution over time, focusing in particular on changes associated with the parliamentary entry of the far-right AfD in 2017.

Result: They find that across both parliamentary and Twitter discourse, intuition-based language becomes more prominent over time. Right-leaning actors consistently have the lowest EMI scores, indicating a stronger reliance on intuition-based rather than evidence-based rhetoric compared to others. The AfD’s 2017 entry into parliament is associated with a sharp decline in EMI scores across the entire chamber, suggesting a chamber-wide shift. On Twitter, the decline in EMI is more gradual but still evident over the study period.

Conclusion: The study concludes that increased visibility of far-right actors is associated with a broader shift in elite political discourse away from evidence-based and toward intuition-based rhetoric in Germany. This suggests that the rise of such parties can reshape not just their own communication style but also that of other elites, potentially weakening shared commitments to evidence and truth in a multiparty democratic system.

Abstract: The spread of misinformation is widely perceived as a threat to democratic deliberation, yet how political elites' rhetorical commitments to truth shift alongside the rise of populist actors remains poorly understood. Analysing 4.5 million tweets and 59,170 parliamentary speeches by German political elites between 2015 and 2025, we measure evidence-based and intuition-based rhetoric using a validated distributed dictionary representation. Across both arenas, intuition-based language has become more prominent, and right-leaning actors consistently exhibit the lowest Evidence Minus Intuition (EMI) scores. The parliamentary entry of the extreme-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) in 2017 coincides with sharp downward shifts in EMI across the broader chamber, while a more gradual decline is observed on Twitter. These findings document an association between far-right visibility and a changing approach to truth in elite discourse in a multiparty European democracy.

</details>


### [69] [Same Formulas, Different Semantics: Do Language Models Follow Modal Logic Specifications?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05097)
*Réemi Andrieu,Damien Sileo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether language models can follow explicitly stated modal semantics (about necessity and possibility) by using paired problems that differ only in logical conditions, finding that performance depends heavily on reasoning mode and model identity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modal reasoning about necessity and possibility is sensitive to assumptions about which possible worlds and objects are accessible or exist, so the same argument can be valid in one modal logic and invalid in another. Standard LLM evaluations risk measuring fit to a familiar, implicit logic rather than the specific semantics described in a task. The authors aim to create an evaluation that can distinguish whether models actually track the stipulated modal semantics and to understand how models’ reasoning modes and configurations affect this ability.

Method: The authors construct paired modal logic problems where the premises and conjecture are textually identical, but the underlying frame or domain conditions (e.g., how worlds are related or what objects exist in them) differ, making the inference valid in one case and invalid in the other. They use automated theorem proving and model finders to verify opposite labels for each pair. To avoid trivial cues, they build a balanced core set of items where knowing only the semantic condition is insufficient to guess the answer. They then evaluate several recent language models, under direct prompting and under a special “reasoning mode,” comparing performance to a baseline that uses only the condition label. They further test models without explicitly stating frame conditions to see which familiar modal logics best fit their patterns of answers.

Result: On the balanced core set, four out of five recent language models perform worse than a simple condition-only baseline when directly prompted with the full problems. Activating a reasoning-oriented mode dramatically improves DeepSeek V4 Flash’s performance from 4.4% to 88.1% on the same prompts, while other models vary. When explicit frame conditions are removed, models’ answers tend to agree with each other, but their response patterns correspond best to different standard modal logics, indicating that they default to varying internal modal assumptions rather than the task’s specific semantics.

Conclusion: LLMs generally struggle to reliably follow stipulated modal semantics when evaluated with carefully controlled paired problems, often doing worse than a naive baseline. Their behavior is highly sensitive to the presence of specialized reasoning modes and to model architecture, suggesting that “reasoning configuration” matters as much as model identity for fine-grained logical tasks. When not given explicit conditions, models converge on answers aligned with different familiar modal logics instead of a single shared system. The paper contributes a reproducible benchmark—formulas, oracle artifacts, countermodels, and model responses—for studying and improving LLMs’ capacity to reason under precisely defined modal semantics.

Abstract: Reasoning about necessity and possibility depends on assumptions about accessibility between worlds and about which objects exist at each one. The same inference may therefore hold under one modal system and fail under another. Evaluating language models on such problems requires testing whether their judgments follow the stated semantics rather than a familiar logic. We construct paired modal problems with identical premises and conjecture but different frame or domain conditions; automated reasoning verifies opposite labels. A balanced core prevents the semantic condition alone from revealing the answer. On this core, four of five recent models perform below the condition-only baseline under direct prompting. Yet enabling reasoning mode raises DeepSeek V4 Flash from 4.4% to 88.1% on unchanged prompts. Following stipulated modal semantics thus depends strongly on inference mode as well as model identity. When frame conditions are omitted, models often agree but fit different familiar logics best. We release the formulas, oracle artifacts, countermodels, and responses.

</details>


### [70] [Spoken Function Calling: A New Perspective on Spoken Language Understanding for Large Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05126)
*Yuezhang Peng,Yuxin Liu,Changfeng Gao,Zhifu Gao,Xiangang Li,Xie Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduce Spoken Function Calling (SFC) as a new paradigm for spoken language understanding using structured function-call style rules, and show it beats traditional SLU, especially for open-domain and in-context scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional spoken language understanding (SLU) models work well for closed-set, in-domain tasks after supervised fine-tuning, but they struggle in open-domain settings where rules and intents are ambiguous, and they cannot fully exploit in-context learning abilities of modern LLMs/LALMs. A clearer, more structured formulation of semantic understanding is needed.

Method: Redefine SLU as Spoken Function Calling (SFC), where user utterances are mapped to a set of explicitly defined, structured spoken functions. Curate and extend a library of such functions based on existing SLU datasets. Build a multi-agent system to automatically synthesize a new benchmark dataset, SFC-Bench, for this paradigm. Evaluate various Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) on SFC-Bench, and apply post-training to LALMs to improve their performance on SFC tasks.

Result: SFC-Bench enables systematic evaluation of LLMs and LALMs under the new SFC formulation. Across experiments, SFC yields higher semantic extraction accuracy than traditional SLU formulations. Post-trained LALMs on SFC-Bench show significant gains in spoken function calling capability and better leverage in-context learning in open-domain settings compared to standard SLU baselines.

Conclusion: Recasting spoken language understanding as Spoken Function Calling with explicit, structured rule definitions alleviates the ambiguity of traditional SLU and better matches the strengths of LLMs and LALMs. This new paradigm not only outperforms conventional SLU approaches in semantic extraction accuracy but also offers a scalable way to extend SLU to open-domain dialogue via curated functions, synthetic benchmarks, and post-training of audio-language models.

Abstract: Spoken Language Understanding (SLU) is the core component of task-oriented dialogue systems and a pivotal link in achieving seamless human-agent interaction. While traditional SLU can effectively extract user semantics for closed-set tasks after in-domain supervised fine-tuning, it faces significant challenges in leveraging in-context learning for open-domain tasks due to its ambiguous rule definitions. This work proposes Spoken Function Calling (SFC), a novel semantic understanding perspective that optimizes semantic understanding with structured rule definitions, to evolve beyond traditional closed-set SLU. Specifically, we curate and extend a suite of spoken functions based on traditional SLU datasets, construct a multi-agent system to synthesize the SFC-Bench dataset, evaluate the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Audio Language Models (LALMs), and enhance the SFC capabilities of LALMs through post-training. Experiments demonstrate that SFC outperforms traditional SLU, substantially enhancing the semantic extraction accuracy for LLMs and LALMs.

</details>


### [71] [Toward Skill-Native LLMs: Skill Entropy for Benchmarking and Training Long-Horizon Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05139)
*Yinghui He,Ling Yang,Jiarui Liu,Yongjin Yang,Lechen Zhang,Yingcheng Wu,Zhenfei Yin,Mengdi Wang,Sanjeev Arora*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Skill Entropy and Skill^2-Bench to evaluate and improve LLMs’ ability to switch between different reasoning skills in long-horizon tasks, and proposes a reinforcement learning framework (Skill-Entropy RL) that uses skill entropy as a training signal to significantly boost performance on such tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM benchmarks and training methods mainly focus on isolated skills (e.g., math, planning, coding) and do not systematically evaluate or improve how well models can switch between different skills within a single multi-step reasoning chain. Realistic long-horizon tasks often require such cross-skill reasoning, and existing metrics lack a principled way to quantify the difficulty of skill switching and the model’s competence at it.

Method: 1) Define Skill Entropy as a quantitative measure of the difficulty of switching from one reasoning skill to another within a task. 2) Construct Skill^2-Bench, a benchmark of cross-skill, multi-step tasks that span 558 skills across 9 domains, with each task labeled by a task-level skill-entropy score and grouped into three difficulty levels. 3) Evaluate multiple frontier and open-source LLMs on Skill^2-Bench to diagnose performance as a function of skill entropy. 4) Propose Skill-Entropy RL, a reinforcement learning framework where at each step the model outputs both an answer and the skill it claims to use; define a reward that mixes correctness of the answer with how well the predicted skill sequence matches the gold skill sequence via skill entropy, and train Qwen models and other data sources with this signal.

Result: Experiments on Skill^2-Bench show that all tested models (8 frontier and 4 open-source) experience accuracy drops as task skill entropy increases, revealing a clear skill-switching gap. Applying Skill-Entropy RL to Qwen3-4B-Instruct improves Skill^2-Bench accuracy from 34.4% to 68.4%, and for Qwen3-1.7B from 14.6% to 40.1%, outperforming strong baselines. The method also works when applied to standard training data like OpenR1-Math, suggesting that skill entropy is a general and reusable training signal beyond the bespoke benchmark.

Conclusion: Skill switching across heterogeneous reasoning skills is a distinct and measurable challenge for LLMs. Skill Entropy and Skill^2-Bench provide a principled way to quantify task difficulty and evaluate models’ cross-skill long-horizon reasoning capabilities. Incorporating skill entropy directly into reinforcement learning as a supervision signal (Skill-Entropy RL) substantially improves performance on such tasks and can be applied broadly to existing training corpora, positioning skill entropy as a useful, general-purpose signal for training more capable long-horizon reasoners.

Abstract: Long-horizon reasoning in recent LLMs demands that the model switch between distinct skills inside a reasoning chain, such as first doing a math derivation, then using the result to plan a schedule. We call such problems cross-skill long-horizon tasks: multi-step tasks whose steps require different reasoning skills and depend on earlier outputs. Existing benchmarks often evaluate individual skills, lacking a principled way to measure how well a model switches between skills. We address this gap from both the evaluation and training sides. We introduce Skill Entropy, a measure of the difficulty of switching from one skill to another. We then propose Skill^2-Bench, a benchmark of cross-skill long-horizon tasks built over 558 skills across 9 verifiable and open-ended domains. Each task is assigned a task-level skill-entropy score and grouped into three difficulty levels. Evaluating 8 frontier and 4 open-source models on Skill^2-Bench reveals a skill-switching gap: accuracy decreases on higher-entropy tasks. We then turn skill entropy from a benchmark scale into a training signal. We propose Skill-Entropy RL, an RL framework where the model predicts not only the answer at each step but also the skill used to produce it. The reward combines step-level correctness with a skill-entropy reward that measures the alignment between the model-predicted skill sequence and the gold skill sequence. On Qwen3-4B-Instruct and Qwen3-1.7B, Skill-Entropy RL improves the Skill^2-Bench score from 34.4% to 68.4% and from 14.6% to 40.1%, respectively, outperforming competitive baselines. The same pipeline can be applied to off-the-shelf training data such as OpenR1-Math, indicating that skill entropy is a reusable training signal. Code available at: https://github.com/Gen-Verse/Skill-Entropy-RL

</details>


### [72] [Reasoning Core: Designing Broad Procedural Data for Completion-Supervised Reasoning Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05148)
*Damien Sileo,Valentin Lacombe,Dimitri Kachler*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Reasoning Core, a suite of 50 procedural generators for reasoning tasks, and shows that fine-tuning on its data improves downstream reasoning benchmarks more than alternative procedural datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While procedural generators are widely used to create scalable reasoning benchmarks, they have been underexplored as curated sources of data for completion-supervised fine-tuning of language models. Existing collections differ in quality, semantics, and difficulty, and it is unclear which design choices actually lead to better downstream reasoning performance. The authors aim to systematically study and improve how procedural data is constructed and audited so it can reliably enhance model reasoning abilities.

Method: The authors build Reasoning Core, a library of 50 procedural generators covering diverse reasoning domains such as math, logic, planning, tracking, formal languages, structured data, games, causal reasoning, and code. Each generator comes with semantic scoring, difficulty controls, and task-specific evaluators. They fine-tune models under a matched completion-supervised protocol across four base-model configurations and multiple training durations, comparing Reasoning Core to Procedural Warmup, Reasoning Gym, SynLogic, and to a baseline without procedural data. They additionally design and run audits that combine model-assisted review, human adjudication, and regression tests to find inconsistencies in the generation pipeline, including target labels and scoring functions.

Result: In the main setting with a 3B-parameter model, training on Reasoning Core data yields the best mean performance on several downstream reasoning benchmarks (DROP, LogiQA, ARC-Challenge) compared with no procedural data and with three alternative procedural collections. Task-level results indicate that not all semantically valid generators are equally useful for training; properties such as compact answer formats and well-calibrated difficulty strongly affect how much a task helps. The audits uncover subtle but nontrivial mismatches between generators, renderers, targets, and scoring components in Reasoning Core and in competing collections.

Conclusion: Reasoning Core is an effective, audited suite of procedural generators for improving language-model reasoning through completion-supervised fine-tuning. Its design emphasizes not just semantic correctness but also training-oriented properties like target compactness and difficulty calibration, which are shown to matter for downstream gains. The work also demonstrates that procedural generation must be paired with systematic auditing, as automatic pipelines alone do not guarantee correctness. The authors release the library, datasets, and audit artifacts to support further research and more reliable procedural benchmarks.

Abstract: Procedural generators produce useful verifiable reasoning problems at scale, but have received less attention as data for completion-supervised fine-tuning. We introduce Reasoning Core, a collection of 50 generators spanning mathematics, logic, planning, state tracking, formal languages, structured data, games, causality, and code, with semantic scorers, difficulty controls, and task evaluators. Under a matched completion-supervised protocol, we compare Reasoning Core with Procedural Warmup, Reasoning Gym, and SynLogic across four base-model settings and multiple training durations. In the primary 3B comparison, Reasoning Core achieves the highest mean scores on DROP, LogiQA, and ARC-Challenge, exceeding both the baseline without procedural data and all three alternative procedural collections. Task-level analyses show that semantic validity alone does not ensure training utility, highlighting compact targets and calibrated difficulty as important design factors. We ran audits combining model-assisted review, human adjudication, and regression testing. Applied throughout Reasoning Core development and to the other collections, they reveal subtle mismatches among generation, rendering, targets, and scoring, a reminder that procedural generation alone does not guarantee correctness. The library, generated datasets, and audit material are publicly available.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [73] [A Long-Run Persistence Theory for AI Systems under the Redundancy-Adjusted Artificial Age Score (AAS)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04012)
*Seyma Yaman Kayadibi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper builds a theoretical framework showing that an AI system can run for infinitely many cycles without its structural ‘age’ blowing up, by defining and analyzing a redundancy-aware Artificial Age Score over repeated operation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems increasingly run continuously and adapt over time, we lack formal guarantees about whether they must inevitably accumulate structural ‘wear’ or complexity. The paper is motivated by the need for a rigorous, long-run notion of aging and persistence for AI systems that accounts for redundancy and allows reasoning about infinite operation without uncontrolled degradation.

Method: The authors extend the Artificial Age Score (AAS) from a static diagnostic to a dynamic, cycle-based functional. For each operation cycle, they compute a structural age using a weighted, redundancy-aware logarithmic penalty on component consistency levels. They then treat the resulting age sequence as a mathematical object and analyze its properties, proving boundedness, convergence behaviors, and asymptotic regimes (such as burdened vs. zero-burden persistence) under various assumptions on component stabilization, total variation, and inter-cycle perturbations.

Result: The paper proves that, under their redundancy-adjusted AAS framework, the cycle-level structural age is well-defined and uniformly bounded across infinitely many cycles, ruling out explosive aging. They characterize several asymptotic regimes of long-run behavior, derive comparative statics and sensitivity bounds, and show convergence properties: bounded persistence under finite total variation, geometric stabilization with damped perturbations, and conditions under which marginal aging goes to zero and even the cycle-level burden converges to zero.

Conclusion: Indefinite operation of an AI system does not mathematically require unbounded structural aging. With redundancy and appropriate regularity conditions, an AI system can undergo infinitely many update cycles while its structural burden remains bounded or even asymptotically vanishes. The proposed framework reframes long-run AI persistence as managing bounded structural burden rather than accepting inevitable cumulative deterioration, offering a basis for analyzing and designing persistent AI systems.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems are increasingly expected to operate over repeated cycles of interaction, adaptation, and update rather than through isolated one-shot outputs. This raises a fundamental theoretical question: can an AI system persist indefinitely without incurring unbounded structural aging? This paper develops a long-run persistence framework for AI systems based on the redundancy-adjusted Artificial Age Score (AAS). The model extends AAS from a static evaluative measure into a cycle-level functional that generates an age sequence across repeated operation. At each cycle, structural age is defined through a weighted, redundancy-aware logarithmic penalty over component consistency levels. Within this framework, cycle-level age is shown to be well defined and uniformly bounded, thereby excluding explosive pointwise aging. On this basis, the paper defines a hierarchy of asymptotic regimes, including burdened persistence, zero-burden persistence, oscillatory persistence, and cumulative terminal burden. It also establishes comparative ordering, sensitivity bounds, convergence under componentwise stabilization, persistence under finite total variation, geometric stabilization under damped inter-cycle perturbations, and a zero-burden characterization under nondegenerate redundancy conditions. The main result is that indefinite cyclic continuation does not require unbounded structural aging: an AI system may pass through infinitely many cycles while its structural age remains bounded, while under stronger regularity conditions its marginal aging vanishes and, in the strongest regime, its cycle-level burden converges to zero. The framework thus provides a formal basis for analyzing long-run artificial persistence as a problem of bounded structural burden rather than inevitable cumulative deterioration.

</details>


### [74] [The LLM Proposes, the Executive Disposes: A Self-Verifying Agent Instrument that Dissociates Commitment Drift from Binding Drift in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04066)
*Mohsen Arjmandi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They design an agent architecture where verification is built-in and structural, then use it to cleanly separate and measure different failure modes in long-horizon agents, even though the agent currently fails at the target task.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon agents are hard to trust because they can misreport internal states and because usual evaluation is post-hoc and entangled with multiple failure modes. The authors want a way to verify such agents and decompose "drift" and goal failures in a principled, experiment-like way.

Method: They build an "agent instrument": a deterministic Executive that owns all beliefs, with a language model limited to sending typed proposals. Claims are only accepted if predictions pre-registered before acting match later observations. They add self-invalidating safeguards (error floors, canary tests) and a render-invisible shadow reference that computes what the full system would have done in each ablation, enabling drift metrics even when components are removed. They then run ablation experiments targeting commitment vs binding mechanisms, tracking goal abandonment and drift over many beats and seeds, with strict run gating for validity.

Result: The instrument invalidated several early architecture runs by catching real defects, demonstrating its self-verification. In controlled ablations, removing the commitment mechanism switches goal abandonment from 0 to 1 without increasing binding error, showing a clean, single-variable failure in long-horizon persistence. Ablating binding repair does not show up as per-beat drift because binding is fully code-owned; instead, the problem manifests upstream as a collapse in hypothesis formation. Across 52 rigorously gated runs on ARC-AGI-3, the agent achieves zero task completions, as pre-registered, so all results are about structure, not task performance.

Conclusion: They propose a verification methodology for long-horizon agent development where correctness is structurally enforced rather than inferred post-hoc. Their instrument can decompose different kinds of drift and goal failures and self-invalidate when measurement assumptions break, even though the current agent is not yet competent at the benchmark task.

Abstract: How do you verify a long-horizon agent when its own state and self-reports are exactly what you cannot trust? We present an agent instrument built so that verification is structural rather than post-hoc. A deterministic Executive owns all belief; a language model may only file typed proposals, and a claim is admitted only when a prediction pre-registered before acting is matched against observation by code. Two properties make the instrument a verifier of its own science, not just of the agent: every run invalidates itself when per-organ write-error, render-size, or salted-canary-echo floors are breached (four of the first eight architecture runs were invalidated, each localizing a real defect); and a render-invisible shadow reference compiles the plan the full system would have committed in every ablation cell, so drift metrics are defined even where the mechanism under test has been removed. Using this instrument we report a clean, single-variable result on a failure every long-horizon agent suffers: ablating the commitment mechanism flips goal-abandonment from 0.00 to 1.00 while binding error stays flat at 0.00 (three seeds per cell, up to 394 reference beats per run, every run gated valid). The binding channel, by contrast, does not reappear as per-beat drift when its repair is ablated -- because binding is code-owned, the failure class is structurally absorbed, its only residue appearing one layer upstream as a collapse in hypothesis formation. We report these under full disclosure that task efficacy is null (zero level completions across 52 gated runs on ARC-AGI-3), pre-registered as a structural defeater. The contribution is a verification methodology for agent development and the drift decomposition it makes measurable.

</details>


### [75] [Monte Carlo Tree Search for Table-to-Multimodal Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04071)
*Teng Lin,Zhiyang Zhang,Yuyu Luo,Nan Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MCTS-Report, a Monte Carlo Tree Search-based framework that uses LLM-driven atomic actions and a multi-dimensional reward to generate coherent, accurate multimodal (text+charts) reports from tables, and evaluates it on a new benchmark (MMRBench) where it outperforms strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatically producing professional, multimodal analytical reports from structured tables is important for data intelligence but challenging. Existing systems rely on rigid, linear pipelines where subtasks like planning, visualization, and narrative generation are handled in isolation. This separation prevents joint optimization of factual accuracy, visual quality, and narrative coherence, and makes it hard to flexibly adapt the report generation process based on intermediate outcomes.

Method: The authors model multimodal table-to-report generation as a progressive construction process explored via Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS). They decompose report generation into atomic actions—chapter planning, selecting visualization tasks, generating charts, organizing insights, and refining the narrative. Each action is executed by a large language model with dynamic, state-conditioned reasoning, and the reasoning trajectory is stored in each search node for context. A multi-dimensional reward guides MCTS, jointly assessing numerical fact consistency using SQL queries, chart quality, alignment between charts and text, and structural completeness. Additional mechanisms include a diversity penalty to avoid redundant charts and a precondition check to prune invalid actions in the search space.

Result: They build MMRBench, a benchmark of real-world tables across six domains, each with expert-refined reference report structures and verifiable key insights. On MMRBench, MCTS-Report achieves an overall score of 77.9 and significantly outperforms strong baselines on metrics for structural completeness, numerical accuracy, chart-text alignment, and insight novelty.

Conclusion: Formulating multimodal report generation as an MCTS-driven, stepwise construction process with LLM-based atomic actions and a rich reward function leads to more accurate, coherent, and diverse reports than traditional pipeline methods. The proposed MMRBench benchmark enables systematic evaluation of such systems, and experimental results show that MCTS-Report is an effective framework for automatic professional multimodal report generation from tabular data.

Abstract: Automatically generating professional multimodal reports comprising both textual analysis and visual charts from structured tabular data is a critical challenge in data intelligence. Existing methods suffer from fixed linear pipelines and isolated subtask processing, which hinder joint optimization of factual accuracy, visual quality, and narrative coherence. To address these issues, this paper proposes MCTS-Report, a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS)-driven framework that formulates multimodal table-to-report generation as a progressive construction process over a structured search space. The core idea is to decompose report generation into atomic actions, including chapter planning, visualization task identification, chart generation, insight organization, and narrative refinement, each executed by an LLM based on dynamic reasoning conditioned on the current report state. We use an LLM to generate step-by-step reasoning and actions during MCTS, storing the reasoning trajectory in each node for context-aware, coherent report construction. To guide the search, we design a multi-dimensional reward function that jointly evaluates numerical fact consistency (via SQL), chart quality, chart-text alignment, and structural completeness, while incorporating a diversity penalty to suppress repeated charts and a precondition check to prune invalid actions. We also construct MMRBench, a comprehensive benchmark comprising real-world tables from six domains, paired with expert-refined reference report structures and verifiable key insights. Experiments on MMRBench demonstrate that MCTS-Report significantly outperforms strong baselines across structural completeness, numerical accuracy, chart-text alignment, and insight novelty, achieving a 77.9 overall score.

</details>


### [76] [FinProBench: Evaluating Financial AI Agents with Role-Grounded Rubrics Derived from Professional Deliverables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04077)
*Ben Wang,Kang Zhou,Lifan Guo,Feng Chen,Chi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FinProBench and a Role-Grounded Rubric Construction (RGRC) pipeline to build professional, role-based evaluation rubrics for financial AI agents, showing that rubrics derived from practitioner deliverables are crucial for specialized roles where model priors are weak.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation rubrics for financial AI agents are often derived from prompts or model outputs and thus miss the tacit, professional standards embedded in real practitioner work, especially in specialized roles. The authors want a benchmark and rubric construction method that aligns evaluation criteria with how real professionals are judged on the job.

Method: They build FinProBench from 1,723 curated real-world deliverables across 57 financial occupations and define a four-stage RGRC pipeline: (1) Deliverable Collection from practitioners in a given role, (2) Competency Extraction from those deliverables, (3) Rubric Synthesis into graded criteria that distinguish quality levels, and (4) Validation. They also classify the 57 occupations into prior-rich conventional roles and prior-sparse role-specialized roles and compare rubrics built via RGRC against prompt-only rubrics. They then evaluate multiple LLM systems and human deliverables using heterogeneous LLM judges and role-level rubrics, and estimate the effort savings from reusing rubrics at role level.

Result: Prompt-only rubrics perform nearly as well as RGRC rubrics on conventional roles (89.2% vs. 90.7%), but RGRC strongly outperforms for role-specialized roles (99.1% vs. 78.0%), showing the value of professional grounding where model priors are weak. Human deliverables score highest on average (73.7/100) but LLM systems are close (around 69.6–70.3/100) with overlapping 95% confidence intervals and complementary strengths. Role-level rubric reuse cuts estimated per-task rubric construction effort by a factor of 6.7 compared with writing rubrics from scratch for each task.

Conclusion: Deriving evaluation rubrics from practitioner deliverables via RGRC better captures tacit professional standards, especially in specialized financial roles that are underrepresented in model priors. FinProBench, built on these role-level rubrics, provides a more realistic benchmark for financial AI agents, demonstrates that humans still lead but LLMs are competitive, and shows that reusable, role-grounded rubrics can make large-scale, profession-aligned evaluation much more efficient.

Abstract: Evaluating financial AI agents requires criteria aligned with real professional work. Existing rubric methods typically derive criteria from task prompts or model outputs, overlooking tacit standards visible only in practitioner deliverables. We introduce FinProBench, a benchmark for professional financial tasks, and Role-Grounded Rubric Construction (RGRC), a reusable pipeline that derives rubrics from deliverables produced by practitioners in the same role. RGRC comprises four stages: Deliverable Collection, Competency Extraction, Rubric Synthesis, and Validation. Its rubrics capture tacit standards, distinguish quality levels, and transfer across tasks within a role. Before analysis, we classified 57 occupations by deliverable genre into 30 prior-rich conventional roles and 27 prior-sparse role-specialized roles. Across all roles, Prompt-only nearly matches RGRC for conventional roles (89.2% vs. 90.7%), but RGRC substantially outperforms it for role-specialized roles (99.1% vs. 78.0%). This split indicates that prompt engineering can approximate rubrics when conventions are well represented in model priors, while professional grounding is essential for standards beyond those priors. FinProBench is built from 1,723 curated deliverables spanning 57 occupations, 8 financial sub-industries, and 161 deliverable types, and releases an initial evaluation set of 20 complete tasks covering 20 roles in 7 sub-industries. With heterogeneous LLM judges and role-level rubrics, human deliverables rank first on average (73.7 vs. 70.3, 70.2, and 69.6 out of 100), while all four systems show overlapping 95% confidence intervals and complementary strengths. Reusing rubrics at the role level reduces estimated per-task construction effort by 6.7 times relative to authoring each rubric from scratch.

</details>


### [77] [FinPerMA: A Theory-Informed, Event-Grounded Personalized-Memory Benchmark for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04095)
*Ben Wang,Kang Zhou,Lifan Guo,Feng Chen,Chi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents FinPerMA, a benchmark for testing how well LLM agents maintain and update personalized user models for investors over time, especially after impactful financial events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are being used as personalized assistants in critical areas like financial advising, but it is unclear whether they can reliably track and update individual user preferences and profiles over extended periods. Existing benchmarks focus mostly on factual memory or loosely defined trajectories and do not rigorously assess how models adapt to user preference changes triggered by specific events (e.g., financial shocks).

Method: The authors build FinPerMA, an event-grounded, longitudinal benchmark using frozen investor trajectories. They generate data via a pipeline that: (1) uses deterministic, theory-based rules to define how events impact an investor’s preferences or situation; (2) employs controlled LLM narration to create realistic interaction histories; and (3) applies automated quality checks. They introduce a Post-Shock checkpoint to test whether an agent correctly integrates a significant event into its persistent user model. They then evaluate seven state-of-the-art LLMs across up to seven different memory configurations, including full-context, summary-based, and retrieval-based memories, on 2,994 questions spanning 276 investor personas.

Result: Across all tested models and memory setups, performance is far from ceiling. Even the strongest full-context configuration reaches only about 0.47 overall accuracy and roughly 39% accuracy on multiple-choice questions. Attribution analysis reveals that summary-based memory often retains factual details but loses subtle preference information that is crucial for personalization. As a result, relatively simple retrieval mechanisms can outperform more complex, purpose-built memory systems, and this advantage becomes more pronounced after preference-shifting shock events.

Conclusion: LLM agents currently struggle to maintain and update high-fidelity, event-sensitive personalized user models in financial advising scenarios. FinPerMA exposes substantial gaps in personalized memory and shows that current summarization-based memory approaches can inadvertently discard key preference signals. The findings encourage rethinking memory design for LLM agents, suggesting that more effective representations and update mechanisms are needed to capture and leverage evolving user preferences, particularly following major events.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly used as personalized assistants in high-stakes domains such as financial advising, yet it remains unclear whether they can maintain and update an individualized user model over long horizons. Existing personalized-memory benchmarks primarily test factual retention or rely on weakly constrained model-generated trajectories, leaving event-driven preference adaptation underexplored. We introduce FinPerMA, an event-grounded benchmark that evaluates personalized memory against frozen longitudinal investor trajectories. Its generation pipeline combines deterministic, theory-informed impact rules, controlled LLM narration, and automated quality screening; a Post-Shock checkpoint isolates whether an agent has integrated a material event into its persistent user model. On 2,994 questions from 276 personas, seven frontier LLMs and up to seven memory configurations remain far from saturated: no full-context configuration exceeds approximately 0.47 overall accuracy or approximately 39% on multiple-choice questions. Attribution analysis shows that summary-based memory often preserves factual details while losing the preference signals needed for personalization; simple retrieval can therefore outperform purpose-built memory systems, with the gap widening after shocks.

</details>


### [78] [BrainBench: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Comprehensive EEG Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04156)
*Yangxuan Zhou,Sha Zhao,Yuning Chen,Chen Wu,Jiquan Wang,Shijian Li,Gang Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a unified benchmark to systematically evaluate how well large language model-based systems can follow instructions to analyze EEG data and generate scientific reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current EEG-related benchmarks and demos focus on narrow decoding tasks or model-specific case studies, so they do not measure whether systems can follow natural-language instructions to perform full, scientifically sound EEG analyses. There is a need for a standardized, reproducible way to test this broader capability, which the authors call comprehensive EEG understanding.

Method: The authors build a benchmark, named (placeholder: \benchmarkname{}), that covers four domains: Foundational Analysis, Sleep Assessment, Neurocognitive Assessment, and Physiological Integration. It aggregates 17 datasets into numerous tasks and many real-data instances. For each instance, a system receives a natural-language instruction and EEG (optionally with other physiological signals) and must output an analysis report and, when requested, concrete artifacts (e.g., numerical results, code outputs). They define multi-faceted evaluation metrics, including numerical, categorical, set-based, sequence, semantic, and artifact validation. They then test multiple representative LLMs in more than 100,000 runs under two paradigms: (1) autonomous code-executing agents (CodeAct) and (2) structured analytical agents (BrainAgent).

Result: Performance differs widely across models, task subsets, difficulty levels, and between the two agent paradigms. No single model or setup dominates, and EEG analysis competence is shown to be strongly dependent on both the underlying LLM and how it is operationalized (e.g., via code-execution vs. structured reasoning workflows).

Conclusion: The proposed benchmark offers a rigorous, reproducible platform for quantifying and comparing LLM-based systems on comprehensive EEG understanding, moving beyond simple decoding tasks. It highlights substantial variability in current systems and establishes a public testbed (to be released with code and continually updated results) for driving methodological and model improvements in EEG-focused AI. 

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) analysis extends beyond assigning predefined labels to recordings; it requires workflows connecting natural-language instructions, signal processing, quantitative evidence, and scientific interpretation. We term this capability \emph{comprehensive EEG understanding}. Existing evaluations, however, primarily target isolated decoding tasks or system-specific demonstrations, leaving the competence of large language models (LLMs) insufficiently quantified. We introduce \benchmarkname{}, a unified benchmark for comprehensive, instruction-conditioned EEG understanding. It comprises four subsets---Foundational Analysis, Sleep Assessment, Neurocognitive Assessment, and Physiological Integration---covering 17 datasets, \numcases{} tasks, and over \numinstances{} real-data instances.
