Given an instruction and EEG recordings with optional physiological signals, a system must perform the analysis and produce a scientifically grounded report and, when required, artifacts. Outputs are assessed through numerical, categorical, set, sequence, semantic, and artifact validation. We evaluate \nummodels{} representative LLMs across more than 100K executions under two paradigms: autonomous code execution with CodeAct and structured agentic analysis with BrainAgent. Results vary substantially across models, subsets, difficulty levels, and execution paradigms, showing that EEG competence depends on the model and its operationalization. \benchmarkname{} provides a reproducible testbed for advancing LLM-based EEG understanding. The code and benchmark will be released soon, with evaluation results continuously updated.

</details>


### [79] [Adversarially Robust Abductive Fusion of Pre-trained Transformer-based Perception Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04190)
*Mario Leiva,Yue Ma,Qinru Qiu,Gerardo Simari,Paulo Shakarian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a domain-knowledge-free metacognitive layer to detect and correct errors in multiple pre-trained ViT detectors under distribution shift, using geometric properties of label embeddings and solving fusion as an abduction problem, achieving robustness to coordinated label-flipping attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pre-trained perception models fail under distribution shift, and simple ensemble methods (e.g., majority voting) or prior metacognitive rule systems either lose recall, are brittle to coordinated failures, or depend on hand-crafted domain knowledge that does not generalize to new environments. There is a need for a transferable, domain-agnostic way to detect and mitigate model errors when fusing multiple imperfect detectors.

Method: They construct per-model Label Vector Pools (LVPs) from the embedding space of each ViT detector using its training data. At test time, detections are evaluated geometrically relative to training prototypes in these LVPs to derive logical error-detection rules, forming a neurosymbolic metacognitive layer that does not require domain priors. Fusion of multiple detectors is cast as a consistency-based abduction problem, which is solved via an exact Integer Programming (IP) formulation and a polynomial-time heuristic to select a consistent set of detections.

Result: On an aerial imagery benchmark with 15 weather-shifted test sets and six ViT detectors, the learned geometric rules match the performance of domain-knowledge-based metacognitive rules within 0.002 F1, and on clean data match the strongest majority-vote variant within 0.005 F1. Under coordinated label-flipping attacks, their method maintains substantially higher F1 than majority-vote baselines; for example, at a 90% flip rate it achieves 0.42 F1 versus 0.35 for MV-Plurality (a 22% relative improvement), and for flip rates above 0.4 it attains the best F1 on every test set.

Conclusion: A domain-knowledge-free, geometry-based neurosymbolic metacognitive layer can effectively detect and correct errors in ensembles of pre-trained ViT detectors under distribution shift and adversarial label-flipping, matching or surpassing domain-knowledge and majority-vote baselines while remaining compatible with additional knowledge-based rules when available.

Abstract: Deploying pre-trained perception models in novel environments degrades their accuracy under distributional shift, and assembling them alone does not recover it: combiners such as majority voting trade recall for precision and are brittle to coordinated failures. Prior metacognitive methods learn logical rules that flag a model's errors, but rely on hand-authored domain-knowledge cues (object-size priors, segmentation masks) that do not transfer to genuinely novel scenes. We show that this metacognitive layer can be learned without any domain knowledge by exploiting vector-space geometry: per-model Label Vector Pools (LVP), built from each model's own training embeddings, yield error-detection rules from the geometry of detections relative to training-determined prototypes, reaching parity with domain-knowledge rules to within $0.002$ every F1 on test set. Because the approach remains neurosymbolic, these geometric rules share a single logical framework and can still be complemented by domain knowledge when available. We frame the fusion of multiple imperfect ViT-based detectors as a consistency-based abduction problem solved at test time by an exact Integer Program (IP) and a polynomial-time heuristic. On an aerial-imagery benchmark of 15 weather-shifted test sets and six ViT detectors, our domain-knowledge-free layer matches the strongest majority-vote variant on clean data (within $0.005$ F1) and, unlike every majority-vote baseline, retains its performance under a coordinated label-flipping attack: at a $90\%$ flip rate it averages $0.42$ F1 versus $0.35$ for MV-Plurality (a $22\%$ relative gain) and attains the highest F1 on \emph{every} test set once the flip rate exceeds $0.4$

</details>


### [80] [MatrAIx: Simulating the World with 8.3 Billion Persona Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04205)
*Xiaomin Li,Yuexing Hao,Jianheng Hou,Jintao Huang,Qianfeng Wen,Shirley Huang,Yifan Liu,Xiaoyi Liu,Yilan Fan,Yijun Wang,Koutian Wu,Ruoqi Gao,Muhammad Ahmed Mohsin,Jing Tang,Brihi Joshi,Heming Liu,Zheyuan Deng,Zonglin Di,Sankalp Jajee,Jiuyao Lu,Zhiwei Zhang,Saksham Kapoor,Ishan Gupta,Yunhan Zhao,Chanwoo Park,Yucheng Lu,Bing Hu,Weihang Xiao,Aravind Mohan,Hanwen Xing,Runyu Zhang,Mihir Kulshreshtha,Yuanda Xu,Qianyu Zhu,Dianzhuo Wang,Yuxin Xiao,Bowen Jiang,Yongye Su,Wenhao Chai,Zuxin Liu,Lawrence Yunliang Chen,Xuandong Zhao,Ethan Ye,Shivam Patel,Jason Xie,Alex Martin Richmond,Weixiang Ding,Emre Okcular,Diya Mathew,Ziheng Wang,Rana M. Shahroz Khan,Zhejian Peng,Fang Wu,Fan Nie,Xinyang Han,Yubin Kim,Jiawei Zhang,Zhenting Qi,Huangyuan Su,Xu Pan,Abinitha Gourabathina,Hyewon Jeong,Hemanth Neelgund Ramesh,Kumail Alhamoud,Kimia Hamidieh,Zidi Xiong,Samuel Schmidgall,Pengrui Han,Yepeng Huang,Yongheng Wang,Bowen Yang,Alex Gu,Yuchu Wang,Akshay Paruchuri,Brenna Li,Hejie Cui,Jiayuan Ding,Chaosheng Dong,Jiahao Wang,Yixuan He,Chi Wang,Pamela Bhattacharya,Tianyi Peng,Paul Pu Liang,Mitchell Gordon,Yilun Du,Marinka Zitnik,James Zou,Prasanna Tambe,Philip Torr,Emily Fox,Asu Ozdaglar,Dawn Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents MatrAIx, a large-scale simulated-user framework that uses diverse personas and interactive environments to evaluate AI systems and digital products more cheaply and scalably than human studies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional human evaluations of AI systems and digital products are expensive, slow, and hard to scale, while offline benchmarks poorly capture real user diversity and interactive behaviors. The authors want a way to do large-scale, realistic, and diverse evaluations without requiring massive human participation each time.

Method: They build MatrAIx with three main parts: (1) Persona 8B, a massive space of 8.3B persona records across 1,290 categorical attributes, sampled via a dependency graph or derived from human-authored profiles, from which they release a cleaned ~1M-persona coreset mixing human-grounded and synthetic records; (2) the MatrAIx Playground, four interactive environments (Survey, AI Chatbot, Web, App) where persona-driven agents interact with AI systems and products; and (3) a set of 1,010 application tasks spanning 25+ domains. Persona agents are instantiated using three LLMs to simulate user behavior across 18,189 evaluation trials. They then run validation studies on persona adherence (do agents behave according to specified traits) and on the quality of extracted human-grounded personas using both human and LLM judges.

Result: They demonstrate that their framework can generate rich, diverse feedback patterns such as differences in willingness to continue after AI failure, sensitivity to price changes, and tolerance for latency, varying systematically with persona attributes. In a 400-trial controlled study across ten behavioral attributes and four environments, persona behaviors matched the specified traits (or were correctly suppressed) in 91.5% of cases. Additional evaluations by human and LLM judges indicate that the human-grounded personas are of good extraction quality.

Conclusion: MatrAIx is an effective end-to-end infrastructure for scalable, population-level, simulated-user evaluation of AI systems and digital products. It captures heterogeneous user preferences and behaviors across many domains and tasks with validated persona fidelity, offering a more realistic and flexible alternative or complement to traditional human evaluation and static offline benchmarks.

Abstract: Human evaluation of AI systems and digital products is costly, slow, and difficult to scale. Offline evaluations are more scalable but often abstract away human diversity and interactive behavior. We therefore introduce MatrAIx, a population-scale simulated-user evaluation infrastructure for testing AI systems and digital products with heterogeneous users. MatrAIx has three core components: First, Persona 8B contains 8.3 billion persona records represented by 1,290 categorical dimensions. Records are either sampled from a dependency graph that preserves correlated attributes or derived from human-authored profiles. We release a quality-filtered coreset of approximately 1 million personas, comprising 599,847 human-grounded and 400,000 synthetic records. Second, the MatrAIx Playground provides four environments in which diverse users evaluate and interact with digital products: Survey, AI Chatbot, Web, and App. Third, MatrAIx provides 1,010 application tasks spanning more than 25 domains, including Commerce, Software, Finance, and Healthcare. We conducted 18,189 evaluation trials across eight representative tasks. Persona agents were powered by three LLMs: Claude Opus 4.8, GPT 5.5, and Claude Haiku 4.5. The resulting feedback captures how decisions and preferences vary across persona backgrounds, including hesitation after a price increase, willingness to continue after an AI assistant fails, and latency tolerance. We conducted two main validation studies: First, a 400-trial controlled study evaluated persona adherence across ten behavioral attributes and all four environments. The declared behavior was expressed or correctly suppressed in 366 trials (91.5%). Second, human and LLM judges evaluated the extraction quality of human-grounded personas. Overall, MatrAIx provides an end-to-end infrastructure for evaluating AI systems and digital products with diverse simulated human users.

</details>


### [81] [Interoceptive Attention as Dynamic Homeostatic Prioritization in a Foraging Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04232)
*St John Grimbly,Nicolas Kuske,Evert A. Boonstra,Bruce A. Bassett,Charel van Hoof,Rowan Hodson,Benjamin Rosman,Ryan Smith,Mark Solms,Jonathan P. Shock*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates how a fixed, limited budget of perceptual precision should be allocated across multiple bodily needs in an active inference foraging agent, showing that dynamically focusing precision on the most pressing need strongly improves survival and learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biological organisms must manage several competing bodily needs (e.g., hunger, thirst) with limited perceptual resources. Increasing the accuracy (precision) of perception for one need reduces the precision available for others. The motivation is to understand how an agent should optimally allocate a fixed perceptual precision budget across needs, and how this allocation impacts both survival and learning within an active inference framework.

Method: The authors build an active inference foraging agent in a gridworld environment (AffectWorld) with four interoceptive channels representing different bodily needs. The agent has a fixed budget of interoceptive precision that can be dynamically reallocated. At each time step, it inspects its beliefs about its own bodily state, identifies the most urgent (most-needed) channel, and reallocates most or all of the precision budget to that channel. This precision shapes the interoceptive likelihood used both in belief updating and planning. They compare this selective-precision agent against control agents: one with precision spread uniformly across channels, one where precision is not available to the planner, and one where precision is misallocated to the least-needed channel. They evaluate performance in terms of survival during learning and learning speed for channel dynamics across multiple gridworld layouts and random seeds, using statistical tests (paired cluster-bootstrap).

Result: In the AffectWorld four-channel gridworld, the selective allocation of precision to the currently most-needed bodily channel more than doubles survival during the learning phase compared to an agent with the same total precision budget spread uniformly ($0.414$ vs $0.199$ survival across 11 layouts, 32 seeds each; paired cluster-bootstrap $p \leq 10^{-4}$). The benefit is partially mediated by planning: when the shaped likelihood is denied to the planning component while retained for perception, about half of the survival advantage disappears. The benefit is also need-specific: deliberately targeting precision at the least-needed channel performs worse than simply spreading precision evenly. Moreover, the attended (high-precision) channel learns its own dynamics about twice as fast and maintains this advantage even when controlling for the number of observations, indicating that precision routing leaves a clear behavioural trace in learning speed beyond survival outcomes.

Conclusion: Dynamically routing a limited budget of interoceptive precision to the most pressing bodily need substantially improves both survival and learning in an active inference foraging agent, compared to uniform or misaligned precision allocation. The advantage arises through both improved perception and more effective planning, and manifests not only in survival but also in faster, channel-specific learning of dynamics. This suggests that biological systems may benefit from tightly coupling attentional-like precision control to internal need states, and that such precision routing leaves measurable behavioural signatures.

Abstract: Biological systems must regulate competing needs under limited perceptual bandwidth, where sharpening one estimate costs the capacity to sharpen the others. Any fixed-budget system therefore has to decide where to allocate its perceptual precision. We study this in a foraging agent that must keep several bodily needs satisfied to survive, modelled with active inference. At each step it reads its own body-state beliefs, identifies the most-needed channel, and reallocates a fixed budget of interoceptive precision toward it, so that the same precision-shaped likelihood feeds both belief update and planning. In AffectWorld, a four-channel foraging gridworld, this selective allocation more than doubles learning-phase survival at matched budget against a uniform-precision agent ($0.414$ vs $0.199$ across 11 layouts, $n{=}32$ seeds each, paired cluster-bootstrap $p \leq 10^{-4}$). Two further results sharpen the mechanism. The benefit runs through planning as well as perception, since denying the shaped likelihood to the planner alone removes about half of it. It is also need-aligned, since aiming precision at the least-needed channel does worse than spreading it evenly. The attended channel additionally learns its own dynamics about twice as fast, and stays ahead even at matched observation count, a behavioural trace of the same precision routing, visible in learning speed, not survival.

</details>


### [82] [The RAIL Principles for Neurosymbolic AI: Reasoning, Assurances, Interfacing and Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04285)
*Agnese Chiatti,Michael Cochez,Cristina Cornelio,Sebastijan Dumancic,Artur d'Avila Garcez,Luis C. Lamb,Lia Morra,Mathias Niepert,Robert Peharz,Alberto Speranzon,Maarten Stol,Annette Ten Teije,Thiviyan Thanapalasingam,Frank Van Harmelen,Emile Van Krieken,Antonio Vergari,Benjie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that neurosymbolic AI—combining machine learning with symbolic reasoning—is a broad, central paradigm for building reliable and trustworthy AI, and proposes the RAIL (Reasoning, Assurances, Interfacing, Learning) framework to analyze and design such systems across many AI domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI is dominated by data-intensive neural networks and language models, which struggle with reliability, transparency, and performance in high‑stakes or low‑data settings. Many practical systems already mix learning with formal reasoning, but are not recognized under a unified conceptual lens. The authors want to (1) show that neurosymbolic AI is not a niche but already pervasive and important, and (2) provide a principled framework to guide the design of next‑generation AI systems that must be trustworthy, efficient, and robust.

Method: This is a perspective/conceptual paper. The authors: (1) define neurosymbolic AI broadly as combinations of machine learning and formal reasoning; (2) distill four design principles—Reasoning, Assurances, Interfacing, and Learning (RAIL); (3) re‑analyze a variety of leading AI systems (e.g., physics‑aware ML, neuro‑guided search like Alpha-* systems, causal learning approaches, and tool‑augmented LLMs) through the RAIL lens; and (4) use these case studies to argue that the framework unifies apparently disparate techniques and clarifies design trade‑offs.

Result: The paper shows that many successful AI systems, including those not typically labeled as neurosymbolic, can be systematically described and compared using the RAIL principles. By mapping examples from multiple subfields onto RAIL, the authors demonstrate that neurosymbolic patterns are widespread and that the framework captures important dimensions of system design such as how reasoning is done, how assurances are provided, how components interface, and how learning is integrated.

Conclusion: Neurosymbolic AI should be seen as a central, unifying paradigm rather than a niche. The RAIL framework—Reasoning, Assurances, Interfacing, Learning—provides a practical guide for analyzing existing systems and for designing future production‑level AI that is more reliable, efficient, and trustworthy. Applying RAIL can help practitioners make more principled architectural choices and better integrate neurosymbolic methods into real‑world AI technologies across diverse application domains.

Abstract: Neurosymbolic AI systems that integrate machine learning and symbolic reasoning are rapidly gaining attention. They complement the data-intensive statistical approaches of neural networks and language models with symbolic reasoning algorithms to function in high-stakes domains or in low-data regimes that characterize many real-world applications. We argue that the neurosymbolic combination of machine learning and formal reasoning is not a niche approach within AI, but rather includes many already successful techniques that are of crucial importance to the development of reliable, efficient and, ultimately, trustworthy systems. This perspective prompts a re-examination of the design of current AI systems. We show that many leading AI systems, including some that are not traditionally considered as neurosymbolic, can be analysed from the perspective of four principles of neurosymbolic AI design: Reasoning, Assurances, Interfacing and Learning (RAIL). Applying the RAIL framework offers a unified view of seemingly disparate AI systems, ranging from physics-aware machine learning to neuro-guided search (such as Google DeepMind's Alpha-* suite), causal learning and tool-augmented Large Language Models. Importantly, the RAIL principles will enable engineers to make better-informed and more principled decisions about the design and deployment of production-level AI systems. In this article, we introduce the RAIL principles, examine how they can be applied across major areas of AI, and illustrate how they may guide practitioners to integrate neurosymbolic methods into next-generation AI technologies.

</details>


### [83] [SafeCommit: Certifying When Memory-Grounded Agents May Safely Act](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04289)
*Mayur Akewar,Ravi Ranjan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SafeCommit is a control layer that decides when an AI agent has enough reliable evidence to safely take irreversible, side‑effectful actions, given uncertain or potentially faulty memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon agents rely on persistent memory and external tools, but their memories can become stale, inconsistent, or corrupted. If the agent acts on such faulty grounding, it may cause harmful side effects. Existing work focuses mostly on what actions to take, not on when the available evidence is sufficiently trustworthy. The paper aims to formalize and control this “premature commitment” failure mode.

Method: The authors formalize the problem as “safe commitment under memory uncertainty.” They propose SafeCommit, a risk-controlled layer that sits between an agent’s internal reasoning and its external actions. SafeCommit builds a set of plausible latent world states using memory, current observations, tool outputs, provenance information, and policy constraints. It then uses conformal prediction to generate an “action certificate”: an action is allowed only if it is proven safe in every world within this calibrated set. If certification fails, the system chooses a low-side-effect probe action designed to distinguish between the problematic worlds, or falls back to a conservative default. The framework also analyzes the statistical guarantees under assumptions about world-set calibration and representation quality.

Result: Under assumptions of calibrated coverage over plausible worlds, SafeCommit ensures that the probability of executing an unsafe but certified action is bounded by a target risk level α. With imperfect world generation, the analysis decomposes risk into calibration error and representation error. A controlled, dependency-free simulator is implemented to explore how tightening risk constraints trades off against utility (e.g., frequency of commits, need for probes) and to reproduce the paper’s empirical findings with a single command.

Conclusion: SafeCommit provides a principled decision layer that governs not just what an agent should do but when it has sufficient evidence to do it safely in the presence of memory uncertainty. By using calibrated sets of plausible worlds and conformal action certificates, the framework offers explicit, tunable guarantees on the risk of unsafe commitments, while fallback probing strategies handle ambiguity and highlight the safety–utility tradeoff in long-horizon agents with persistent memory.

Abstract: Long-horizon agents increasingly use persistent memory and tools to take actions with external side effects. A central failure mode is premature commitment: an agent acts before resolving whether its memory grounding is stale, conflicting, incomplete, or corrupted. We formalize this problem as safe commitment under memory uncertainty and introduce SafeCommit, a risk controlled layer between agent reasoning and external execution. The layer constructs a calibrated set of plausible latent worlds from memory, observations, tool outputs, provenance, and policy constraints. It permits a side effectful action only when a conformal action certificate shows that the action is safe in every retained world. Otherwise, it selects a low-side-effect probe that targets the worlds blocking certification, or returns a conservative fallback. Under calibrated world coverage, the probability of an unsafe certified commit is at most the target level α; with imperfect world proposal, the bound separates calibration and representation error. A dependency-free controlled simulator illustrates the safety-utility tradeoff and reproduces all reported results with one command. The goal is to offer a concrete approach for deciding not only what an agent should do, but when the available evidence is sufficient to safely do it.

</details>


### [84] [NeuMoSync: End-to-End Neuromodulatory Control for Plasticity and Adaptability in Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04358)
*Seyed Roozbeh Razavi Rohani,Khashayar Khajavi,Wesley Chung,Mandana Samiei,Mo Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NeuMoSync is a neuromodulation-inspired architecture that adds neuron-specific, context-aware modulation signals to deep networks, significantly improving plasticity, forward transfer, and backward adaptation in continual learning across diverse benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In continual learning, neural networks must learn tasks sequentially without forgetting old knowledge while still remaining plastic enough to adapt to new tasks. Standard deep networks often lose plasticity, show limited forward and backward transfer, and struggle under settings like random labels, concept drift, class-incremental, and domain-incremental learning. Biological brains, however, use global neuromodulatory systems to coordinate local plasticity and activity across regions. The paper is motivated by bridging this gap: importing brain-inspired global coordination and neuromodulation principles into deep learning architectures to maintain adaptability over long task sequences.

Method: The authors propose NeuMoSync, an architectural extension to standard deep networks. Each neuron is augmented with a learnable feature vector that encodes network-wide historical context across tasks. On top of the base network, a higher-level neuromodulatory module takes as input both the current data and the evolving network state (via these neuron-specific context vectors). It then outputs neuron-specific modulation signals that dynamically control both activation dynamics and synaptic plasticity. The system is trained end-to-end and evaluated within standard continual learning settings. Extensive ablations remove or vary components (such as the neuron-specific context vectors or the neuromodulatory module) to quantify their contribution. The learned modulatory signals are further analyzed for interpretability and coordination structure across tasks.

Result: NeuMoSync achieves strong performance on multiple continual learning benchmarks: random-label memorization tasks (Random Label CIFAR-10, Random Label MNIST), concept drift benchmarks (Shuffle CIFAR-10, Shuffle Mini-ImageNet), class-incremental settings (Class Split ImageNet, Class Split CIFAR-100), and domain-incremental tasks (Permuted MNIST). Compared to existing CL methods, NeuMoSync better preserves plasticity and improves both forward adaptation (learning new tasks effectively) and backward adaptation (improving or maintaining performance on previous tasks after learning new ones). Ablation studies show that removing neuron-specific context vectors or the neuromodulatory coordination module degrades performance, confirming their necessity. Analysis of the learned modulatory signals reveals meaningful, interpretable patterns of coordination that vary across tasks and learning stages.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that integrating brain-inspired global neuromodulatory and coordination mechanisms into deep neural networks can substantially improve continual learning. By giving each neuron a context-tracking feature vector and using a higher-level module to synthesize neuron-specific modulation signals conditioned on both inputs and the evolving network state, NeuMoSync maintains plasticity and supports effective forward and backward transfer across diverse CL scenarios. The authors argue that such global coordination mechanisms are a promising direction for building more robust, adaptive, and biologically motivated continual learning systems, and they release code to facilitate further research and extensions.

Abstract: Continual learning (CL) requires models to learn tasks sequentially, yet deep neural networks often suffer from plasticity loss and poor knowledge transfer, which can impede their long-term adaptability. Drawing high-level inspiration from global neuromodulatory mechanisms in the brain, we introduce Neuromodulation and Synchronization (NeuMoSync), a novel architecture that integrates dynamic, neuron-specific modulation into deep neural networks to enhance their adaptability and plasticity. NeuMoSync extends standard neural network architectures with learnable feature vectors for each neuron that track network-wide historical context and with a module operating at a higher level of abstraction. This module synthesizes neuron-specific signals, conditioned on both current inputs and the network's evolving state, to adaptively regulate activation dynamics and synaptic plasticity. Evaluated on diverse CL benchmarks, including memorization (Random Label CIFAR-10 and Random Label MNIST), concept drift (Shuffle CIFAR-10 and Shuffle Mini-ImageNet), class-incremental learning (Class Split ImageNet and Class Split CIFAR-100), and domain-incremental learning (Permuted MNIST), NeuMoSync demonstrates strong performance in retaining plasticity and achieves improvements in both forward and backward adaptation compared with existing methods. Ablation studies validate the necessity of each component, while analysis of the learned modulatory signals reveals interpretable coordination patterns across tasks. Our work underscores the potential of integrating global coordination mechanisms into deep learning systems to advance robust, adaptive continual learning. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/RoozbehRazavi/NeuMoSync.

</details>


### [85] [Improving Auto-Design of Neural PDE Solvers with a Domain-Specific Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04384)
*Shengxin Kong,Liwen Xu,Jingwen Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ADSL-PDE, a structured search representation and compiler for automatically designing neural PDE solvers, which greatly improves search efficiency and stability compared to direct code-generation approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatically designing neural PDE solvers with LLMs is difficult because the search space of all possible Python programs is huge and extremely sparse in valid, numerically stable solvers. Direct code generation forces the model to spend most of its effort avoiding syntax, semantic, and stability errors rather than reasoning about solver quality. The authors aim to restructure this search problem so that exploration focuses on meaningful solver design decisions instead of low-level implementation details.

Method: The authors introduce ADSL-PDE, a domain-specific, structured search state that encodes the key functional choices in a neural PDE solver—such as network architecture, physical constraints, loss objectives, data sampling, and optimization settings—while abstracting away implementation details. A deterministic compiler translates any valid search state into an executable solver. On top of this representation, an evolutionary agent performs iterative proposal, evaluation, and refinement of solver designs, guided by empirical performance feedback on PDE benchmarks.

Result: Using ADSL-PDE, the evolutionary agent more efficiently explores the design space of neural PDE solvers and avoids large regions of invalid code. On several PDE benchmarks, the approach yields faster search progress and more stable optimization, with reported performance improvements exceeding 52% within the first ten evolution iterations compared to baselines.

Conclusion: Encoding neural PDE solvers as structured design states, compiled deterministically to executable code, reshapes the search space so that LLM-driven or evolutionary search focuses on valid, consequential design decisions instead of low-level programming issues. This leads to significantly better and more stable auto-design of PDE solvers and illustrates a general principle: effective LLM-based design systems require appropriate search representations, not just stronger reasoning in the LLM itself.

Abstract: Neural PDE solver auto-design is fundamentally a search-space representation problem. In the space of unrestricted Python programs, valid solvers form an extremely sparse subset: most candidate programs are syntactically incorrect, semantically incompatible, or numerically unstable. Direct code generation therefore forces an LLM to spend most of its search capacity navigating implementation failures rather than reasoning about solver quality. ADSL-PDE addresses this challenge by introducing a structured search state between solver concepts and executable code. It represents the functional decisions that determine a neural PDE solver (architecture, physical constraints, objectives, sampling, and optimization) while abstracting away low-level implementation details. A deterministic compiler maps each valid search state to an executable solver. In effect, ADSL-PDE reshapes the search space: it removes large regions of invalid programs, increases the density of meaningful candidates, and preserves the compositional freedom needed to discover previously unseen designs. Solver evolution can thus operate over design decisions rather than code artifacts. Built on this representation, our evolutionary agent iteratively proposes, evaluates, and refines solver search states using empirical feedback. Across multiple PDE benchmarks, ADSL-PDE improves both search efficiency and optimization stability, achieving an improvement of more than 52% within the first ten evolution iterations. These results suggest a broader principle for LLM-driven auto-design: effective agents do not merely require stronger reasoning, but rather a search representation that concentrates exploration on valid and consequential decisions.

</details>


### [86] [Architectural Implications of Agentic AI Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04458)
*Jirong Yang,Peizhe Liu,Chaojie Zhang,Jovan Stojkovic*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how emerging agentic AI workloads behave in datacenters, shows that current uniform server architectures are a poor fit, and proposes Agora, a prototype system that improves utilization and tail latency via role-aware CPU scheduling and GPU memory oversubscription.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems—where LLM-based agents perform multi-step workflows involving tools and orchestration—are increasingly deployed in datacenters, yet we lack an architectural understanding of their resource usage and performance behavior. Existing server designs are optimized for more uniform, monolithic inference workloads, which may mismatch the fragmented, heterogeneous, and bursty nature of agentic workflows, potentially wasting CPU/GPU resources and harming latency. The paper aims to fill this gap and guide both software mechanisms and future server architectures.

Method: The authors first construct a taxonomy of agentic AI workflows, characterizing their components (LLM calls, tools, orchestration) and how these map to hardware usage. They conduct a production study at Microsoft Azure combined with a controlled study using open-source agent frameworks to measure fragmentation, CPU/GPU interaction, temporal load patterns, and locality effects. Based on these observations, they derive architectural implications and design Agora, a prototype system for commodity servers. Agora implements dynamic CPU core harvesting for background throughput tasks, tail-latency-aware protection against tool spikes, GPU memory oversubscription with state prefetching to hide swap latency, and role-based CPU core pooling with affinity-aware scheduling. They then evaluate Agora’s impact on utilization, throughput, and agent tail latency.

Result: Empirical studies show that agentic AI workloads produce fragmented and heterogeneous execution: many small orchestration and tool tasks on the CPU interleaved with GPU LLM inferences, repeated crossings of the CPU-GPU boundary, low average utilization with sudden spikes, and wide variation in GPU usage due to diverse model compositions and tools. This leads to stranded CPU and GPU capacity, inefficiencies from homogeneous CPU provisioning, and degraded microarchitectural locality when multiple agents are multiplexed on shared cores. Agora’s mechanisms—CPU core harvesting, GPU memory oversubscription with prefetching, and role-aware scheduling—substantially improve server utilization and throughput while keeping agent tail latency within acceptable bounds, even under bursty tool activity.

Conclusion: Agentic AI introduces fragmented, heterogeneous, and bursty workflows that do not align well with conventional uniform server architectures, causing underutilized CPU/GPU resources and locality problems. A better match requires recognizing distinct software roles, managing CPU and GPU resources with finer granularity, and actively exploiting idle capacity while protecting latency-sensitive operations. Agora demonstrates that such role-aware and oversubscribed designs can significantly improve utilization and throughput without sacrificing tail latency. The paper concludes that future server architectures for agentic AI should explicitly account for agentic workflow structure, CPU-GPU coordination costs, and microarchitectural locality, and it outlines these as key directions for further research and system design.

Abstract: Agentic AI is emerging in datacenters, but its architectural implications remain unexplored. We organize agentic workflows in a taxonomy and present its first architectural characterization with a production study at Microsoft Azure and a controlled study of open-source frameworks. We show that agentic execution is fragmented and heterogeneous. Requests expand into a workflow of LLM inferences, tool invocations, and orchestration decisions that repeatedly cross the CPU-GPU boundary. Our taxonomy explains how this fragmentation turns into resource demand. As orchestration and tools run on the host, the CPU sits on the critical path. Execution structure sets the load over time, which stays low with sudden spikes. Model composition sets how evenly the workflow uses the GPUs. Diversity in tasks and tools widens this range even further. These characteristics expose architectural mismatches of conventional uniform servers. Fragmented execution strands CPU and GPU capacity despite bursty demand. Different software roles make homogeneous CPU provisioning inefficient. Finally, multiplexing many agents onto shared cores degrades microarchitectural locality. Guided by our findings, we derive implications for agentic servers and examine them through Agora, our prototype for commodity servers. Agora dynamically harvests idle CPU cores for co-located throughput work, while protecting agentic tail latency against tool spikes. It oversubscribes GPU memory by placing more agents on each GPU, prefetching the next agent's state to hide swap latency. To match the machine to the heterogeneous roles, Agora pools cores by role and applies affinity-aware scheduling to restore locality. It automatically tunes mechanisms to the workload. Agora improves utilization and server throughput while preserving agent tail latency. Our insights also identify key directions for future server architectures for agentic AI.

</details>


### [87] [CARGO-VL: Counterfactual Arbitration with Risk-Constrained Group Optimization for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04509)
*De Jiang,Zhengyang Zhang,Kehong Yuan,Shaohua Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes CARGO-VL, a training framework that makes vision-language models more reliable when image and retrieved text disagree or are both wrong, and demonstrates that it improves conflict handling and abstention behavior over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models often rely on both image content and retrieved text, but these two sources can conflict or both be insufficient. Current post-training methods score examples independently and fail to enforce that the model behaves coherently when evidence is counterfactually altered (e.g., changing only the text or only the image). There is a need for models that can identify which modality is trustworthy in each case, abstain when neither is reliable, and maintain consistent, principled behavior across systematic changes in the available evidence.

Method: The authors introduce CARGO-VL, a group-relative training framework where each training group consists of matched variants of the same base example under four evidence conditions: image-correct (A/V), text-correct (A/T), both-correct/aligned, and both-wrong (N). Instead of optimizing each instance independently, the objective is defined over the whole group. It includes (1) condition-wise correctness terms, and (2) transition rewards that encourage answer invariance when evidence changes but remains supportive, source equivariance when the correct modality changes, and appropriate switching from answering to abstaining when evidence becomes insufficient. A primal-dual controller dynamically trades off between the risks of unsafe (unsupported) answers and excessive deferral/abstention. They also create XMC (eXtended Modal Conflict), a dataset with four-condition conflict examples, and evaluate transfer to CMC-Bench and Modality-Bias benchmarks. Ablation studies investigate the contributions of transition-based relational signals and the adaptive risk controller.

Result: Experiments on conflict-handling benchmarks (CMC-Bench, Modality-Bias) show that models trained with CARGO-VL outperform pointwise baseline objectives in several dimensions: more accurate resolution of conflicts between image and text, reduced incidence of unsupported answers, and better balance between reliance on visual and textual modalities. Results are consistent across multiple random seeds. Ablation analyses reveal that both the relational transition components and the primal-dual risk controller contribute complementary improvements, confirming that the group-relative, counterfactual-consistency-based objective is key to the observed gains.

Conclusion: CARGO-VL demonstrates that training vision-language models with group-relative objectives over counterfactual evidence bundles can substantially improve their reliability in multimodal settings with conflicting or insufficient evidence. By explicitly encouraging consistent transitions across evidence states, and adaptively managing the trade-off between unsafe answering and over-abstention, the framework leads to better conflict arbitration and unsupported-answer avoidance than standard pointwise objectives. The findings support counterfactual consistency and structured multimodal conflict resources (like XMC) as practical tools for building safer, more trustworthy vision-language systems.

Abstract: Vision-language systems combine images with retrieved text, but these sources can disagree or jointly fail to support an answer. Reliable models must identify the trustworthy source and abstain when neither is adequate. Existing post-training objectives score instances independently and therefore do not enforce coherent behavior under counterfactual evidence changes. We introduce CARGO-VL, a group-relative framework that optimizes matched variants covering aligned, image-correct, text-correct, and both-wrong (A/V/T/N) evidence states as one bundle. Its objective couples condition-wise correctness with transition rewards for answer invariance, source equivariance, and answer-to-abstention switching, while a primal-dual controller balances unsafe answers against excessive deferral. We also contribute XMC (eXtended Modal Conflict), a four-condition conflict training resource, and evaluate transfer on CMC-Bench and Modality-Bias. Across multiple seeds, CARGO-VL improves conflict handling, unsupported-answer avoidance, and modality balance over pointwise baselines. Ablations identify complementary benefits from relational transition signals and adaptive risk control, supporting counterfactual consistency as a practical objective for reliable multimodal evidence arbitration.

</details>


### [88] [Leak-Resistant Unlearning: A New Benchmark for Evaluating Multi-Hop Reasoning Consistency and Recovery Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04519)
*Haoting Qian,Qingjie Zhang,Zhicong Huang,Cheng Hong,Han Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new benchmark to test how robustly machine unlearning methods can remove knowledge from LLMs, especially under multi-hop reasoning and recovery attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unlearning benchmarks focus mainly on simple, single-hop questions and limited multi-hop cases, which do not capture how interconnected knowledge in LLMs can leak via alternative reasoning paths or be restored via post-unlearning adaptation. There is a need to rigorously test whether unlearning truly and robustly removes sensitive knowledge rather than only passing static, narrow evaluations.

Method: The authors design a benchmark (\unlearning) that systematically probes LLMs with diverse multi-hop reasoning paths and simulates recovery attacks, such as lightweight fine-tuning or adaptation after unlearning. They evaluate 3 LLMs and 6 representative unlearning methods using 2 carefully curated datasets, and define metrics to capture forget quality, robustness under attacks, and remaining model utility.

Result: Experiments show that current unlearning methods often fail under more complex multi-hop queries and are susceptible to recovery attacks, which can partially restore the supposedly forgotten knowledge. This reveals significant gaps between claimed unlearning performance on static benchmarks and actual robustness in more realistic, adversarial settings.

Conclusion: The benchmark demonstrates that existing LLM unlearning techniques are not robust: they leak information through alternative reasoning paths and are vulnerable to post-unlearning recovery. The authors highlight the need to consider a three-way trade-off between how well models forget, how robust that forgetting is to attacks, and how much utility is retained, and position their benchmark as a tool for guiding future, more reliable unlearning methods.

Abstract: Benchmarking machine unlearning methods is critical to understand whether sensitive knowledge is removed from large language models (LLMs) or not. Current unlearning benchmarks include mainly single-hop questions and a narrow set of multi-hop questions. Although effective, they still face two challenges. (1) Knowledge is not isolated, whereby diverse multi-hop reasoning paths can potentially induce knowledge leakage than normal queries. (2) Unlearning may be fragile: unlearned knowledge can be partially recovered through recovery attacks such as lightweight post-unlearning adaptation, making static evaluation insufficient. Therefore, in this paper, we introduce \unlearning as a novel benchmark to understand robust LLM knowledge removal across diverse reasoning paths and recovery attacks. We experiment with this benchmark on 3 models, 6 unlearning methods, and 2 carefully curated datasets. Results show that existing methods are vulnerable to multi-hop reasoning paths and recovery attacks. We further explore the trade-off among forget quality, robustness, and model utility for LLM unlearning.

</details>


### [89] [What Is a Skill Worth? Structure-Aware Shapley Valuation of Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04562)
*Tao Li,Junfeng Liu,Qinghua Zhao,Yifan Li,Lei Wang,Bo Shao,Xuejun Liu,Linjun Shou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents SkillSV, a Shapley-value-based, structure-aware framework to quantify the contribution of individual components within complex agent skills, enabling better understanding, pruning, and compression of these skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agent skills become complex structured artifacts optimized by automated feedback loops, it is difficult to understand which internal components (rules, examples, scripts, heuristics) are actually valuable, especially under a fixed agent and task distribution. Existing data or prompt-span valuation methods are inadequate because they ignore the structured dependencies, hierarchies, and context limitations of skills. A dedicated valuation method is needed to assign credit accurately to internal skill units and support safe editing and compression.

Method: The authors define the problem of skill valuation for fixed skills and propose SkillSV, a Shapley-style valuation framework tailored to structured skills. SkillSV first compiles a skill into discrete units with explicit dependencies and hierarchical organization, ensuring that counterfactual skills (where some units are added or removed) remain valid. It uses paired deletion and length-neutral padding to disentangle the intrinsic value of content from its cost in limited prompt context. Because agent evaluations are noisy and expensive, SkillSV employs a rollout-budgeted estimator to approximate Shapley values efficiently. The resulting per-unit scores capture both direct contributions and interaction effects among units.

Result: On four agentic benchmarks, SkillSV is empirically evaluated for faithfulness (how well valuations reflect true contributions), actionability (whether scores guide beneficial edits), and explanatory power (whether they reveal internal structure and interactions). Experiments show that SkillSV recovers known interactions between units, preserves the overall performance lift of the original skill when aggregating unit values, and effectively guides safe pruning and compression decisions without degrading agent performance.

Conclusion: SkillSV provides a principled, structure-aware way to assign credit to components of complex agent skills, improving interpretability and editability over existing valuation methods that ignore structure. By accounting for dependencies, hierarchy, and context cost while using efficient Shapley-style estimation, SkillSV yields faithful, actionable valuations that help understand, prune, and compress skills in agentic systems.

Abstract: Agent skills are increasingly optimized by automated feedback loops, producing long structured artifacts whose internal value remains unclear. We study skill valuation: assigning credit to the internal units of a fixed skill, such as rules, examples, scripts, and heuristics, under a fixed agent and held-out task distribution. Skill valuation differs from data or prompt-span valuation because skill units are structured: they may depend on other units, belong to a document hierarchy, trigger agent behavior, and consume limited prompt context. We introduce SkillSV, a structure-aware Shapley-style framework for skill valuation. SkillSV compiles a skill into units, dependencies, and hierarchy, so that only valid counterfactual skills are evaluated. It uses paired deletion and length-neutral padding to separate content value from context cost, and estimates the resulting values with a rollout-budgeted estimator for noisy agent evaluations. On four agentic benchmarks, we assess the faithfulness, actionability, and explanation of SkillSV: it recovers unit interactions, preserves aggregate skill lift, and guides safe pruning and compression.

</details>


### [90] [Joint UAV Flight and Opportunistic Routing under Reinforcement Learning for Delay-Tolerant Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04590)
*Xiao Wang,Shun-Ren Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes JUROR, a joint UAV flight control and opportunistic routing approach for delay-tolerant networks, optimized via PPO with centralized training and decentralized execution, to improve delivery performance under sparse connectivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Delay-tolerant networks face sparse delivery and congestion due to intermittent contacts, finite buffers, and limited message TTL. Existing routing approaches under such constraints struggle to maintain good end-to-end performance, especially when relying only on store-carry-forward in sparsely connected environments. There is a need to jointly leverage controllable UAV mobility and decentralized routing decisions to enlarge contact opportunities and improve performance while preserving decentralized operation.

Method: The authors formulate the joint UAV flight and routing problem as a factored partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP) with sequential coupling between motion and routing and a per-step team reward. They adopt a centralized training and decentralized execution (CTDE) multi-agent reinforcement learning paradigm using a PPO-based framework. Decentralized actors make decisions based on local observations, while a centralized critic uses global network statistics during training. Additionally, they integrate an optional multi-horizon hotspot predictor to offer auxiliary supervision on likely future contact hotspots.

Result: Simulation experiments under four distinct traffic modes show that the proposed JUROR scheme achieves significant performance gains compared with well-known DTN routing protocols PRoPHET and MaxProp, while still operating under the constraint of contact-limited decentralized execution.

Conclusion: Joint optimization of UAV flight control and opportunistic routing via a CTDE PPO-based framework can substantially improve performance in DTNs with sparse connectivity. By modeling the setting as a factored POMDP and using decentralized actors with a centralized critic (and optional hotspot prediction), JUROR effectively increases contact opportunities and outperforms classic DTN routing schemes in simulations.

Abstract: The growing deployment of delay-tolerant networks (DTNs) has made store-carry-forward (SCF) communication indispensable under sparse connectivity.
  However, intermittent contacts, finite buffers, and limited message time-to-live (TTL) often give rise to sparse delivery and congestion, leading to substantial end-to-end performance degradation.
  To address this challenge, this study explores the joint optimization of decentralized opportunistic routing and controllable unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) flight, aiming to enlarge future contacts through discrete UAV headings while enabling per-node replication under contact-limited observations.
  Building upon this architecture, we study cooperative factored routing--UAV control under centralized training and decentralized execution (CTDE) and propose JUROR (Joint UAV flight and Opportunistic Routing, based on the proximal policy optimization (PPO) framework.
  In our design, we first cast the problem as a factored partially observable Markov decision process with sequential motion--routing coupling and a per-step team reward; subsequently, decentralized actors act on local observations while a training-time critic uses global statistics, and an optional multi-horizon hotspot predictor provides auxiliary supervision.
  Simulation results over four traffic modes demonstrate effective gains over PRoPHET and MaxProp, while retaining contact-limited decentralized execution.

</details>


### [91] [Agreement Before Diversity: Verification-First Complementarity for Heterogeneous Language-Model Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04618)
*Ruitong Li,Binjie Guo,Aisheng Mo,Guowei Su,Jie Li,Ru Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ABD is a decision rule for ensemble language models that keeps an anchor answer if corroborated by trusted samples; otherwise it synthesizes a new heterogeneous answer, with formal identities relating its accuracy and cost to agreement coverage and anchor quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensembles of heterogeneous language models can produce diverse candidate responses, but lack a principled, auditable rule for when a new synthesized answer should replace an existing one. The paper aims to formalize this replacement authority and understand how verification and agreement structure impact accuracy and cost, without relying on independence or calibrated confidence assumptions.

Method: The authors introduce Agreement-Before-Diversity (ABD), a frozen, label-free decision rule. An anchor answer is chosen, and two additional trusted samples are drawn. If both corroborate the anchor under a predefined equivalence relation, the anchor is retained; otherwise, a heterogeneous synthesis is performed to generate a replacement answer. They develop a gating mechanism around this rule and prove two exact identities that characterize ABD’s accuracy relative to unconditional synthesis and to never synthesizing, in terms of agreement coverage, anchor advantage on a protected subset, and notions of authorized recovery/destruction. They then evaluate ABD on benchmarks such as LiveCodeBench-v6 and GPQA-Diamond under blind, exact-ID evaluation with controlled experimental conditions.

Result: ABD achieves higher accuracies than baselines: 59.43% on LiveCodeBench-v6 compared to 52.57% for a strong single model (Single9) and 52.00% for HAC, and 75.00% on GPQA-Diamond versus 72.78% for both controls. Theoretical identities show that the performance differences can be exactly attributed to specific protected strata defined by the gate’s coverage and behavior, and that expected inference cost is about eight minus five times the agreement coverage in model calls. Empirically, they observe zero discordant items in the protected stratum on LiveCodeBench, and localized discordances on GPQA-Diamond and under anchor perturbations, supporting their analytical framework.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that merely increasing diversity in heterogeneous ensembles is insufficient; what matters is the authority structure governing when diversity can override existing answers. ABD provides such a structure: a transparent, auditable decision rule that uses agreement among trusted samples as a prerequisite for avoiding synthesis, and otherwise delegates to a heterogeneous synthesis. Their theoretical identities and empirical evaluations demonstrate that this approach can improve accuracy and make performance and cost trade-offs precisely analyzable at the level of specific protected strata. The overarching message is that diversity offers potential candidates, but a principled verification mechanism like ABD is needed to grant replacement authority.

Abstract: Heterogeneous language-model ensembles expand the space of candidate responses, yet they lack a principled criterion for when a newly generated answer should supersede an already supported one. We decouple candidate headroom from replacement authority, rendering the latter as an explicit, auditable object. Our proposed method, Agreement-Before-Diversity (ABD), is a frozen, label-free decision rule: an anchor answer is retained if two additional trusted samples corroborate it under a fixed equivalence relation; otherwise, it is replaced by a heterogeneous synthesis. For this gating mechanism, we prove two exact identities. The first shows that the accuracy gap relative to unconditional synthesis is determined jointly by the agreement coverage and the anchor's advantage on the protected subset. The second shows that the gap relative to never synthesizing reflects a contrast between authorized recovery and authorized destruction. Neither identity assumes independence or calibrated confidence, and the expected inference cost is approximately eight minus five times the coverage in number of calls. Under blind, exact-ID evaluation, ABD achieves 59.43% on the complete LiveCodeBench-v6 (vs. 52.57% for Single9 and 52.00% for HAC; n = 175) and 75.00% on an untouched GPQA-Diamond split (both controls at 72.78%; n = 180). Furthermore, these identities localize every aggregate difference to an enumerable protected stratum: no discordant items occur among the 3 protected cases on LiveCodeBench, where coverage bounds the gate's contribution to 1.71 points a priori; 13 versus 8 discordant cases among 132 on GPQA-Diamond; and 12 versus 0 among 71 under a frozen anchor perturbation. Diversity supplies potential; verification structure supplies authority.

</details>


### [92] [A/B Agent: A Self-Evolving Agent for Strategy Iteration in Industrial A/B Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04625)
*Zhuohang Jiang,Yuxin Chen,Yongsen Pan,Zheng Hu,Wenqi Fan,Qing Li,Hongyang Wang,Jun Wang,Wenwu Ou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose an AI A/B-testing agent that organizes historical recommendation strategies hierarchically and uses them, plus new A/B feedback, to automatically generate and iteratively improve recommendation strategies, yielding significant GMV gains in production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial recommendation systems depend on continuous A/B testing to improve strategies, but current practice is expert-driven, repetitive, and slow. Historical experiment knowledge is fragmented and hard to reuse. Existing RAG-based agents only retrieve prior strategies in a flat way, ignoring hierarchical structure across business scenarios, stages, and objectives, leading to poor retrieval, weak cross-scenario transfer, and no true closed-loop learning from sequential A/B results.

Method: They design A/B Agent, a closed-loop system with three components: (1) Historical Strategy Knowledge Organization: encoding past recommendation strategies and experiments into a hierarchical experience tree over scenarios, stages, objectives, and contexts; (2) Autonomous Target-Aware Strategy Generation: using a Tree-RAG process that traverses multiple paths in the experience tree to retrieve relevant, transferable evidence and synthesize executable recommendation strategies for new tasks; (3) Experiment-Guided Strategy Self-Evolution: continuously analyzing online A/B feedback, autonomously tuning strategy parameters, and updating the experience tree so the agent improves over time.

Result: Through extensive offline simulations and online deployment in a real short-video e-commerce recommender, A/B Agent improves GMV by 4.829% while also preserving positive performance on all guardrail metrics, demonstrating better effectiveness than baseline approaches.

Conclusion: A/B Agent enables closed-loop, data-driven optimization of industrial recommendation strategies by hierarchically structuring historical experiment knowledge, generating target-aware strategies via Tree-RAG, and self-evolving from ongoing A/B feedback, leading to significant real-world business gains and robust performance across metrics.

Abstract: Industrial recommendation strategy iteration heavily relies on large-scale A/B experimentation. Traditional tuning requires experts to repeatedly design strategies, configure experiments, analyze results, and adjust parameters, making the process labor-intensive and time-consuming. Meanwhile, valuable knowledge from historical experiments is often fragmented, making systematic reuse difficult through manual expert effort alone. Existing RAG agents partially alleviate this burden by retrieving prior strategies, but typically organize experience in a flat manner, overlooking the hierarchical relationships among business scenarios, recommendation stages, optimization objectives, and experimental contexts. This often results in mismatched retrieval and limited cross-scenario transfer, while preventing agents from continuously refining strategies and parameters through sequential A/B feedback. %
To address these limitations, we propose A/B Agent, a closed-loop A/B agent for industrial recommendation strategy optimization. The framework comprises three tightly coupled core components: Historical Strategy Knowledge Organization, Autonomous Target-Aware Strategy Generation, and Experiment-Guided Strategy Self-Evolution. It organizes historical strategies into a hierarchical experience tree, retrieves transferable evidence through multi-path Tree-RAG to generate executable strategies, and continuously analyzes online A/B feedback to guide autonomous tuning and update the experience tree for self-evolution. Extensive offline and online evaluations demonstrate its effectiveness, including a 4.829% improvement in GMV in a real-world short-video e-commerce recommendation system while maintaining positive gains across all guardrail metrics.

</details>


### [93] [AI Literacy for Legal Translation: Developing Digital Resilience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04641)
*Łucja Biel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how generative AI affects professional legal translation and proposes an AI literacy framework with four dimensions to help legal translators use AI critically and responsibly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the growing use of generative AI in legal translation, which brings not only efficiency and new possibilities but also linguistic, technical, legal, ethical, and cognitive risks, and to clarify what new competences legal translators need.

Method: Conceptual and pedagogical analysis: the authors examine the impact of AI on legal translation practice, theorize AI literacy as part of legal translation competence, structure it into four dimensions (foundational, procedural, critical, strategic), and derive teaching activities to build these literacies in translator education.

Result: A structured AI literacy framework specifically tailored to legal translators is proposed, conceptualized as a transversal competence supporting digital resilience, along with concrete examples of classroom activities to cultivate each literacy dimension in training programs.

Conclusion: AI does not alter the core goals of legal translation but demands an expanded professional competence centered on AI literacy. By embedding the four-dimensional AI literacy framework into legal translator education, future professionals can integrate AI tools in ways that are critical, responsible, and aligned with professional and ethical standards.

Abstract: Generative AI is transforming legal translation by introducing opportunities alongside linguistic, technical, legal, ethical and cognitive risks. This chapter examines the implications of AI for professional legal translation and proposes an AI literacy framework tailored to the profession. It argues that AI does not change the fundamental objectives of legal translation but requires an extension of professional competence through AI literacy. The proposed framework comprises four mutually reinforcing dimensions, foundational, procedural, critical and strategic, and conceptualises AI literacy as a transversal component of legal translation competence that fosters digital resilience. It further discusses the pedagogical implications of this framework by proposing classroom activities designed to develop AI literacy in legal translator education, enabling future translators to integrate AI critically, responsibly and in accordance with professional standards.

</details>


### [94] [Calibrating Artificial Guilt: Neurally Grounded Reward Shaping for Prosocial Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04663)
*Aaditya Mehta,Arya Shah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They calibrate a ‘guilt’ weighting for social outcomes from human fMRI/behavior data and use it to shape rewards in multi-agent RL, finding it makes agents’ choices more human-like than standard hand-tuned shaping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In cooperative multi-agent RL, prosocial or social preferences are usually injected via ad-hoc reward shaping coefficients whose scale is chosen by hand. The authors want a principled, data-driven way to set these social reward weights, and they test whether human neural and behavioural responses to social outcomes can quantitatively calibrate a guilt signal that can then be transferred to artificial agents.

Method: They analyze the SoDec responsibility fMRI dataset with 40 participants, running a subject fixed-effects regression of changes in momentary happiness on different outcome-type counts. From the regression they derive a ‘guilt weight’ defined as the contrast between Partner-negative and Social-negative outcomes. They then build a two-agent Social Lottery environment and train independent PPO actor-critic agents under four reward shaping regimes: (1) neurally calibrated guilt weight, (2) uniform constant shaping, (3) no shaping (selfish), and (4) an oracle shaping with unit coefficient. They evaluate each condition over 1,000 episodes, comparing agents’ safe-choice rates against human data via KL divergence.

Result: The regression yields a guilt weight of about 1.118 with a small-to-moderate effect size (Cohen’s d=0.214). When this neurally calibrated guilt weight is used to shape rewards in the Social Lottery, the resulting agents’ rate of choosing the socially safe option (0.459) closely matches the human rate (0.484) and produces a very small KL divergence (0.0012). The other three shaping regimes yield choice distributions that diverge from humans by one to three orders of magnitude more in KL.

Conclusion: Human neurobehavioural data can be used to quantitatively calibrate social preference signals (like guilt) for multi-agent RL reward shaping. When applied in a simple social decision environment, the neurally calibrated weight produces agent behaviour that closely tracks human choices, outperforming common hand-tuned or unshaped baselines. This suggests that human-derived priors can serve as effective constraints for designing prosocial artificial agents.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning often adds social terms to individual rewards, yet the scale of those terms is usually chosen by hand. We ask whether a guilt signal can instead be calibrated from human neural and behavioural data and transferred to artificial agents. Using the public SoDec responsibility fMRI dataset (40 participants), we fit a subject-fixed-effects regression of momentary-happiness changes on outcome-type counts and recover a guilt weight as the Partner-negative minus Social-negative contrast ($\hat{w}=1.118$, Cohen's $d=0.214$). We embed this weight in a two-agent Social Lottery environment and train independent Proximal Policy Optimization actor-critics under four shaping regimes: neurally calibrated, uniform constant, zero (selfish), and a unit-coefficient oracle. Across 1{,}000 evaluation episodes per condition, the calibrated agents track the human Social safe-choice rate most closely ($0.459$ vs.\ human $0.484$; $\mathrm{KL}=0.0012$), while the other three conditions deviate by one to three orders of magnitude in KL. Human neurobehavioural priors can therefore act as quantitative constraints on prosocial reward shaping.

</details>


### [95] [Traceable LLM-Generated Hazard Scenarios for Operational Safety Analysis of Aviation Systems Using ASRS Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04697)
*Cristian Mascia,Roberto Pietrantuono,Daniel Rodriguez,Stefano Russo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI-assisted method to automatically generate realistic aviation hazard scenarios from pilot safety reports, structuring contributing factors and narratives, and studying how LLM configuration affects scenario quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional operational hazard analysis in aviation must reason about complex interactions between weather, ATC, airspace, aircraft operations, and human factors, which is different from aircraft-level functional hazard assessment and is difficult and labor-intensive to do manually from large text corpora like NASA ASRS reports. There is a need for scalable, systematic, and traceable ways to propose candidate hazard scenarios for safety assessment.

Method: Use large language models on NASA’s ASRS free-text incident reports to: (1) take a specified adverse outcome and infer a structured set of categorical contributing factors plus a coherent event-sequence narrative; (2) assign a plausibility score based on historical co-occurrence statistics and link each generated scenario to similar real ASRS reports for traceability; (3) develop a hybrid “evolutionary abduction” variant where the LLM first generates a structured hypothesis, then conditions narrative generation on that structure to improve coherence and consistency; and (4) experimentally compare multiple LLMs, zero- vs few-shot prompting, and with/without fine-tuning, evaluating the realism and validity of both the factor structures and narratives.

Result: The approach can automatically generate hazard scenarios with associated contributing-factor structures, plausibility scores, and links to real ASRS reports. The hybrid evolutionary-abduction variant that conditions narrative generation on an evolved structured hypothesis improves scenario correctness and reduces variability in generated narratives. Different LLMs, prompting strategies, and fine-tuning settings significantly affect the validity and realism of outputs, demonstrating the importance of configuration choices.

Conclusion: AI-assisted generation of operational hazard scenarios from large safety-report corpora is feasible and can support aviation safety analysis by proposing structured, plausible, and traceable hazard hypotheses. Conditioning narratives on separately generated structured hypotheses (evolutionary abduction) yields higher-quality scenarios, and careful selection of model and prompting strategy is critical for obtaining realistic, valid results suitable for use in safety engineering workflows.

Abstract: Operational hazard analysis of aviation system operations must consider interactions among weather, ATC actions, airspace constraints, aircraft operations, and human factors - distinct from the functional hazard assessment applied at the aircraft-system level. We present an AI-assisted approach that generates candidate hazard scenarios from NASA's Aviation Safety Reporting System (ASRS). Given a target adverse outcome, it produces a structured hypothesis as categorical factors and a narrative scenario describing an operational event sequence consistent with the structure. Each scenario includes by a plausibility score from historical co-occurrence evidence and traceability to the most similar held-out ASRS reports. We then propose a hybrid variant, conditioning narrative generation on a structured hypothesis produced via evolutionary abduction, improving correctness and reducing variability. We evaluate multiple large language models, zero-shot versus few-shot prompting, and optional fine-tuning, measuring how prompting and model choice affect the validity and realism of the generated structures and narratives.

</details>


### [96] [Diagnosing Tool-Selection Reasoning in LLM Agents with Canary Tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04719)
*Atul Anand,Sourav Chattaraj*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes “canary tools” as diagnostic probes embedded in tool-using LLM agents to reveal *how* and *why* they choose the wrong tools, offering a taxonomy and measurement framework for tool-selection vulnerabilities across models and capability tiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent evaluations usually only show that a language model chose an incorrect tool but provide little insight into the underlying reasoning failure. As tool-using agents become more prevalent and safety-critical, understanding specific tool-selection weaknesses is important for safety, reliability, and model comparison. The authors aim to move from a single coarse “wrong tool” signal to a finer-grained diagnostic of tool-use reasoning, and to determine how such weaknesses vary with model capability and deployment tier.

Method: The authors design “canary tools,” artificial diagnostic tools inserted into an agent’s Model Context Protocol tool set, each crafted to target a single type of tool-selection failure. They define six canary types: semantic decoys, parameter traps, capability mirages, prerequisite blindness, temporal decoys, and granularity traps. They then construct 120 tasks and run eight models (six hosted, two 8B open-weight, across three capability tiers) under three canary-density conditions and three random seeds, totaling 8,640 runs, plus a 2,880-run ablation where obvious “give-away” phrases in the canary descriptions are softened. Task outcomes are graded by an independent judge model and cross-checked by a second judge, with agreement quantified by Cohen’s kappa. They compute a canary susceptibility rate (CSR) per task and analyze how it varies by model, tier, and canary type, and how it correlates with task success.

Result: They find large differences in canary susceptibility across models: the per-task CSR spans about a 36-fold range, with Claude Opus 4.8 the least susceptible and Llama 3.1 8B the most. However, higher capability tier does not monotonically imply better safety: some mid-tier hosted models are more vulnerable than both cheaper and more advanced siblings, and within a provider the less expensive model can be safer. The canary taxonomy behaves in a capability-stratified way: “capability mirage” canaries most effectively trap frontier models, while the other categories mostly fail to trigger on strong models but reliably fire on smaller open models, indicating they discriminate by capability rather than being intrinsically weak probes. Softening the canaries’ explicit cue phrases barely changes frontier models’ CSR, suggesting that the probes capture reasoning failures rather than simple phrase-matching. Susceptibility negatively correlates with task success (Spearman ρ = -0.34), and the most robust models maintain performance even under heavy canary pressure. The authors also release their framework, canary schemas, tasks, and logs.

Conclusion: Canary tools provide a structured, multi-dimensional way to diagnose how LLM agents reason about and choose tools, going beyond coarse “right vs wrong tool” metrics. The study shows that tool-selection vulnerabilities vary widely across models and are not fully captured by capability tier, and that different failure modes dominate for different capability levels. The relatively stable susceptibility under softened cues supports that canaries test reasoning instead of surface heuristics. These findings suggest that fine-grained tool-use diagnostics are necessary for safety evaluation, that cheaper models may sometimes be safer for tool use, and that canary-based taxonomies can help benchmark and improve agent tool-selection robustness. The public release of artifacts enables further research and comparative evaluation.

Abstract: Agent evaluations tell us that a model picked the wrong tool, but rarely why. We introduce canary tools: diagnostic probe tools planted in an agent's Model Context Protocol (MCP) tool set, each engineered to probe one specific tool-selection weakness. A six-type taxonomy (semantic decoys, parameter traps, capability mirages, prerequisite blindness, temporal decoys, and granularity traps) turns a single "wrong tool" outcome into a multi-dimensional profile of how a model reasons about tools.
