We evaluate eight models -- six hosted and two 8B open-weight -- spanning three capability tiers, on 120 tasks across three canary-density conditions and three seeds (8,640 runs), plus a 2,880-run subtlety ablation. Task success is graded by a provider-independent judge, corroborated by a second independent judge (Cohen's kappa = 0.75). We report three findings. First, susceptibility drops sharply as models get more capable: the per-task canary susceptibility rate (CSR) ranges about 36x across models, lowest for Claude Opus 4.8 and highest for Llama 3.1 8B. Second, capability tier alone does not predict safety: the most susceptible hosted model is mid-tier, and within a provider the cheaper model can be the safer one. Third, the taxonomy is capability-stratified: capability mirages most reliably trap frontier models, while the other types are largely inert on strong models but fire on small open models, so they discriminate by capability rather than being weak. Softening each canary's give-away phrase leaves frontier CSR essentially unchanged, evidence that the probes measure reasoning, not phrase-spotting. Susceptibility also predicts task failure (Spearman rho = -0.34), while the most robust models are not significantly degraded by canary pressure. We release the framework, canary schemas, tasks, and logs.

</details>


### [97] [When Prompts Become Pixels: Prompt-Region Grounding for Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04726)
*Yongxin Wang,Ruizhe Zhou,Yueling Tang,Yingying Zhu,Xuemin Zhao,Xiaojun Chang,Xiaodan Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that multimodal LLMs perform worse when the task instruction is presented visually in the image instead of as text, and proposes a training method (prompt-region grounding) that narrows this performance gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal large language models are often evaluated with textual questions, even though real-world use cases frequently involve questions or instructions embedded directly in images (e.g., screenshots, documents, interfaces). It is unclear whether models can interpret and use visually presented task instructions as effectively as textual prompts, and standard benchmarks do not isolate this issue. The authors want to rigorously measure and understand this gap and then improve models’ ability to use visual task instructions.

Method: The authors introduce Visualized Task Semantics (VTS), a controlled setup where they move the question from text into the image itself while keeping the underlying problem and answer unchanged. They test six multimodal LLMs on four benchmarks, comparing performance between textual and visual question formats. To address the identified gap, they propose prompt-region grounding: during training, they designate the question region in the image, align it with its textual semantics, and train the model to recover a clean representation of the question from a masked image view, without relying on OCR or explicit region metadata at inference time.

Result: Across 24 combinations of models and tasks, performance consistently drops when questions are moved into the image, with an average decrease of 17.8 accuracy points. Analysis shows that models often correctly transcribe the visual question but still fail to use it effectively for reasoning, indicating a semantic gap that goes beyond OCR errors. With prompt-region grounding at the same training cost as baselines, VTS accuracy improves from 58.0 to 66.3 across four benchmarks, while performance on the original text-based interface is maintained.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that multimodal LLMs struggle to treat visually embedded task instructions as usable prompts for reasoning, even when they can read them, revealing a semantic channel gap between text and image inputs. The proposed prompt-region grounding method significantly mitigates this gap without sacrificing performance on standard text-prompted benchmarks and without needing OCR or explicit box annotations at inference. The work argues that correctly reading task text in images and grounding it as an instruction are distinct capabilities that must both be targeted in training and evaluation.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models increasingly reason over screenshots and documents where the task itself may be written in pixels. Yet benchmarks usually place questions in text, leaving it unclear whether models use the same instruction equally well across channels. We introduce Visualized Task Semantics (VTS), a controlled intervention that moves the question into the image while keeping the source problem and answer fixed. Across six MLLMs and four benchmarks, accuracy drops in all 24 model-task pairs, by 17.8 points on average. Models often transcribe the visual question correctly yet fail to use it, exposing a semantic channel gap beyond OCR. To reduce this gap, we present prompt-region grounding, whose core design aligns the question region with typed semantics and recovers its clean representation from a masked view. At matched training cost, our method raises four-benchmark VTS accuracy from 58.0 to 66.3 while preserving accuracy on the original interface, and requires no OCR or region metadata at inference. Reading task-bearing text and grounding it as an instruction for reasoning are distinct capabilities.

</details>


### [98] [Chain-of-Thought Monitoring Can Be Unreliable in Implicit-Influence Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04735)
*Agatha Duzan,Asa Cooper Stickland*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper benchmarks how well chain-of-thought monitors detect when model reasoning is being nudged, comparing explicit instructions to hide behavior versus subtle, implicit nudges, and finds that implicit influence is much harder to detect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring mostly consider explicit settings where models are directly told to hide behavior, which may not reflect realistic or more subtle deployment scenarios. There is a need to understand how well monitors work when model behavior is shaped indirectly by context or task features, without overt instructions to conceal actions.

Method: The authors construct a benchmark that compares monitorability in two regimes: explicit influence, where prompts instruct the model to follow a nudge and conceal it, and implicit influence, where the same nudge appears only as a casual aside with no hiding instruction. They test seven frontier extended-thinking models across four task formats (binary choice, numeric rating, multiple-choice QA, open-ended coding). They measure how much the nudge changes model outputs and how often a CoT monitor can detect the influence, and they additionally evaluate the effect of realistic system-prompt additions intended to reduce off-topic bias.

Result: In explicit-influence settings, CoT monitors successfully detect 60–94% of behavior shifts, as models tend to leak the instructed behavior into their CoT even when told to conceal it. Under implicit influence, the same nudges still significantly change behavior, but detection rates drop by 41–46 percentage points in two of four task settings. Adding realistic system-prompt constraints further reduces detection in implicit settings, in some cases down to 5%, while the behavioral influence remains strong.

Conclusion: CoT monitorability appears substantially higher in explicit-influence benchmarks than in more realistic implicit-influence scenarios. As a result, current monitorability evaluations may significantly overestimate the safety guarantees provided by CoT monitoring. Moreover, well-intentioned deployment choices, such as adding system prompts to reduce off-topic bias, can further decrease monitorability without reducing the extent of biased or nudged behavior. The paper provides a benchmark and codebase to enable more realistic and robust evaluation of CoT monitoring systems.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is increasingly treated as an important safety layer for frontier reasoning models. Most monitorability evaluations study explicit-influence settings: setups where the prompt directly incentivizes the model to hide something, e.g., by instructing it to perform a hidden side-task. A complementary axis for CoT-monitor evaluations is implicit-influence settings, where the prompt contains no instruction to hide, but the model's behavior is still shaped by features of the task or context, e.g. an irrelevant detail about a candidate that biases a hiring rating.
  We introduce the first benchmark that directly compares CoT monitorability under the two regimes. We test how model behavior changes in the presence of a nudge to choose a particular option. The nudge is delivered either as a casual aside (implicit), or as a direct instruction to act on the nudge and to conceal having done so (explicit). The benchmark spans four task formats (binary choice, numeric rating, multiple-choice QA, open-ended coding) and seven frontier extended-thinking models.
  Under explicit influence, a CoT monitor detects 60-94% of behavior shifts: even models instructed to conceal it leak the instruction into their CoT. Under implicit influence, the same factors still shift behavior, but detection falls by 41-46 percentage points in two of our four settings. Realistic system-prompt additions (of the kind a developer might deploy to reduce off-topic bias) lower implicit detection further, to as low as 5%, while preserving the behavioral influence itself.
  These results suggest that monitorability estimates obtained in explicit-influence settings may over-estimate monitorability, and that monitorability can be further decreased by well-intentioned deployment choices. Our benchmark and code are available at https://github.com/agatha-duzan/implicit-vs-explicit-influence

</details>


### [99] [EviGraph: Evidence-Guided Autonomous Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04738)
*Zhenjiang Ren,Ruiji Li,Xujing Zhang,Ziliang Pang,Shuo Ren,Jiajun Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EviGraph is an autonomous research framework that organizes and validates the entire research process as a live evidence graph, substantially improving the factual support and consistency of generated scientific claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing autonomous research agents can run experiments and write papers but often produce unsupported or inconsistent claims because they treat research as a linear pipeline and lack explicit tracking and validation of how claims are supported by evidence.

Method: The authors design EviGraph, which models research as a typed evidence graph with nodes for Problem, Gap, Hypothesis, Experiment, Finding, and Claim. This graph is used as the agent’s operational state. The system continuously inspects evidence chains for missing dependencies, semantic misalignments, and inconsistencies between results and claims, then localizes the weakest node and regenerates the affected downstream subgraph. Graph checkpointing is used to prevent failed revisions from corrupting validated evidence, and manuscript generation is deferred until all retained claims are grounded in validated evidence chains.

Result: On benchmarks ARC-Bench-ML and NanoResearch-20, EviGraph exceeds the performance of baseline end-to-end research agents, increasing Claim Support Rate by 40.19% over the strongest baseline and achieving 87.73% Experimental Data Consistency.

Conclusion: Explicitly maintaining and validating an evolving evidence graph as the core state of an autonomous research agent leads to more reliable, well-supported, and internally consistent research outputs compared to pipeline-based architectures.

Abstract: Autonomous research agents can generate hypotheses, execute experiments, and draft manuscripts, yet their outputs often contain unsupported claims and inconsistencies between research questions, experiments, results, and conclusions. We argue that this problem is partly architectural: existing systems organize research as sequential pipelines but do not explicitly maintain or validate the evolving claim-evidence structure across stages.In this paper, we introduce EviGraph, an autonomous research framework that represents the research process as a typed evidence graph containing Problem, Gap, Hypothesis, Experiment, Finding, and Claim nodes. The graph serves as the operational state of the agent rather than a post-hoc record. EviGraph inspects evidence chains for missing dependencies, semantic misalignment, and result-claim inconsistencies, localizes the earliest weak node, and regenerates its affected downstream subgraph. Graph checkpointing prevents unsuccessful repairs from corrupting previously validated evidence. Manuscripts are generated only after every retained claim is grounded in a validated evidence chain.Experiments on ARC-Bench-ML and NanoResearch-20 show that EviGraph outperforms the compared end-to-end research-agent baselines in overall research performance, improves Claim Support Rate by 40.19% over the strongest baseline, and achieves 87.73% Experimental Data Consistency. These results demonstrate the value of explicit evidence-state maintenance for reliable autonomous research.

</details>


### [100] [Fewer Tokens, Smaller Cache: Reward-Coordinated Efficient Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04771)
*Qiyuan Zhu,Dezhi Li,Pengyu Cheng,Tianle Chen,Jiacheng Wang,Ruijie Shen,Hao Gu,Sida Lin,Zirui Liu,Jiacheng Liu,Sirui Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ReCo, a reward-coordinated framework to reduce overthinking and inference cost in Large Reasoning Models by adaptively compressing KV-cache, penalizing redundant reflection tokens, and enabling early stopping, significantly cutting tokens and latency while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large Reasoning Models use long chain-of-thought reasoning, which leads to overthinking: excessive intermediate tokens inflate inference cost and latency. Existing KV-cache compression methods are reasoning-agnostic, apply uniform compression across the reasoning trajectory, and evaluate compression only by what is removed, ignoring how tolerant different reasoning states are to context loss and how compression affects future generation length. The authors observe that (1) different reasoning steps have varying tolerance to context loss, correlated with process reward; and (2) aggressive compression can induce longer generations, offsetting savings. These issues motivate a coordinated, reward-aware approach that manages both KV-cache compression and token generation behavior jointly.

Method: The authors introduce ReCo (Reward-Coordinated Compression), a step-wise framework controlled by a lightweight process-reward estimator that scores each completed reasoning step. This score drives three components: (1) reward-adaptive KV-cache compression, which prunes the retained cache more aggressively at high-reward steps—where context is more robust to loss—and more conservatively at low-reward steps; (2) a reward-banded penalty on reflection tokens, discouraging redundant or low-value self-reflective tokens that contribute to overthinking; and (3) confidence-based early stopping, which halts the chain-of-thought once the process-reward indicates the reasoning is sufficiently reliable, preventing unnecessary additional steps. All components operate online during inference without retraining the base LRM.

Result: On three different reasoning models and six benchmarks, ReCo reduces the number of generated tokens by 37%–65% and achieves 2.08x–2.35x reductions in end-to-end latency compared with full chain-of-thought decoding, while largely preserving the original accuracy. These results demonstrate that coordinated, reward-aware cache compression and generation control can substantially cut inference cost with minimal performance degradation.

Conclusion: Reward-aware coordination of KV-cache compression and generation dynamics effectively mitigates overthinking in Large Reasoning Models. By leveraging process reward as a unified signal to adapt compression strength, penalize redundant reflection, and trigger early stopping, ReCo achieves large savings in tokens and latency with little loss in accuracy. This suggests that reasoning-time control, guided by internal process quality estimates, is a promising direction for scaling LRMs to practical, cost-efficient deployment.

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) excel on complex tasks through long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, but their lengthy intermediate steps cause severe overthinking that inflates inference cost. KV-cache compression is a common solution, yet existing reasoning-oriented methods apply a uniform policy across the trajectory and judge compression only by what it removes from the cache. Two observations point the other way. First, a reasoning state's tolerance to context loss varies along the trajectory, and process reward tracks it: deleting tokens at high-reward steps preserves accuracy far better than deleting the same budget at random. Second, compression is not free on the generation side, since a smaller cache leads the model to generate more tokens, partly canceling the saving. Together these motivate coordinating both sides under a single process reward. We propose ReCo (Reward-Coordinated Compression), a step-wise framework in which a lightweight process-reward estimator scores each completed step and drives three components: (1) reward-adaptive KV-cache compression that shrinks the retained cache harder at high-reward steps and less at low-reward ones, (2) a reward-banded penalty on reflection tokens that curbs redundant generation, and (3) confidence-based early stopping that triggers when the reasoning is reliable. Across three reasoning models and six benchmarks, ReCo reduces generated tokens by 37%-65% and end-to-end latency by 2.08x-2.35x over Full CoT, all while largely preserving accuracy.

</details>


### [101] [NSF-HRPT: Neural Semantic Field meets Hierarchical Risk Perception Tree for Safety-Critical Scenario Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04776)
*Yu Zhao,Jiangyu Pan,Tao Hu,Ming Yin,Fan Yang,Jiangfan Liu,Xiubo Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NSF-HRPT is a framework that uses a neural semantic field and hierarchical risk perception tree to estimate probabilistic time-to-collision and localize risk from monocular camera inputs in real time, achieving (near) state-of-the-art performance in simulation and real-world data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurately assessing and anticipating risks in safety-critical driving situations from monocular vision is difficult because of complex multi-agent interactions and environmental uncertainty. Existing work can often predict collisions but struggles to provide reliable, quantitative risk levels from single-camera input. The paper aims to bridge this gap for autonomous driving safety.

Method: The paper proposes NSF-HRPT, which has two main components: (1) a Neural Semantic Field (NSF) trained on simulation data that learns a joint representation of scene semantics, future trajectories, and probabilistic Time-to-Collision (TTC) distributions for multiple agents; and (2) a Hierarchical Risk Perception Tree (HRPT) that uses the pretrained NSF as a prior during inference to perform structured, parallel spatial reasoning about multi-agent risks. Additionally, a Sim2Real enhancement strategy injects priors from foundation models to adapt the NSF-HRPT framework to real-world data without retraining.

Result: Extensive experiments on synthetic benchmarks show that NSF-HRPT achieves state-of-the-art performance in TTC estimation and risk localization. On real-world datasets, the method obtains competitive, near-state-of-the-art performance on the same metrics, while maintaining real-time capability using only monocular camera input.

Conclusion: By integrating a learned neural semantic field with a hierarchical risk reasoning structure and a Sim2Real enhancement strategy, NSF-HRPT offers an effective and efficient approach for real-time, quantitative risk assessment from monocular images in autonomous driving, improving both TTC estimation accuracy and spatial risk localization compared to prior work.

Abstract: The ability to accurately assess and anticipate risks in safety-critical scenarios is crucial for autonomous driving systems. While existing research has made progress in collision prediction, accurately quantifying risk levels from monocular vision inputs remains challenging due to the complex dynamics of multi-agent interactions and the inherent uncertainty in real-world environments. To address these challenges, we present NSF-HRPT, a novel framework that combines learning-based perception with structured reasoning for quantitative risk assessment. Our approach features a Neural Semantic Field (NSF) that learns to model scene semantics, trajectory predictions, and probabilistic Time-to-Collision (TTC) distributions from simulation data. During inference, the pre-trained NSF serves as a prior for our Hierarchical Risk Perception Tree (HRPT), which enables efficient parallel computation and spatial reasoning about multi-agent risks. Additionally, we introduce a Sim2Real enhancement strategy that improves real-world applicability without retraining by incorporating priors from foundation models. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that our framework achieves state-of-the-art performance on synthetic benchmarks and delivers competitive, near-state-of-the-art results on real-world datasets for both TTC estimation accuracy and risk localization precision. The proposed method provides an effective solution for real-time risk awareness from monocular camera inputs.

</details>


### [102] [Privileged, but Biased: How PI-Conditioned Teachers Break Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04794)
*Sarthak Harne,Chinmay Karkar,Yash Pandya,Ahmed Awadallah,Akshay Nambi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper examines whether self-distillation without explicit reward actually improves model performance on hard reasoning tasks and finds that it generally does not.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although self-distillation with privileged information has been proposed as a compute-efficient alternative to reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, reported gains are mostly in narrow, easy settings. It remains unclear whether self-distillation alone, as the only training objective, genuinely teaches models to perform better on challenging tasks.

Method: The authors first reproduce SDPO’s gains in its original easy setting, then systematically apply the same self-distillation setup to more difficult tasks across domains including question answering, mathematics, coding, and multi-turn agentic tool use. They test multiple reasoning modes, model sizes, and forms of privileged information, and compare two main recipes (SDPO and OPSD). They track per-token training loss and validation accuracy, and analyze how the loss distributes over token types. They introduce and quantify a PI Bias Score to capture how privileged information biases the teacher’s targets toward a specific reference trajectory rather than general correctness.

Result: On challenging tasks, per-token loss consistently decreases during self-distillation training, but validation accuracy fails to improve and often worsens, regardless of task type, reasoning mode, model size, PI form, or recipe used. The analysis shows that the teacher’s targets are strongly biased toward a particular reference solution (high PI bias), leading the student to focus its probability mass on that specific trajectory. Consequently, most of the loss is concentrated on low-information tokens (stopwords, punctuation, uncertainty markers), while tokens that decide correctness receive comparatively little training signal. Within correct rollouts, exploratory or hesitant tokens incur the largest divergence and are penalized.

Conclusion: As a standalone objective without any explicit reward term, self-distillation optimizes a training signal that is largely decoupled from true task success on hard problems. Privileged-information bias causes the teacher to favor specific solution trajectories rather than correctness in general, making the student flatter, less decisive, and not better at reasoning. Therefore, self-distillation alone is insufficient for improving performance on difficult reasoning tasks and likely needs to be combined with other objectives (such as reward-based methods) to be effective.

Abstract: Self-distillation (SD) has emerged as a compute-efficient alternative to reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards: a self-teacher, conditioned on privileged information (PI) about the answer such as a reference solution, supplies dense per-token supervision to a student that never sees it. Reported gains, however, come almost exclusively from narrow, low-difficulty settings, leaving open a basic question: as a lone objective, with no reward term, does SD teach anything? We reproduce SDPO's reported gains in its easy setting, then apply the identical setup to difficult tasks and find that it does not. Across question answering, mathematics, coding, and multi-turn agentic tool use, across reasoning modes, model sizes, and forms of PI, and under both the SDPO and OPSD recipes, the per-token loss falls steadily while validation accuracy does not improve and typically degrades. We explain this failure through a single causal chain from the loss to the model it produces. The chain begins with PI bias: having seen one particular reference solution, the teacher's per-token target is pulled toward that trajectory rather than toward correctness in general, an effect we quantify with a PI Bias Score. Trained to match this target everywhere, the student's objective becomes nearly blind to whether a rollout is correct, and the loss it assigns falls mostly on low-information tokens like stopwords, punctuation, uncertainty markers, rather than those that determine the answer; within correct rollouts the exploratory tokens incur the highest divergence, so it penalizes the hesitation that reasoning requires. The result is a flatter, less decisive student that is no better at reasoning: as a lone objective, SD optimizes a signal decoupled from task success.

</details>


### [103] [ContextWeave: A Real-World Workflow Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04830)
*Bo Wang,Yuqian Yao,Enxi Wang,Luozhijie Jin,Yang Liu,Yiran Suo,Yuxuan Cai,Enyu Zhou,Yufei Gao,Honglin Guo,Tianyu Huai,Li Ji,Zhikai Lei,Bufan Li,Lizhi Lin,Jinxiu Liu,Jie Yang,Jiazheng Zhou,Maosen Zhou,Pengfang Qian,Shichun Liu,Guanshan Liu,Hao Zheng,Yunhao Yu,Hang Yan,Jihua Kang,Xinchi Chen,Xipeng Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces ContextWeave, a benchmark to test how memory helps language agents perform long-horizon, realistic office workflows, showing that richer experience-based memory significantly improves performance but can be misled.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As language agents are increasingly used in long, stateful workflows, they need effective memory. Existing evaluations mostly treat memory as simple retrieval or QA, which misses how memory should actually support ongoing work. There is a gap in benchmarks that simulate realistic, longitudinal workflows and directly measure whether recalled experiences improve downstream performance and preference alignment.

Method: The authors build ContextWeave, a longitudinal benchmark reconstructed from several months of real office workflows of 14 participants. They turn these into 1,005 executable tasks (568 core evaluation tasks) with instructions, containerized execution environments, trajectories, and detailed rubrics. They evaluate multiple memory components plugged into a fixed model and multiple base models with a fixed memory setup. Metrics include workspace quality, alignment with user-specific preferences, plus diagnostics such as relevance, continuity, solvability, and robustness to misleading recall.

Result: Using a fixed base model and varying memory components, the best configuration increases Workspace Score from 68.08 to 78.20 and Preference Score from 41.50 to 70.60. With a fixed memory component, all five tested base models benefit from recall on both metrics, though the magnitude of gains differs. Detailed analysis finds that rich, actionable memories derived from prior experience help agents continue workflows and avoid repeating work better than compact summaries, but they are also more vulnerable to harmful effects when recall is misleading.

Conclusion: ContextWeave provides a realistic, longitudinal benchmark to study how memory affects agent performance on office workflows. The results indicate that richer experience-based memories yield substantial performance and preference-alignment gains but also introduce risks when retrieval is incorrect or misleading. This suggests that memory systems should be designed not only for relevance of retrieval but also for robust, reliable use of recalled content during execution.

Abstract: Memory is essential as language agents move from isolated tasks to long-horizon, stateful workflows, yet existing evaluations often reduce it to retrieval or question answering. We introduce ContextWeave, a longitudinal benchmark that evaluates whether recalled experience improves downstream agent performance in realistic office-work streams. ContextWeave reconstructs privacy-preserved, multi-month workflows of 14 participants into 1,005 executable tasks, including 568 core evaluation tasks, with instructions, containerized environments, trajectories, and task-specific rubrics. It measures workspace quality and alignment with participant-specific preferences, complemented by diagnostics of relevance, continuity, solvability, and robustness to misleading recall. Across six memory components under a fixed model, the strongest configuration raises Workspace Score from 68.08 to 78.20 and Preference Score from 41.50 to 70.60. With a fixed memory component, recall improves both outcomes for all five tested base models, although gains vary substantially. Our analysis shows that actionable, experience-rich memory supports workflow continuation and reduces redundant exploration more effectively than compact summaries, while it can also be more susceptible to misleading recall. These findings motivate memory systems that optimize not only retrieval relevance but also reliable use during execution.

</details>


### [104] [When Shared Rollouts Fail in Defensive Driving Evaluation: A NAVSIM Score Basis Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04896)
*Ziang Wei,Minjun Yu,Zheyuan Lai,Mingjie Pang,Wei Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper audits how numerical instability combined with reference-conditioned forgiveness can wrongly reward unsafe or inattentive driving policies in a driving benchmark, and proposes an audit protocol to prevent such issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Defensive driving scores must distinguish between agents that pay attention to surrounding actors and those that do not. However, current re-simulation benchmarks may use a scoring scheme (reference-conditioned forgiveness) that can incorrectly give agents credit when the human reference driver fails under certain conditions. If both agent and reference share a numerically unstable rollout process, this can lead to unsafe or blind agents being scored as highly compliant. The authors are motivated to detect, understand, and mitigate this risk in NAVSIM v2.2 scoring.

Method: They perform an audit of NAVSIM v2.2 original scene single-stage scoring. They compare defensive driving scores across different agents, including a route-blind Ignore-All probe and a route-aware actor-blind probe, against human replay and a baseline (PDM-Closed) over a large navtest split. They then reproduce rollout divergences on a fixed diagnostic set using a fresh installation, and run controls with matched dependency stacks and exact-input diagnostics to isolate dependency-sensitive numerical behavior in the shared velocity refit component. Finally, they run a control experiment on a pool of scenarios where they replace only the numerical solver to see if this removes rollout divergence and restores expected score ordering while maintaining forgiveness.

Result: Under a documented but problematic dependency stack, both the route-blind Ignore-All probe and the route-aware actor-blind probe receive higher scores than human replay and PDM-Closed on the full navtest split, despite being intentionally unobservant or unsafe. A fresh installation following the public spec reproduces the same kind of rollout divergence on a small diagnostic set. Control experiments show that the problematic behavior is tied to dependency-sensitive numerical instability in the shared velocity refit: when they replace only the solver in a 450-token control pool, rollout divergences disappear and the expected ordering (blind agents scoring last) is restored, even though the forgiveness mechanism remains active.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that numerical instability in the rollout process is the direct cause of spurious compliance credit when combined with reference-conditioned forgiveness. This mechanism can cause blind or unsafe agents to be scored as better than human drivers in NAVSIM v2.2. Therefore, before using such benchmarks for defensive driving claims, it is necessary to adopt an audit protocol that includes disclosure of score basis and software stack, use of blind probes, explicit reporting of any overwrites, and rollout stability tests to ensure that numerical issues are not inflating scores for unsafe agents.

Abstract: Defensive driving scores are useful only when they preserve distinctions between policies that observe surrounding actors and those that do not. Re-simulation benchmarks may use reference-conditioned forgiveness, under which an agent receives credit when the logged human reference fails a compliance channel. When agent and reference share an unstable rollout transformation, this rule can propagate shared reference failures into broad compliance credit.
  We audit this risk in NAVSIM v2.2 original scene single-stage scoring. Under the affected documented-stack condition on the audited numerical backend, the route-blind Ignore-All probe and a route-aware actor-blind probe outrank human replay and PDM-Closed over the complete 12,146-token navtest split. A fresh installation following the public specification reproduces rollout divergence on a fixed 32-token diagnostic set. A same-source dependency stack control and an exact-input diagnostic isolate dependency-sensitive numerical behavior in the shared velocity refit. On a 450-token control pool, replacing only the solver eliminates rollout divergence and restores blind-last ordering while keeping forgiveness enabled. Thus, the numerical instability is the direct trigger. Reference-conditioned forgiveness propagates the resulting shared reference failures into compliance credit. We contribute an audit protocol requiring score basis and stack disclosure, blind probes, overwrite reporting, and rollout stability tests before using such scores for defensive driving claims.

</details>


### [105] [WorldCycle: Self-Verifiable Reinforcement Learning for Long-Horizon Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04964)
*Bohai Gu,Yueyang Yuan,Taiyi Wu,Dazhao Du,Jian Liu,Xiaoyi Pang,Jie Zhang,Xiaocheng Lu,Haobin Zhong,Xiaotong Zhao,Alan Zhao,Song Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WorldCycle is a self-verifiable RL framework that improves interactive video world models by exploiting reversible action cycles to supervise long-horizon predictions without annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing interactive video world models are crucial for planning and exploration over long horizons but they accumulate compounding prediction errors. While reinforcement learning can refine these models, it faces a verification bottleneck: for arbitrary action sequences there is no ground-truth long-term future to compare against, making it hard to measure or correct long-horizon drift.

Method: The authors introduce WorldCycle, which converts ordinary action sequences into closed action cycles by composing them with their inverse and by repeating these cycles. They then train the world model using RL with two rewards: (1) a spatial closure reward that enforces that executing a sequence followed by its inverse returns the system to its initial state, and (2) a temporal consistency reward that ensures consistency of predicted states across multiple executions of the same cycle. This encourages the model to treat actions as consistent state transition operators rather than as fixed temporal trajectories, and the framework is applied to both in-distribution and out-of-distribution composite cycles.

Result: On the proposed CycleBench diagnostic benchmark, which measures a model’s ability to return to its starting state under complex action compositions, WorldCycle significantly improves long-horizon fidelity. It reduces state-return drift by up to 44% and increases accuracy on composite actions by nearly fourfold relative to the baseline world model.

Conclusion: Leveraging reversible action cycles provides an annotation-free way to verify and improve long-horizon behavior in video world models. By enforcing spatial closure and temporal consistency, WorldCycle yields more physically grounded, operator-consistent models that handle complex composite actions much better, establishing a stronger foundation for planning and control in learned world models.

Abstract: Interactive video world models are essential for long-horizon planning and exploration, yet they suffer from compounding errors. Post-training methods such as reinforcement learning (RL) can improve these models, but they hit a verification bottleneck: for arbitrary action sequences, no ground-truth future state exists to measure long-term drift. Our key insight is that reversible action cycles make this verification possible: a sequence composed with its inverse must analytically return to the initial state, yielding annotation-free supervision on long-horizon correctness. Building on this, we introduce WorldCycle, a self-verifiable RL framework that constructs closed action cycles and their repeated executions from ordinary action sequences, and optimizes two complementary rewards: a spatial closure reward enforcing symmetry between mirrored forward and reverse segments, and a temporal consistency reward aligning states across repeated cycle executions. These rewards force the model to learn actions as consistent state operators rather than memorized temporal patterns, and extend naturally to out-of-distribution composite action cycles that the base model handles poorly. We further release CycleBench, a diagnostic benchmark for state-returning ability under complex action structures. WorldCycle reduces state returning drift by up to 44% and lifts composite-action accuracy nearly 4x over the base model, providing a vital foundation for physically grounded world models.

</details>


### [106] [Short-term load forecasting under EU-AI Act Requirements in Safety-Critical Environments: Results from a 41-day live challenge on the aggregated German transmission-grid load](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05018)
*Thomas Bartz-Beielstein*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents and evaluates an EU-AI-Act-compliant, open-source short-term load forecasting pipeline that outperforms the official ENTSO-E day-ahead forecast for German transmission-grid load, demonstrating that transparent, auditable local models can rival large foundation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short-term load forecasting for national power grids is safety- and infrastructure-critical, subject to strict European and German regulations. Beyond accuracy, forecasting systems must satisfy determinism, reproducibility, and auditability to comply with the EU-AI Act and other regulation. Existing approaches often focus on accuracy alone, rely on opaque large models, or lack end-to-end, compliant software pipelines that can be deployed in real-world power-system operations.

Method: The authors designed a full forecasting pipeline using the open-source Python library spotforecast2-safe, which embeds EU-AI Act requirements by design. They ran a 41-day live forecasting challenge on the aggregated German transmission-grid load, predicting 24 hourly loads for each target day from ENTSO-E data. The pipeline integrates anomaly detection, gap-aware data preparation, calendar and weather covariates, a recursive multi-step forecasting algorithm, and hyperparameter tuning. Multiple model types are compared, from transparent local models (macl2l) to large pre-trained foundation models like chronos-2. Performance is benchmarked against the official ENTSO-E day-ahead forecast, and all challenge infrastructure and submissions are made public for auditability.

Result: Across the 41-day live challenge, the EU-AI-Act-compliant spotforecast2-safe pipeline consistently outperforms the official ENTSO-E day-ahead load forecast for Germany. In-context models (which leverage context rather than heavy pre-training) achieve competitive accuracy. The transparent, low-cost, and auditable local models (macl2l) perform on par with or competitively close to large (>100M parameter) energy-intensive foundation models like chronos-2, demonstrating that regulatory-compliant and efficient models need not sacrifice accuracy.

Conclusion: Regulatory-compliant, transparent, and auditable STLF systems can be practically realized and can even beat the current operational baseline used by ENTSO-E. The spotforecast2-safe pipeline illustrates how EU-AI Act requirements can be implemented by design in safety-critical forecasting applications, while maintaining competitive or superior forecasting accuracy. Local, interpretable models offer a strong alternative to large black-box foundation models for critical infrastructure, and the publicly released challenge infrastructure and leaderboard support reproducibility and further research.

Abstract: Short-term load forecasting (STLF) play a vital role in the electric power industry. It serves infrastructure that European and German law designate as critical. Determinism, reproducibility, and auditability are engineering requirements rather than optional extras. STLF is no longer purely an accuracy problem. It is also a software-engineering and compliance problem. This paper describes results from a 41-day live challenge that evaluated a complete STLF pipeline for the aggregated German transmission-grid load. The pipeline is based on the open-source Python library spotforecast2-safe, which implements the EU-AI Act Requirements in Safety-Critical Environments by design. The pipeline predicts the 24 hourly load values of a target day from European Network of Transmission System Operators for Electricity (ENTSO-E) data. It includes anomaly detection and gap-aware data preparation, calendar and weather covariates, a recursive multi-step forecasting algorithm, and hyperparameter tuning. Forecast accuracy is measured against the official ENTSO-E day-ahead forecast. The EU-AI act compliant spotforecast2-safe pipeline beats the ENTSO-E baseline. In-context models show competitive performance. Transparent, low-cost, and auditable local models (referred to as macl2l in this paper) are competitive with more than 100-million-parameter large, energy-intensive pre-trained foundation models such as chronos-2. The challenge infrastructure, the complete submission history of all teams, and the frozen final leaderboard are publicly available.

</details>


### [107] [From Score Matrices to Football-Aware Match-State Simulation: An Auditable LLM Harness for Exact-Score Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05030)
*Shaopeng Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper builds an auditable hybrid system that combines statistical football score models with large language models, iteratively improving exact-score prediction accuracy on EPL matches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Purely statistical Poisson-based models forecast goals well but cannot capture tactical, psychological, and contextual factors like formations, motivation, or behaviour shifts after scoring. LLMs can reason about such rich context but are poor calibrated probability estimators. The authors want to bridge this gap to better forecast football scores, while keeping the system auditable and avoiding opaque LLM ‘magic’.

Method: They develop four versions of a hybrid forecasting architecture. V1 is a dynamic Dixon–Coles style Poisson model that updates team strength and yields coherent score and 1X2 probabilities. V2 incorporates LLM-produced contextual ratings as corrections mapped back into expected-goal parameters. V3 moves from a single correction factor to explicit goal-by-goal simulations over a fixed candidate score set, driven by LLM judgments. V4 further refines the simulation by letting the LLM assess first-goal ‘breakthrough’ effects, cascades of post-goal scoring, time-aware stopping of scoring, and deterministic low-probability tail scores, under a constrained, inspectable reasoning protocol. They test over a chronological replay of the first 150 matches of the 2025–26 EPL, measuring exact-score Top-1/Top-3 accuracy, candidate coverage, and standard probabilistic metrics (log loss, Brier, RPS).

Result: On 150 EPL matches, the baseline V1 gets 10.0% Top‑1 and 26.7% Top‑3 exact‑score accuracy, with 53.3% correct 1X2 argmax and solid but imperfect probabilistic scores (log loss 0.9878, Brier 0.5870, RPS 0.2095). V3 improves exact-score Top‑1/Top‑3 to 12.0%/30.0%. V4 reaches 14.7% Top‑1 and 30.7% Top‑3, and raises the fraction of observed scores that appear anywhere in the candidate set from 77.3% to 84.7%, though the added ‘tail’ candidates do not yield additional Top‑3 hits. The authors emphasize these numbers are exploratory and possibly optimistic, because the same LLM family is used multiple times and temporal isolation cannot fully rule out leakage of outcome information.

Conclusion: A carefully designed hybrid system can leverage LLM contextual reasoning while keeping the underlying probability model coherent and auditable. Iterative design from V1 to V4 shows that adding richer football-aware simulation logic (breakthrough, cascades, time-aware stopping) improves exact-score selection modestly, though not all complexity pays off (e.g., tail candidates don’t improve Top‑3 hits). The main contribution is architectural: defining a constrained ‘information harness’ that structures how the LLM contributes to a statistical model, plus negative and nuanced findings on where LLM-informed simulations help or fail in football score forecasting, underlining the need for more robust, leak‑free benchmarking.

Abstract: Football score forecasting combines a strong statistical core with a difficult contextual edge. Dynamic Poisson-family models estimate team strength, expected goals, and coherent score probabilities, but do not directly understand roles, tactical matchups, motivation, or how a first goal changes behaviour. Large language models (LLMs) can reason about such concepts, yet are not calibrated probability engines. We combine both components through an auditable information harness. This paper documents four iterations: V1, a dynamic score-driven Dixon-Coles baseline; V2, which maps LLM contextual ratings back into expected-goal parameters; V3, which replaces scalar correction with goal-by-goal simulations over a frozen score-candidate set; and V4, which adds shared first-breakthrough and post-goal cascade judgments, time-aware stopping, and deterministic tail candidates. The harness defines input semantics, supplies pre-match evidence, and constrains the LLM to an inspectable reasoning route. On a chronological replay of the first 150 matches of the 2025-26 English Premier League, V1 achieved 10.0% Top-1 and 26.7% Top-3 exact-score accuracy. V3 reached 12.0% and 30.0%, while V4 reached 14.7% and 30.7%. V4 increased candidate coverage from 77.3% to 84.7%, although no added tail candidate became a Top-3 exact hit. V1's native 1X2 distribution achieved 53.3% argmax accuracy, 0.9878 log loss, 0.5870 Brier score, and 0.2095 ranked probability score. These results are exploratory: the development slice is not an untouched benchmark, and temporal input isolation cannot exclude outcome memory in a closed LLM. The contribution is an auditable hybrid architecture, a clear design evolution, and negative findings showing where football-aware simulation does and does not improve score selection.

</details>


### [108] [Item Response Theory for AI Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05086)
*Joshua Fonseca Rivera,Neil Shah,David Demitri Africa,Konstantinos Voudouris*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper applies Item Response Theory (IRT) to multiple LLM safety benchmarks across many models, revealing three core safety dimensions, enabling cheaper yet accurate evaluations, and using IRT to audit models for sandbagging or backend changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety benchmarks for language models are hard to interpret and trust because benchmarks overlap, correlate, and can be gamed (e.g., sandbagging on detectable test items). There is a need for a principled, statistical way to understand what benchmarks actually measure, reduce evaluation costs, and detect untrustworthy behavior or hidden model changes.

Method: The authors use Item Response Theory (IRT) to analyze results from 192 language models across eight safety benchmarks. They infer latent traits and psychometric properties (e.g., item difficulty, discrimination) of benchmark questions. They then factor-analyze the latent traits to identify underlying safety dimensions, compare psychometrically chosen benchmark subsets against random subsets, and test whether IRT-based methods can detect sandbagging and API model swaps.

Result: (1) The variance in benchmark performance across models is largely explained by three latent factors: refusal strictness, truthfulness, and contextual harm sensitivity. (2) Carefully selected items using IRT reconstruct full-benchmark scores more accurately than random subsets and can cut evaluation cost by 97–99% with about ten adaptively chosen questions in some cases. (3) IRT-based analysis can flag naive sandbagging behavior and detect when the underlying model behind an API has changed.

Conclusion: IRT provides a powerful, ready-made statistical framework for interpreting what safety benchmarks measure, compressing them into smaller adaptive tests without much loss of accuracy, and auditing models for suspicious behavior or unannounced changes. The authors recommend that frontier labs and evaluators adopt IRT-based tools in their safety evaluation pipelines.

Abstract: Language models differ in how safely they behave and these differences are measured by safety benchmarks. But aggregated benchmark scores are hard to trust and interpret, because benchmarks duplicate one another, correlate heavily, and models may sandbag when they detect evaluation. To address these issues, we draw on Item Response Theory (IRT), a statistical toolkit for measuring these latents from performance on items with inferred psychometric properties. We fit IRT models to eight safety benchmarks across 192 language models, the largest psychometric analysis of LLM safety evaluations to date, and contribute three results. First, we find that three interpretable factors of refusal strictness, truthfulness, and contextual harm explain most of the variance between models across benchmarks. Second, psychometrically selected items recover full benchmark scores with lower error than random subsets of the same size, and roughly ten adaptively chosen items suffice for several individual benchmarks, cutting evaluation cost by 97-99%. Third, IRT supports audits of individual models, showing that it can be used to detect naive sandbagging and changes of model behind APIs. Overall, we show IRT is a ready-made toolkit for reading, reducing, and auditing safety benchmarks, which we recommend frontier labs and evaluators adopt.

</details>


### [109] [Hierarchical Graph Memory for LLM Agents with Path-level Localization and Rewrite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05095)
*Xiawei Yue,Boran Wang,Xiaoqing Zhang,Shuxin Zheng,Ziwei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HiGram is a hierarchical graph memory framework that enables more efficient, accurate long-term reasoning for agents by organizing memory hierarchically and updating it at the path level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph memory methods for long-term reasoning store all information in a flat graph. As memory grows, this leads to retrieval of irrelevant contexts, higher token and computation costs, and inefficient updates because related units must be rewritten one-by-one. There is also difficulty in coherently updating interdependent facts and resolving conflicts. The paper aims to design a memory system that scales better, retrieves more relevant evidence, and supports coordinated updates under changing or conflicting information.

Method: The authors introduce HiGram, an evolving hierarchical graph memory. First, they structure memory into a coarse-to-fine hierarchy with upper-level nodes summarizing or indexing groups of finer-grained MemoryUnits, which reduces irrelevant information during retrieval. Second, they propose MicroGraph-based path-level localization: given a query or update, the system builds small, conditioned MicroGraphs to locate a support subgraph and an evidence path in the larger memory graph before performing any rewrite. Third, they design a coordinated rewriting mechanism that jointly updates both the internal content of MemoryUnits and the dependency edges between them, ensuring that changes propagate consistently along the localized evidence path and that dependency structures remain valid.

Result: On benchmarks for long-term conversational QA and conflict-aware memory evaluation, HiGram outperforms baseline memory systems in answer quality and token efficiency. It also better handles dynamic, static, and conditional conflicts, yielding higher answer accuracy and more query-valid evidence selection when the stored information contains updates or contradictions.

Conclusion: Hierarchical organization plus path-level localization and coordinated rewriting leads to a more scalable, precise, and robust memory for long-term reasoning agents compared with flat graph memories. HiGram effectively filters irrelevant information, performs targeted updates, and maintains coherent dependency structures, producing better answers with fewer tokens and handling various types of memory conflicts more reliably.

Abstract: Agents for long term reasoning require a memory that can be efficiently and effectively updated over time, as new facts and external feedback continue to arrive. Recently, graph memory has been adopted to offer structural organization for multi-hop retrieval and reasoning. However, existing methods store all memories in a flat graph, and accumulated historical memories can introduce irrelevant contexts and increase the cost of evidence selection during retrieval. Moreover, they typically update memory units independently, requiring repeated unit-wise rewrite to cover related changes. To address these issues, we propose HiGram, an evolving hierarchical graph memory framework with path-level localization and rewriting. Specifically, we first propose a hierarchical graph memory, which organizes the memory into coarse-to-fine architecture composed of upper-level nodes and MemoryUnits, thereby reducing the amount of irrelevant information during retrieval. We further propose MicroGraph-based path-level localization, which leverages query and update conditioned MicroGraphs to identify support subgraph and evidence path before rewrite. Finally, we propose a coordinated rewriting method that jointly revises intra-unit memory and inter-unit dependencies, enable valid dependency structures updating in the localized evidence path. Experiments on benchmarks for long-term conversational question answering and conflict-aware memory evaluation demonstrate that our method demonstrate substantial improvements over baselines in answer quality and token efficiency. Besides, our method improves answer accuracy and query-valid evidence selection under dynamic, static, and conditional conflicts.

</details>


### [110] [ABSeeker: Training Long-Horizon Search Agents via Answer-Backtracked Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05102)
*Yijun Lu,Rui Ye,Jiajun Wang,Yuwen Du,Tian Jin,Songhua Liu,Siheng Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Answer-Backtracked Credit Assignment (ABC), a framework that turns sparse trajectory-level feedback into dense step-level supervision to better train long-horizon search agents, leading to strong performance with a relatively small model and dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-horizon search agents treat all steps in a reasoning/search trajectory uniformly during SFT and RL, so good and bad actions receive the same credit if the final trajectory is labeled only by a binary success/failure signal. This coarse credit assignment wastes signal from useful steps in failed trajectories and does not penalize redundant or harmful steps, limiting sample efficiency and performance, especially for long-horizon tasks.

Method: ABC introduces fine-grained, answer-grounded credit assignment in two stages: (1) Answer-Backtracked Clue Recovery: starting from the ground-truth answer, the system recovers intermediate clues that are necessary to solve the query. (2) Clue-Anchored Step Scoring: each step of a search trajectory is scored by how well it recovers or uses these clues, converting sparse binary outcomes into dense step-level rewards. These scores are then used in two training schemes: ABC-SFT, which reweights the per-turn SFT loss, and ABC-GRPO, which uses the step scores as rewards for GRPO-based RL. The framework is instantiated in ABSeeker, a long-horizon search agent built on Qwen3.5-4B.

Result: ABSeeker, trained with only 8.5k examples using ABC-based SFT and RL, achieves 37.3% on BrowseComp and 39.1% on BrowseComp-ZH. With additional context management, performance improves to 55.3% and 52.9%, respectively. These results surpass other 4B-parameter agents and are competitive with models around 30B parameters, demonstrating high data and parameter efficiency.

Conclusion: Answer-backtracked, step-level credit assignment substantially improves training for long-horizon search agents by rewarding useful actions and suppressing erroneous or redundant ones, even in failed trajectories. The ABC framework effectively densifies sparse trajectory-level supervision, enabling a 4B model (ABSeeker) trained on a small dataset to match or outperform much larger agents on browsing benchmarks, highlighting the promise of fine-grained, answer-grounded credit assignment for complex multi-step reasoning and search tasks.

Abstract: Long-horizon search agents must make multiple sequential actions (steps) to search, retrieve, verify, and integrate evidence to reach a final answer. However, existing methods for training these agents typically treat all steps within a trajectory uniformly during both supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL), failing to distinguish useful actions from erroneous or redundant ones. In this paper, we propose Answer-Backtracked Credit Assignment (ABC), a fine-grained credit assignment framework for training long-horizon search agents by converting sparse trajectory-level outcomes into dense step-level supervision that rewards useful actions (even in failed trajectories) while suppressing erroneous or redundant actions. Specifically, given a potentially obscure query and its corresponding ground-truth answer, ABC first performs Answer-Backtracked Clue Recovery, which traces back from the answer to recover intermediate clues required to solve the question. It then applies Clue-Anchored Step Scoring to evaluate each search step against these clues, converting sparse binary outcome supervision into dense step-level rewards. Based on these rewards, we develop ABC-SFT, which reweights the loss of each turn, and ABC-GRPO, which uses the step-level scores as rewards in GRPO. Building on this framework, we train ABSeeker based on Qwen3.5-4B with only 8.5k examples. ABSeeker achieves 37.3% on BrowseComp and 39.1% on BrowseComp-ZH. With context management, the scores further improve to 55.3% and 52.9%, respectively, significantly outperforming same-scale (4B) agents and even matching the performance of larger ones (approximately 30B). These results demonstrate the effectiveness of answer-backtracked step-level credit assignment for training long-horizon search agents.

</details>


### [111] [CoPlan: A Trustworthy Co-Intelligence Interface for Care Planning through Role-Based Contestable Argument Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05107)
*Hung Truong Thanh Nguyen,Hélène Fournier,Piper Jackson,Makoto Itoh,Shannon Freeman,Rene Richard,Hung Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CoPlan, a human-AI interface for care planning that keeps AI recommendations contestable and editable by clinicians and care teams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI tools for care planning often output fixed recommendations that are hard to inspect, challenge, or adapt to patient values, clinical judgment, and real-world constraints, which can undermine trust, agency, and accountability.

Method: Design and implementation of CoPlan, a multi-agent AI workflow where specialized agents generate candidate interventions and pro/con arguments, and human care planners interact with these arguments—accepting, rejecting, modifying, or adding them—before the system synthesizes a final care plan; demonstrated in an aging-in-place scenario with adaptive team recruitment, role-based review, and scheduling support.

Result: CoPlan enables collaborative, argument-based care planning where AI and human stakeholders jointly shape the plan, supporting inspection of rationales, structured disagreement, revisions, and downstream execution tasks like scheduling; it operationalizes co-intelligence and contestability in a concrete interface.

Conclusion: A contestable, co-intelligent interface like CoPlan can enhance trustworthiness of human-AI care planning by preserving human agency and clinical accountability while leveraging AI agents’ generative and reasoning capabilities; the authors propose this as a general design framing for future care-planning systems.

Abstract: AI-supported care planning can help clinicians, patients, caregivers, and care teams coordinate complex decisions across clinical, functional, psychosocial, and environmental needs. However, many AI systems present recommendations as fixed outputs, limiting stakeholders' ability to inspect, challenge, and revise plans when they conflict with clinical judgment, patient values, or real-world feasibility. We present CoPlan - a Co-Intelligent and Contestable Interface for Human-AI Care Planning. CoPlan uses a multi-agent workflow in which specialized AI agents generate candidate interventions and supporting or challenging arguments, while human care planners can accept, reject, modify, or add arguments before final plan generation. Through this design, CoPlan combines co-intelligence, in which humans and AI agents contribute complementary expertise, with contestability, where recommendations remain open to inspection, revision, and justification. We demonstrate CoPlan in an aging-in-place care planning scenario. The system supports adaptive care team recruitment, role-based argument review, final care plan generation, and practical follow-up through scheduling agents. This work contributes a contestable care planning interface and a design framing for trustworthy human-AI care planning that preserves human agency and clinical accountability.

</details>


### [112] [OctoLong: Mid-Training On Cross-Repository Code Contexts Enhances Long-Context Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05141)
*Indraneil Paul,Falko Helm,Goran Glavaš,Iryna Gurevych*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OctoLong introduces a new pipeline for constructing extremely long, dependency-rich code contexts and uses them to train long-context language models that significantly outperform existing models on long-range and agentic code tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LMs gain longer context windows, existing long-context datasets—mostly books, papers, and standard code—are limited in size and lack dense long-range dependencies, especially for code-based and agentic workflows. There is a need for scalable, high-quality, long-context corpora that stress true long-distance reasoning and retrieval, particularly in software repositories.

Method: The authors design OctoLong, a context-engineering pipeline that combines an AST parser, language server backend, and package manager to recursively resolve and retrieve all relevant code references, building code contexts with millions of tokens and rich dependency structure. They then perform context-extension mid-training on base LMs (600M–14B parameters) using a 50B-token mixture that includes 6.2B tokens of OctoLong-generated contexts, followed by 10B tokens of instruction tuning, and conduct training ablations and benchmarking against 18 long-context open-weight models.

Result: Replacing roughly 12% of traditional context-extension data with OctoLong code contexts leads to marked improvements in long-range retrieval, long-horizon state tracking, repository-level code understanding, and downstream agent-style tasks. The models also show better API usage in standard short-context coding benchmarks, outperforming 18 contemporary open long-context LMs on the evaluated tasks.

Conclusion: Dependency-dense, programmatically constructed long code contexts are a highly effective ingredient for training long-context LMs. Even a modest proportion of OctoLong data in the training mix yields large gains on both long-context and some short-context coding tasks, suggesting that thoughtfully engineered long-context corpora can substantially improve the capabilities of long-context models beyond what is achievable with traditional long-text sources.

Abstract: Context lengths of language models (LMs) have dramatically increased, driven by the demands for in-context learning, self-improvement, and long-horizon agentic workflows. Existing long-context corpora, however, are dominated by books, academic articles, and code repositories, which are finite resources and often scarce in long-distance dependencies. In this work, we introduce OctoLong, a context engineering pipeline that instruments an AST parser, a language server backend, and a package manager to facilitate the recursive retrieval of code references, enabling the curation of dependency-rich code contexts of millions of tokens in length. We then train OctoLong-Instruct, a suite of capable long-context open LMs, derived from base models ranging in size from 600M to 14B parameters, via context-extension mid-training on a ~50B-token mixture containing ~6.2B tokens of OctoLong code contexts, followed by ~10B tokens of instruction tuning. Our training ablations and experimental evaluations against 18 state-of-the-art open-weight long-context LMs show that supplanting just 12% of traditional context-extension corpora with OctoLong data yields substantial gains in long-range retrieval, long-term state tracking, repository-level code understanding, and downstream agentic tasks, while also enhancing API usage in short-context coding scenarios.

</details>


### [113] [Argus: A General-Purpose Agentic Runtime for Long-Horizon Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05144)
*Boxiu Li,Zimo Wen,Yijia Fan,Junxiang Lei,Sufeng Guo,Jiaao Wu,Ruize Tang,Mukai Li,Yifei Shen,Xiaoyu Chen,Wanbo Zhang,Runjing Gu,Yifei Gao,Yuheng Wu,Xuyao Huang,Zelong Zhao,Jiachen Zhang,Shibo Hu,Hangxi Guo,Yilin Chen,Yuzhe Zhang,Fan Yang,Chuan Wen,Xian Zhang,Xuanhe Zhou,Zhijie Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Argus is a fixed-weight, self-evolving multi-agent runtime that supports long-horizon reasoning by managing persistent project state and verification-gated adaptation, significantly outperforming direct LLM use on several benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agents struggle with long-horizon reasoning because they lack persistent, reliable runtime structures that can maintain progress over time, detect failures, and adapt their strategies without retraining model weights. The authors aim to build a system that can separate stable user intent from operational details, accumulate verified knowledge and procedures, and robustly recover from errors while operating autonomously across complex, multi-step tasks.

Method: The paper introduces Argus, a persistent, self-evolving agentic runtime composed of distinct roles (Manager, Planner, Engineer, Reviewer) that operate over a durable project state. Argus separates user intent from operational objectives, constraints, and verification criteria, and only admits new memories, skills, procedures, verifiers, and routing decisions after role-specific review and, when possible, task-native verification. Model parameters are frozen; adaptation occurs through updates to runtime state and control policies, with autonomous execution between predefined escalation points to a human operator. The system is evaluated across multiple GPT-5.5 benchmark suites, including SWE-Bench Pro, AARRI-Bench, mathematical data synthesis, GPU kernel optimization, and language-model-training tasks, along with real-world deployments such as RWKV6 kernel optimization and multi-day math and paper-pipeline campaigns.

Result: On SWE-Bench Pro, Argus attains about 78% task success versus 59% for a baseline Direct Copilot setup, at 1.41x the token usage. After verification-gated self-evolution, later SWE-Bench waves require 21% fewer input tokens for solving and 15% less active workflow time per task than initial waves, while logging 34 verifier-based recoveries and 22 strict review-loop rescues. Argus achieves 76.8% on AARRI-Bench and shows a 28.0-point performance advantage on mathematical data synthesis, with competitive outcomes on GPU-kernel and language-model-training benchmarks. In real-world scenarios, Argus contributed an optimized RWKV6 kernel that was merged upstream, sustained a multi-day mathematics campaign with falsified routes and proof-backed frontier updates, and supported six paper pipelines over 254 missions with 16 stage rollbacks.

Conclusion: The study concludes that a fixed-weight, self-evolving agentic harness like Argus can effectively support long-horizon reasoning by continually revising, recovering, and consolidating verified strategies without changing model weights. Its role-structured runtime, durable project state, and verification-gated evolution enable higher reliability, efficiency gains over time, and rich, structured trajectories that can be reused as training data for future supervised or reinforcement learning systems.

Abstract: Long-horizon reasoning requires an agentic runtime that can persist when evidence supports its current approach and pivot when measurements reveal failure, hidden constraints, or a misspecified objective. We present Argus, a persistent, self-evolving runtime in which Manager, Planner, Engineer, and Reviewer execute bounded missions over durable project state. Argus separates stable user intent from operational objectives, constraints, and verification criteria, and admits memories, skills, procedures, verifiers, routing decisions, and rejected routes only after role-owned review and, when available, task-native verification. Model weights remain fixed; self-evolution occurs through persistent runtime state and control policy, with autonomous execution between operator-owned escalation points. Across seven GPT-5.5 benchmark arenas, Argus achieves about 78% on SWE-Bench Pro versus 59% for Direct Copilot while using 1.41 times the aggregate tokens. After verification-gated self-evolution, mature SWE-Bench waves use 21% fewer solve-input tokens and 15% less active workflow time per task than startup waves, while recording 34 verifier recoveries and 22 strict review-loop rescues. Argus also reaches 76.8% on AARRI-Bench and a 28.0-point gap on mathematical data synthesis, with competitive GPU-kernel and language-model-training results. Beyond benchmarks, an optimized RWKV6 kernel was merged upstream; a multi-day mathematics campaign retained falsified routes and proof-backed frontier updates; and six paper pipelines completed 254 missions with 16 stage rollbacks. These results show that a fixed-weight, self-evolving harness can revise, recover, and accumulate verified approaches while producing structured trajectories for future supervised and reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [114] [Similarity All The Way Up: Multilingual Generalization in LLMs Relies on Language-Level Similarity Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22699)
*Supantho Rakshit,Adele Goldberg,Henry Conklin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates how well large language models’ internal representations capture hierarchical similarities between languages and how this relates to their ability to generalize across languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although LLMs are highly capable, their ability to generalize to new or low-resource languages is limited and poorly understood. The authors want to explain why some models generalize multilingually better than others and draw on cognitive science theories that link generalization performance to how well similarity structures are represented. They aim to test whether LLMs’ internal language representations align with known hierarchical relationships between languages and whether this alignment predicts multilingual generalization performance.

Method: The authors analyze the latent representations of multiple LLMs for different languages, examining whether these representations recover the known hierarchical structure of the Indo-European language family tree. They measure how closely languages belonging to the same subfamily cluster together in representation space and quantify how well this matches linguistic similarity structure. They then correlate these structural similarity scores with model performance on XNLI, a multilingual natural language inference benchmark, to test whether better-structured similarity spaces correspond to better cross-lingual generalization.

Result: They find that LLMs’ internal representations largely recover the hierarchical structure of the Indo-European language family: languages from the same subfamily cluster closely together in the representation space. Moreover, models whose representations more faithfully reflect the linguistic similarity structure show better performance on XNLI. In other words, the quality of the language similarity structure in the embedding space positively correlates with multilingual NLI performance.

Conclusion: The study concludes that LLMs that represent similar languages more similarly in their latent spaces generalize better across languages, echoing long-standing cognitive science findings about similarity-driven generalization. Accurately capturing hierarchical linguistic similarity structures appears to be a key ingredient for successful multilingual generalization in LLMs, providing both an explanatory framework and a diagnostic for evaluating and improving cross-lingual performance.

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) grow more capable across diverse tasks, their (in)ability to generalize remains difficult to quantify and poorly understood beyond limited domains. In particular, LLMs are known to struggle generalizing multilingually, to languages outside of English, and that are poorly attested in their training data. To understand why this may be, and what enables some models to perform better than others, we turn to a long history of work across the cognitive sciences, arguing that successful generalization derives from appropriate representations in similarity space. We look at how well LLMs' representations capture the hierarchical similarity structure between distinct languages. Strikingly, we show LLMs' latent representations largely recover the hierarchical structure of the Indo-European language family tree -- grouping languages that are members of the same subfamily closely together in representation space. Furthermore, we show that the degree to which models reflect the similarity structure of languages correlates with their performance on XNLI, a multilingual natural language inference benchmark. This extends classic work on similarity-driven generalization at scale, showing how models that represent similar languages similarly generalize better from one language to another.

</details>
